# Infrastructure — Domain-Specific Financial Model Review Skill

This file contains **infrastructure-domain knowledge only**: the physical, contractual, operating,
regulatory, demand, service-performance, lifecycle and asset-economics relationships that an
infrastructure specialist should understand when reviewing a financial model. Generic spreadsheet
review, formula engineering, audit evidence, severity, reporting, governance, generic corporate-finance
mechanics, generic debt-schedule procedures and generic financial-statement controls belong to the core
audit infrastructure and are intentionally not repeated here.

The skill has been rebuilt from the prior rail-specific context into an infrastructure-wide parent skill.
The original rail expertise is preserved and deepened as a specialist module. The current web-research
foundation has been benchmarked against authoritative international infrastructure and PPP materials
available in August 2026, including the 2026 APMG PPP Certification Guide v2, the 2026 EPEC Guide to
Public-Private Partnerships, the World Bank PPP Reference Guide and 2019 PPP Risk Allocation Tool and
Contractual Provisions guidance, World Bank sector toolkits, the 2025 Port Reform Toolkit, ICAO airport
economics/charges guidance, the World Bank Railway Reform Toolkit, water/NRW materials, current
regulated-network examples, the European Commission Broadband Investment Handbook 2024, climate
PPP toolkits, peer-reviewed infrastructure cost and demand forecasting research, current Partnerships Victoria
harmonised PPP deeds/guidance, UK PFI/PPP finance guidance, and peer-reviewed/practitioner work specifically on PPP
financial models and project-finance debt sculpting. A subsequent online-book deep dive also incorporated
durable modelling principles from Edward Farquharson, Clemencia Torres de Mästle and E.R. Yescombe's World Bank/PPIAF
book *How to Engage with the Private Sector in Public-Private Partnerships in Emerging Markets*; Timothy Irwin's
World Bank book *Government Guarantees: Allocating and Valuing Risk in Privately Financed Infrastructure Projects*;
the World Bank framework on managing government guarantees; and José Luis Guasch's World Bank concession-renegotiation
work as a targeted gap check. A further contract-mechanics pass used the Australian National PPP Guidelines (including the
Practitioners' Guide, Social Infrastructure Commercial Principles and Public Sector Comparator Guidance), UNCITRAL's 2019
Legislative Guide and Model Legislative Provisions, and current UK NISTA PFI contract-management guidance. The withdrawn
UK *Standardisation of PF2 Contracts* was used only as a historical mechanics reference for durable concepts such as payment-
remedy interaction, insurance reinstatement, lifecycle/handback security and termination valuation pathways; it is **not**
treated as current UK procurement policy. The ADB PPP Handbook was screened as a secondary cross-check. Peer-reviewed PPP
financial-model research was used as corroboration rather than as a source of generic spreadsheet-engineering rules.
A further advanced-concession and operating-contract pass incorporated the openly accessible work of Eduardo Engel,
Ronald Fischer and Alexander Galetovic on least-present-value-of-revenue concessions, PPP public-finance economics and
renegotiation incentives; Deborah Lucas and Jorge Jimenez Montesinos' NBER/University of Chicago chapter on fair-value
assessment of public infrastructure and transferred risk; the World Bank / Global Infrastructure Hub PPP Contract Management
Tool based on more than 250 PPP projects; and the World Bank's 2026 performance-based road asset-management guidance.
These sources are used as conditional economic lenses, not as universal prescriptions: variable-term concessions, revenue
floors/caps, fair-value support analysis and output-based road contracts are applied only where the project's actual contract,
procurement or authority methodology makes them relevant.
A further project-finance and bankability books pass incorporated the full open-access IFC/World Bank volume
*Project Finance in Developing Countries* (Priscilla Anita Ahmed and Xinghai Fang) and the World Bank/PPIAF/ICA
*Attracting Investors to African Public-Private Partnerships: A Project Preparation Guide*, together with FHWA's open
highway-P3 financing guidebooks, project-finance materials and toll-concession contract guidance. Jeffrey Delmon's PPIAF-
listed project-finance/PPP books were screened as corroborative structure and risk-allocation references where accessible.
These older sources are used only for durable limited-recourse, completion-support, security, public-support, concession-fee
and currency-risk mechanics; their historical market terms, financing percentages and policy settings are not treated as current
benchmarks or universal requirements.
A further economic-appraisal and live-PPP-operations pass used a deeper read of the current 2026 EPEC *Guide to Public-Private
Partnerships*; the EIB's 2023 *Economic Appraisal of Investment Projects*; ADB's 2017 *Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of
Projects* and sector economic-analysis materials; UNIDO's 1996 *Guidelines for Infrastructure Development through Build-Operate-
Transfer (BOT) Projects*; and South Africa GTAC/National Treasury material on managing PPP agreements. EPEC 2026 is treated as
current European good-practice guidance, subject to national rules. EIB and ADB materials are authoritative institutional appraisal
methodologies and are used to strengthen conditional authority/economic-appraisal review, not to impose their discount rates, shadow
prices or decision thresholds universally. The older UNIDO and South African materials are used only for durable contract-management,
construction-delay, variation, payment-administration and transition mechanics; dated policy, market and legal settings are not treated
as current requirements.
A final historical-practitioner pass incorporated David Whittaker's 2010 *PFI PPP Financial Modelling and Analysis: A Practical
Guide*. It is used selectively for durable PPP model-lifecycle mechanics, especially the transition from financial close to a live
operating model, actual-to-reforecast integration, lender/shareholder history, staged service payments, distress restructuring and
secondary-market stake analysis. Its UK PFI policy process, historic accounting/tax treatment, numerical financing assumptions,
ratio targets, Excel/VBA conventions and workbook-layout preferences are **not** treated as current or universal requirements; the
book itself states that its numerical examples are fictional.

A further **user-supplied source pass in August 2026** reviewed the complete domain content of Edward Bodmer's
*A-Z Project Finance Modelling Course* and *Advanced Project Finance Modelling — Addressing Circular References and
Complex Sculpting*, together with the World Bank-led *Public-Private Partnerships Reference Guide Version 3* and the
Asian Development Bank *Public-Private Partnership Handbook*. The material was screened concept-by-concept against
this skill. Durable infrastructure/PPP/project-finance business mechanics were incorporated below, including PPP/PSP
contract boundaries, funding-versus-financing, tariff/reset/subsidy architecture, output-based aid, brownfield workforce
and local-participation obligations, project-finance structuring versus risk analysis, coverage-horizon interpretation,
multi-tranche sculpting, reserve/LC economics, cash-sweep appropriateness, hedge-profile/average-life issues, pre-COD
actual-to-remaining-funding logic, stage-dependent project risk, and authority portfolio/subnational fiscal exposure.

Those Bodmer materials also contain extensive Excel/UDF/VBA, formula-writing, workbook-layout, FAST, shortcut,
scenario-interface and generic accounting/audit techniques. **Those generic modelling mechanics are intentionally not
imported into this infrastructure skill.** Only the underlying infrastructure/project-finance economic relationships are
retained. Likewise, historical numerical examples in the ADB and Bodmer materials are treated as illustrations rather
than current benchmarks.


A subsequent **domain-boundary and sovereign-financial-sustainability pass** reviewed four additional user-supplied
sources end-to-end: ICAEW's 2024 *Financial Modelling Code*; Edward Bodmer's *Intermediate Corporate and Project
Finance Modeling from A-Z* course outline; the World Bank/PPIAF *Toolkit for Public-Private Partnerships in Roads &
Highways* financial-model guidance; and ADB's 2019 *Financial Analysis and Evaluation — Technical Guidance Note*.
The ADB material materially strengthens the conditional sovereign/public-operator parts of this skill: network-level
recurrent-cost sustainability, unconstrained O&M requirements, budget allocation versus actual execution, entity-versus-
project viability, predictable versus discretionary support, real project financial evaluation versus nominal entity analysis,
incremental with/without-project financial cash flows, tariff/toll achievability, project-financing-plan WACC, switching-value
resilience, controllable financial covenants, and implementation/completion re-evaluation. The road-toolkit material is used
only for durable highway-specific model-maturity, toll/subsidy, multi-debt and currency-boundary lessons; its simplified
educational models and dated macro/security settings are not treated as bankable templates.

The ICAEW Code was reviewed concept-by-concept, including model purpose, layout, navigation, input presentation,
transparency, consistency, labels/units, checks, hard-coding, circularity, range names and VBA. Those are deliberately
**excluded from this file because they are generic spreadsheet/model-engineering practices**, not infrastructure business-domain
criteria. The same boundary is applied to the generic Excel/formula content of the Bodmer course. Only Bodmer's durable
project-finance business concepts — project stages, changing risk, contract-led cash flow, physical output, construction timing,
outages, debt sizing/sculpting, defaults, cash waterfalls, reserves, sweeps/traps, refinancing and project-credit ratios — are
retained, and only where not already covered more deeply below.

This pass also reconciled the previously supplied EPEC contract-life/VfM material and the geothermal project-finance thesis
against the current skill so that domain concepts from earlier source passes were not lost. EPEC's contract-management and VfM
material is treated as institutional good-practice guidance subject to the governing jurisdiction and contract. The geothermal
thesis is treated as a specialist secondary source: durable resource-confirmation, phased-risk, wellfield/plant and tariff mechanics
are retained, while its case-specific percentages, costs, capacity factors, degradation rates and financing terms are not standards.


A further **user-supplied operating-distress, technology-project-finance and sector-model pass in August 2026**
reviewed the World Bank *Port Reform Toolkit — Financial Model Manual* for a port concession, the World Bank/PPIAF
*Numerical Model for Financial Simulation of Highway PPP Projects — User Guide*, New Energy Risk's 2021
*Industrial & Energy Technology Project Finance — A Startup and Developer's Guide to Scaling and Commercial Success*,
and the UK National Infrastructure & Service Transformation Authority's *Navigating the risks of PFI project distress —
Part 2: Project company financial stress* (first published 2024, rebranded 2026). The sources are used selectively for
durable domain mechanics: port public/private scope and mixed brownfield/greenfield terminal economics; vehicle-class,
toll, periodic-maintenance and public-support mechanics in road concessions; FOAK/NOAK technology maturity, FEL/FEED,
contract maturity, project-company separateness, technical evidence and financing-market fit; and operating-PFI distress
causation, residual SPV risk, forward-looking solvency headroom and lender/authority monitoring. Their historical model
defaults, debt ratios, hurdle rates, accounting/tax assumptions, financing-market prices, fixed reserve sizes, legal-liability
period examples and technology-readiness thresholds are **not** treated as current or universal requirements.

The New Energy Risk guide's "SACP/HC-SACP" terminology is retained only as a useful qualitative lens for whether a
technology project's contracts and structural protections can support a stand-alone project credit. It is not treated as a
formal rating methodology, and the skill does not assume that every counterparty must be investment grade or that one weak
counterparty mechanically determines the project's rating. Likewise, FEL stages and TRL labels are used as evidence-maturity
taxonomies rather than universal financing gates. The World Bank road model's automatic subsidy-to-target-ADSCR mode is
treated as a **funding-gap diagnostic**, not evidence that government support is committed; its project-specific "bankruptcy"
test and its illustrative IRR/DSCR ranges are not imported as bankability rules.



A further **user-supplied project-finance structuring, PPP-scoping and hospital-sector pass in August 2026**
reviewed four additional sources: Edward Bodmer's *Project Finance Masterclass* programme; Francis Ndupuechi's 2003
University of Leeds PhD thesis *Financial Engineering for Project Finance*; the Philippines National Government Agency
*Public-Private Partnership Manual, Volume 4 — Annexes* (2014 draft); and the Asian Development Bank's 2013
*Guidebook on Public-Private Partnership in Hospital Management*. The sources were screened against the existing skill
rather than copied into it. The durable incremental mechanics retained below include early reverse revenue-requirement
scoping, do-minimum and enabling-infrastructure alternatives, financing-package choice under coverage and market
constraints, fixed versus index-linked debt cash timing, credit-wrap economics, asset-based/Sharia-compliant financing
boundaries, refinancing-model audit and distribution-NPV bridges, environmental/social/consent obligations that affect
project cash or schedule, and a substantially deeper hospital/health-service PPP module.

The Ndupuechi thesis is a historical research source built around an illustrative health-sector concession and early-2000s
market conditions. Its model-specific gearing optima, bank/bond pricing, monoline fees, tax treatment, inflation cases and
other numerical findings are **not** current benchmarks. Its generic spreadsheet verification/validation methods are also
excluded because this file is domain-specific. What is retained is the transaction logic: financing form changes project cash
timing and flexibility; maximum sustainable leverage is constrained by the binding financing/risk conditions rather than by
an arbitrary debt percentage; and apparently matched index-linked revenue and debt can still fail under basis, lag,
principal-accretion or distribution mechanics.

The Philippines NGA manual is a 2014 **draft** and the ADB hospital guidebook is jurisdiction- and period-specific.
Philippine statutory thresholds, equity ratios, hurdle rates, construction S-curves, environmental classifications,
procurement rules, reimbursement lags and sample contract percentages are therefore not treated as universal. Their durable
value is in the causal model bridges: public-service need and technical alternatives to the private revenue requirement;
retained/enabling works to bankability; lender security to contract cash flows; safeguards/consents to cost, schedule and
permitting; and hospital demand, payer mix, service scope, licensing, staffing and quality to financial sustainability.

The Bodmer masterclass is a programme outline rather than a complete technical text. It is used as a **coverage challenge**
for risks and structures — project versus holdco debt, stage-dependent risk, level/sculpted/bullet repayment, traffic and
major-maintenance risk, subordinated capital, intercreditor arrangements, development-period risk and project-finance
insurance — only where the detailed mechanics are supported by the project's documents or by the other authoritative
sources already embedded in this skill.


A further **toll-road traffic-risk deep dive in August 2026** incorporated the complete World Bank/PPIAF/Global Infrastructure
Facility guide *Toll-Road PPPs: Identifying, Mitigating and Managing Traffic Risk*. This source materially deepens the road,
bridge and tunnel module below around traffic-study maturity, opening-day versus long-term demand, survey and network-model
evidence, willingness-to-pay and traffic-capture mechanics, error/uncertainty/bias, public- and lender-side traffic due diligence,
shadow-bid financial modelling, traffic-risk screening, and the choice between availability, blended-availability, shadow-toll,
minimum-revenue/revenue-sharing, public subordinated support, full user-pay and flexible-term structures. The guide's empirical
forecast-error studies, example traffic-risk scores, illustrative toll bands, financial-ratio examples and historic project cases are
**evidence and diagnostics, not universal thresholds**. The core rule retained is that traffic risk must be decomposed, stress-tested
and allocated according to the project's actual demand evidence, financial resilience, affordability, fiscal capacity and contract.

The newly supplied copy of Bodmer's *Project Finance Masterclass* and both newly supplied copies of Ndupuechi's *Financial
Engineering for Project Finance* were verified as byte-identical duplicates of the versions already reviewed for the prior source
pass. They therefore produced no incremental doctrine in this version; their previously incorporated project-finance concepts remain
fully preserved below rather than being duplicated again.

A further **energy-efficiency/ESCO and power-generation practitioner pass in August 2026** reviewed the complete
user-supplied books *How to Finance Energy Management Projects: Solving the "Lack of Capital Problem"* (Woodroof &
Thumann, 2013) and David Whittaker's *Power Generation Financial Modelling & Analysis: A Practical Guide* (2013).
The former materially expands the skill below around energy-service performance contracting, project-development
agreements, investment-grade energy audits, baseline and measurement-and-verification logic, PACE/property-linked
finance, managed energy service agreements, host-side solar PPAs, energy-efficiency lender underwriting, commissioning,
O&M persistence and the distinction between measured savings, adjusted-baseline avoided cost and broader ancillary
benefits. The latter deepens power-generation unit economics, technology-specific physical drivers, construction-to-COD
bridges, fuel/efficiency logic, planned and forced outage treatment, tariff/incentive decomposition, project/equity/blended
return boundaries, tariff-to-bankability analysis and energy-specific audit evidence.

Both books are historical practitioner sources and contain material that is no longer current or is jurisdiction-specific.
Accordingly, the skill **does not** import their 2012-2013 tax incentives, ROC/LEC regimes, U.S. federal programme details,
legacy lease-accounting/off-balance-sheet classifications, historical tax-credit amounts, illustrative ESCO insurance terms,
sample PACE lien rules, sample technology costs/capacity factors/IRRs, private-equity return targets, model sheet colours,
Excel/VBA conventions or generic spreadsheet-audit procedures as present-day standards. Likewise, the books' fixed
"good project" payback rules, claimed generic stock-price uplift from energy announcements, suggested percentage uplifts
for ancillary energy benefits and any claim that a particular financing form is automatically off balance sheet are **not**
universalised. Durable causal relationships are retained only where they improve domain review, and every legal, tax,
accounting, incentive, tariff, PACE, lease, PPA and M&V requirement must be checked against the current transaction,
contract and jurisdiction.



A further **independent web-research pass in August 2026** targeted domains that remained comparatively underdeveloped in
this skill rather than repeating generic project-finance doctrine. The pass used current or still-operative primary institutional
sources and peer-reviewed research, including RICS *Valuation of Development Property* (2019, effective 2020) and RICS
research on *Performance Metrics, Required Returns and Achieved Returns for UK Real Estate Development*; the U.S. OCC
*Commercial Real Estate Lending* Comptroller's Handbook (Version 2.0, March 2022, with 2025 amendments); peer-reviewed
real-estate feasibility and hurdle-rate research; ICAO's fourth-edition *Airport Economics Manual* material; the Equator
Principles EP4; the World Bank's 2025 *Transport Resilience Financing, Resources and Opportunities*; the current Basel
Framework's specialised-lending taxonomy/slotting approach; and peer-reviewed battery-storage revenue-stacking research in
*Electric Power Systems Research*.

These materials are incorporated only where they add **business-domain capability**. The RICS/OCC material is used to build a
full real-estate/development-finance module around development stages, residual land value, GDV/NDV, profit metrics, timing,
ADC/construction lending, cost-to-complete, interest reserves, presales/preleasing, stabilization and lender monitoring. ICAO is
used to deepen airport aeronautical/non-aeronautical economics, charging cost bases, traffic-linked charge structures, economic
overview and infrastructure financing. EP4 is used only where a project's financing actually requires Equator/IFC-style E&S due
diligence, translating E&S obligations into permits, conditions precedent, capex, opex, schedule, monitoring and remedy costs
rather than creating a generic ESG checklist. The World Bank transport-resilience work is used to connect climate risk,
maintenance, adaptation capex, contractual allocation, public funding, blended finance and insurance to project economics.
The battery-storage paper adds the critical interaction between revenue stacking, dispatch constraints, state of charge and
physical degradation.

Historical, jurisdiction-specific or accounting-specific numerical rules are **not** universalised. In particular, U.S. supervisory
LTV limits, statutory HVCRE definitions, old-style lease accounting, historical airport charge levels, planning-viability margins,
fixed developer hurdle rates, sample battery market prices and any specific RICS/OCC/ICAO example figures remain source- or
jurisdiction-specific evidence. The skill instead retains the durable relationships that an institutional lender, investor, developer
or model auditor should test in the actual transaction.

Historical traffic-growth rates, toll levels, availability-payment deductions, DSCRs, gearing, cost-overrun
percentages, capex benchmarks, regulatory returns, asset lives, tariff formulas and other numerical examples
are **not** universal requirements. Durable infrastructure-economic relationships are retained; project-,
contract-, regulator-, jurisdiction-, technology- and valuation-date-specific assumptions must be supported by
the applicable current framework and evidence.

## Model type and applicability

Apply this skill to infrastructure models including:

- greenfield and brownfield PPPs / P3s;
- concessions, BOT/BOOT/DBFO/DBFM/DBFOM and similar long-term infrastructure arrangements;
- availability-payment and performance-payment infrastructure;
- user-pays concessions and demand-risk assets;
- regulated monopoly networks and utilities;
- state-owned or corporatised infrastructure operators where physical asset economics drive the model;
- operating infrastructure acquisitions, asset recycling and refinancing cases;
- expansions, augmentations and major lifecycle-renewal programmes; and
- mixed structures where public support, user revenue, regulated revenue and ancillary revenue coexist.

Infrastructure sectors covered by this parent skill are:

1. **Rail / railway infrastructure** — passenger, freight, network infrastructure and integrated or
   separated operators.
2. **Roads / toll roads / bridges / tunnels** — real-toll, shadow-toll, availability and hybrid structures.
3. **Airports** — aeronautical and non-aeronautical economics, traffic and capacity.
4. **Ports / terminals** — landlord ports, terminal concessions and integrated port operations.
5. **Water / wastewater** — utilities, bulk water, treatment, network and NRW-related economics.
6. **Power generation / energy infrastructure** — contracted IPPs, renewable and thermal generation,
   capacity/availability structures, PPAs and grid/interconnection interfaces.
7. **District energy / heat networks** — generation, distribution, customer connection and heat-offtake
   economics where project-financed or concession-like.
8. **Regulated utility / network infrastructure** — electricity/gas networks and analogous RAB/RAV-style
   regulated systems where applicable.
9. **Social infrastructure** — hospitals, schools, justice/corrections and similar availability-based assets.
10. **Digital / communications infrastructure** — fixed broadband, fibre, backhaul, open-access and
    communications networks.
11. **Solid waste / resource recovery** — collection, transfer, processing, landfill and related concession
    economics where material.
12. **Energy efficiency / ESCO / distributed-energy services** — performance contracting, measured-savings
    projects, property-linked retrofits, host-side PPAs and other service/savings-backed distributed-energy structures.

This file does **not** attempt to replace oil and gas, mining or property specialist skills. Where a power asset
is itself the core infrastructure investment, apply the energy module in this skill. Where a project combines an
infrastructure concession/network with a separate underlying industrial business, apply this skill to the
infrastructure/project-finance interface and the relevant specialist skill to that business.

## PPP / private-participation contract archetype and economic boundary

Before applying PPP or limited-recourse mechanics, identify the **actual private-participation structure**. Labels are
not reliable across jurisdictions. The model should reflect the functions, funding obligations, payment rights and risks
actually transferred.

For each material arrangement identify:

- asset ownership during construction, operations and expiry;
- responsibility for design / rehabilitation / construction;
- responsibility for routine O&M and lifecycle / replacement capex;
- responsibility for new investment / expansion;
- customer interface, billing and collection;
- payment source and compensation basis;
- demand / collection / operating / investment risk borne by the private party;
- contract term and renewal/extension rights; and
- handback / transfer obligations.

### Service and management contracts are not automatically project-financed PPPs

A **service contract** typically transfers a narrow activity — for example billing, meter reading, maintenance or leak
reduction — while the authority retains the broader service obligation and capital investment. Compensation may be a
unit fee, fixed fee, cost-plus arrangement or performance-linked payment. Do not impose concession debt, lifecycle,
handback or user-revenue risk merely because a private contractor is involved.

A **management contract** transfers broader day-to-day management and operating responsibility, often with a fixed fee
plus performance incentive or profit-share, while major investment remains public. Where the operator provides working
capital or limited investment, model that specific exposure rather than reclassifying the entire asset as privately
financed infrastructure.

### Lease / affermage structures have a distinct revenue and capex boundary

Under a lease/affermage-type arrangement, the private operator may bear O&M, collection and customer-credit risk while
the authority retains major investment responsibility. Distinguish the cash mechanics:

- under a lease, the operator may retain customer revenue and pay a specified lease/rental amount to the authority; and
- under an affermage, the operator may collect customer revenue, remit an agreed fee — often linked to units sold — and
  retain the balance.

Do not charge the operator with authority-retained rehabilitation/expansion unless the contract transfers it. Conversely,
do not omit customer default, leakage or operating risk where the operator's remuneration is exposed to collections.
These structures can create a **late-term under-maintenance incentive** if the private party does not bear long-life
renewal or handback consequences; test the actual maintenance and condition obligations rather than assuming whole-life
incentive merely from private operation.

### Concession and BOT-family structures

A concession typically transfers a broad package of service delivery, O&M, collection and investment responsibility,
often with user-pay revenue and public ownership/control of the underlying infrastructure. BOT/BOOT/BTO/DBFO/DBFOM
structures can instead focus on a new or major project component and may be paid by users, an authority/offtaker or a
hybrid mechanism.

Where an offtaker purchases project output, distinguish:

- fixed/capacity/availability payment;
- energy/output/throughput payment;
- take-or-pay / minimum-purchase commitment; and
- service-quality or availability conditions.

A take-or-pay structure can transfer demand risk back to the public/offtaker even when the physical asset is privately
financed. Do not label the project "user risk" simply because end users ultimately consume the service.

Ownership timing — BOT versus BTO versus BOO/BOOT — matters only to the extent it changes security, tax, operating
rights, handback/transfer, residual value or other project economics. Do not infer financing or risk allocation from the
acronym alone.

### Joint venture and hybrid structures

Where public and private parties jointly own the operating/project company, distinguish the government's roles as:

- shareholder;
- contracting authority/offtaker;
- regulator/tariff setter; and/or
- grantor of land, subsidy or guarantees.

Those roles can have conflicting incentives. A government dividend interest does not eliminate its regulatory or service
obligations, and a tariff decision should not be treated as certain merely because government is also a shareholder.

For **hybrid arrangements**, decompose the project function-by-function and cash-flow-by-cash-flow. A management
contract with a material performance-at-risk component, a lease with defined expansion obligations, a co-financed
network extension or a mixed concession/availability structure should be modelled from its actual components rather
than forced into one textbook category.

### Funding and financing are different concepts

For infrastructure and PPP analysis, keep the distinction explicit:

- **funding** is the ultimate source that pays for the service or asset over time — user tariffs, taxes, availability
  payments, subsidies, grants, regulated charges or other public/private revenue; while
- **financing** is the capital advanced before those funding streams are received — equity, shareholder debt, bank debt,
  bonds, bridge finance or other capital-market instruments.

Private finance does not create free infrastructure funding. Debt and equity are repaid or remunerated from user,
authority or other project cash flows. An authority model that treats private debt as if it eliminates the underlying
service-payment/fiscal burden is economically incomplete; a project model that treats a loan draw as project revenue is
likewise wrong.

## What an elite infrastructure financial-model auditor must understand

An elite infrastructure model reviewer must be able to reconstruct the **economic bargain and physical project**
behind the workbook. The specialist capability is not generic spreadsheet checking; it is knowing which
infrastructure relationships must exist, which documents define them, and how failures propagate through a
limited-recourse or long-term infrastructure model.

The reviewer should be able to move fluently across eight connected disciplines:

1. **Project agreement / concession economics** — payment mechanism, indexation, performance deductions,
   change/relief events, termination, handback, revenue support and the difference between contractual base case
   and current forecast.
2. **Project-finance bankability** — funding availability, conditions precedent, multi-tranche debt, CFADS
   perimeter, reserves, distribution controls, refinancing, hedging, cure/support mechanisms and debt/concession
   tail.
3. **Technical-to-financial translation** — capacity, reliability, degradation, resource/traffic/demand, operating
   efficiency, outage, maintenance and acceptance assumptions must reconcile with technical advisers' models and
   physical constraints.
4. **Construction programme and cost economics** — critical path, float, package interfaces, long-lead items,
   testing/commissioning, cost profile, escalation, contingency, claims and completion support must connect to
   funding and revenue dates.
5. **Operations, lifecycle and asset condition** — O&M performance, lifecycle replacement, actual condition,
   availability standards, reserve funding, residual life and handback must remain coherent through the full
   concession.
6. **Public-sector and regulated economics** — affordability, direct and contingent fiscal commitments, subsidy,
   regulatory revenue, public-sector comparator/value-for-money logic and government support where the model is
   used by an authority or regulator.
7. **Sector-specific physical engine** — traffic/capacity for transport; PPA/resource/dispatch for power; water
   balance for utilities; throughput and bottlenecks for ports; take-up/capacity for digital; and equivalent
   sector-specific cause-and-effect.
8. **Cross-adviser reconciliation** — legal, technical, traffic/demand, cost/QS, tax/accounting, insurance and
   financing advice must describe one project. A model can be internally elegant but wrong if those external
   disciplines are inconsistent with each other.

The highest-value infrastructure findings often sit **between disciplines**: a technical completion date that does
not satisfy the payment-mechanism acceptance test; a lender model that assumes cash is drawable before conditions
precedent; a lifecycle plan that passes engineering review but is unfunded; a PPA that protects fuel price but not
heat-rate underperformance; or a handback standard that survives after senior debt has been fully repaid and lender
oversight has disappeared.

## Classify the infrastructure case before reviewing it

Do not review all infrastructure models as if they were the same project-finance template. Classify at least:

### Revenue / payment regime

- **User-pays** — private/project company earns primarily from users, such as real tolls, fares, airport
  charges, port tariffs or utility tariffs.
- **Government-pays / availability** — government or authority pays for an available, performing service,
  substantially independent of end-user volumes.
- **Usage-linked government payment** — government payment varies with usage, such as shadow tolls or
  output-based payments.
- **Regulated revenue** — price/revenue follows a regulatory determination, price cap, revenue cap,
  building-block/RAB framework or another regulated mechanism.
- **Contracted-capacity / offtake** — revenue follows contracted capacity, take-or-pay, minimum volume,
  reserved capacity or similar commercial commitments.
- **Hybrid** — combinations of user charges, availability payments, grants/VGF, minimum-revenue support,
  subsidies, regulated charges and ancillary income.

The classification determines which risks truly sit with the project. **Demand risk, revenue risk and
availability/performance risk are different risks.** Do not infer risk transfer merely from the word “PPP”.

### Delivery / operating stage

- pre-award / bid;
- financial close / notice to proceed;
- construction;
- commissioning / acceptance;
- ramp-up / early operations;
- mature operations;
- major expansion / augmentation;
- refinancing / acquisition / asset recycling; or
- expiry / handback / transition.

A model built at financial close should not use the same uncertainty and asset-condition assumptions as a
mature operating asset with years of actual performance.

### Asset / service scope

Identify exactly what the project company is responsible for. Depending on the project this may include some
or all of:

- land / right of way / site access;
- design;
- construction;
- financing;
- major equipment / rolling stock / systems;
- operations;
- routine maintenance;
- lifecycle and major maintenance;
- customer billing / collection;
- service quality / availability;
- ancillary commercial activity;
- environmental or remediation obligations; and
- asset handback / transition.

Do not charge the project for obligations retained by the authority, and do not omit costs or risks transferred
to the project company.

## Current international PPP and infrastructure guidance — status discipline

Where a model or review invokes PPP “best practice”, distinguish operative/current guidance from older or
superseded materials. As at August 2026:

- **APMG PPP Certification Guide v2 (2026)** is the refreshed current version of the international PPP body
  of knowledge developed with the World Bank and MDB community. The prior 2016 guide remains useful
  historically but should not be mistaken for the latest edition.
- **EPEC Guide to Public-Private Partnerships (2026)** is the current EPEC high-level guide to preparing,
  procuring and delivering PPPs.
- **World Bank PPP Reference Guide 3.0 (2017)** remains a major international reference framework for the
  PPP lifecycle.
- **World Bank Guidance on PPP Contractual Provisions (2019)** and the **PPP Risk Allocation Tool 2019**
  remain important global contract/risk references. The Risk Allocation Tool covers economic and social
  infrastructure across multiple sectors.
- Sector rules, concession agreements, regulator determinations, laws and technical standards can have
  greater project-specific authority than any generic international guide.
- The **Australian National PPP Policy and Guidelines** remain published by the Australian Government as the agreed
  cross-government framework, but jurisdiction-specific policies and requirements take precedence where they differ.
- **UNCITRAL's 2019 Legislative Guide and Model Legislative Provisions on PPPs** are authoritative legal-framework
  references. They inform the review of PPP legal architecture, award, contract duration/extension/termination and
  related legal rights; they are not a substitute for the executed project agreement or a financial-model specification.
- **UK NISTA 2025–2026 operational PFI guidance** is current guidance for management of legacy PFI contracts. It is useful
  for understanding insurance, contract review, gaps/ambiguities, performance and expiry issues in operating projects.
- The UK's **Standardisation of PF2 Contracts (2012)** is withdrawn. Use it only as a historical source of detailed contract-
  mechanism examples where those concepts are corroborated by the actual contract or current guidance; never treat its
  drafting as an operative universal requirement.

Do not make “best practice” synonymous with one jurisdiction's standard form. The durable principle is to
understand the actual risk allocation, payment mechanism and service obligations of the project being
modelled.

## Legal, regulatory and institutional operating boundary — model consequences only

Infrastructure PPP economics can depend on whether the legal/regulatory environment actually permits the contractual model to operate.
This skill does not perform a generic legal-framework review. It tests only legal/institutional conditions that change project cash flow,
timing, risk allocation or enforceability.

Where material, confirm the economic assumption is supported for matters such as:

- authority to award the concession / enter the long-term payment obligation;
- tariff-setting or adjustment power and the responsible regulator/authority;
- land, permit, licence and environmental approvals needed for construction/operation;
- foreign ownership, currency conversion and profit/debt-service repatriation rights;
- enforceability of security, direct agreements, step-in and termination compensation;
- dispute-resolution / arbitration route where a material cash-flow right depends on it; and
- institutional responsibility for measuring performance, certifying payment, approving variations or administering tariff resets.

A model should not assume a tariff increase, government payment, asset transfer or lender remedy that the responsible institution lacks
legal authority to implement. Conversely, institutional or legal uncertainty should not become a generic risk premium when the actual
contract/law already resolves the point.

Where the regulator or authority must certify performance before payment, weak institutional capacity can create **timing and dispute
risk** even if the payment formula is clear. Model that risk only where there is evidence it can affect cash collection or approvals.

## Evidence hierarchy, model authority and cross-adviser reconciliation

Infrastructure projects commonly have several generations of the same commercial assumption. Before deciding
what the model **should** contain, identify the document or adviser output that is authoritative for the model's
purpose and date.

### Document maturity and binding status

For a financial-close or operating PPP model, the working hierarchy for an economic term is generally:

`executed agreement / regulator determination → final agreed financing or project schedule → binding offer / award
commitment → agreed heads of terms → adviser base case / approved technical study → management assumption`

This is not a substitute for legal interpretation and it is not absolute. A technical performance assumption may
be governed by an approved design report rather than the project agreement; a tax assumption by applicable law; a
regulated tariff by the regulator; and a current operating forecast by actual performance data. The durable rule is
that **later or more binding evidence should not be silently replaced by an earlier bid or feasibility assumption
where the same economic term has been superseded**.

Distinguish at least:

- executed/signed;
- agreed but awaiting execution;
- binding commitment or lender credit-approved term;
- heads of terms / indicative offer;
- base-case adviser estimate; and
- unresolved management assumption.

A model may legitimately contain unresolved assumptions before close, but they should not be presented as if they
were already contractually fixed.

### Assumption maturity and model convergence

Document authority is only part of the problem. Major PPP assumptions should **converge as the project matures**. A useful
project-specific maturity path is:

`early feasibility estimate → adviser/reference assumption → market-tested assumption → supplier/lender binding term → executed contract → operating actuals/current evidence`

The exact path differs by assumption. The reviewer should identify the maturity state of material inputs such as:

- construction price, escalation and completion date;
- operating cost, lifecycle and performance commitments;
- traffic/demand/resource and tariff assumptions;
- debt amount, tenor, margin, fees and hedging;
- insurance terms;
- government support; and
- commissioning, acceptance and service-start conditions.

A late-stage or operating model should not retain a stale feasibility estimate merely because it was once approved if a
more mature source has superseded it. Conversely, do not replace a contractual base-case assumption with an operating
forecast where the original assumption still has a defined contractual use. The audit question is therefore both **which
source is authoritative** and **whether the assumption has progressed to the maturity expected at the model's date and
purpose**.

### Cross-adviser reconciliation envelope

A bankable infrastructure model normally sits downstream of multiple specialist workstreams. Where those advisers
exist, reconcile the model to the **substance** of their outputs:

- **legal/commercial** — concession term, payment mechanism, relief/change/termination, rights, liabilities,
  contractual milestones, security/direct agreements and support;
- **technical** — design capacity, construction programme, commissioning, availability, efficiency, operating
  regime, maintenance, lifecycle, degradation and handback;
- **traffic/demand/resource** — volume, ramp-up, elasticity, market share, resource yield or customer take-up;
- **cost/QS** — construction cost, committed contracts, escalation, provisional/undefined scope, contingency,
  claims/variations and cost-to-complete;
- **insurance** — required cover, deductibles, business interruption, reinstatement, premium-sharing and proceeds;
- **finance** — facilities, draw conditions, interest/fees, amortisation, reserves, hedges, covenants, security and
  distributions; and
- **tax/accounting** — only where these alter project cash flow, financing capacity or the required infrastructure
  accounting presentation.

Do not assume the financial model is the source of truth merely because it is numerically complete. When the
technical model says 92% availability, the O&M contract guarantees a different standard, and the revenue model
uses a third value, the inconsistency is a substantive infrastructure issue requiring resolution.

### Technical-model bridge

Where a separate technical/operational model feeds the financial model, preserve a traceable bridge from physical
outputs to financial inputs. Examples include:

- hourly or monthly energy output → annual saleable energy;
- traffic model vehicle/passenger flows → chargeable transactions/revenue;
- construction programme → capex profile, IDC and service commencement;
- lifecycle plan → major-maintenance cash flow and reserve requirement;
- water/heat mass or energy balance → billed volume and variable input cost; and
- availability/reliability simulation → contractual payment deductions.

Aggregation must not erase timing, seasonality, curtailment, downtime, peak-capacity or commissioning constraints
that are economically material.

### Contract gaps, ambiguities and disputed interpretations

An executed contract can still be incomplete, internally inconsistent, ambiguous or difficult to operationalise. For a
material model term, do not turn one legal/commercial interpretation into a certain cash flow merely because the workbook
requires a number. Where the issue is genuinely unresolved:

- identify the relevant clause/schedule and competing plausible interpretations;
- distinguish established operating practice from a contractual amendment or waiver;
- determine whether historic practice is binding, discretionary or disputed;
- identify any contractual escalation, expert determination, dispute or variation route; and
- where the value impact is material, retain an explicit alternative/downside treatment until the interpretation is resolved.

This is not a generic legal-document review. The infrastructure-model issue is whether uncertainty in payment, deduction,
indexation, lifecycle, compensation, refinancing, termination or another material economic right is falsely represented as a
single certain model assumption.



## Lender environmental and social bankability — conditional Equator / IFC-style module

Apply this module only where the financing documents, lender policy, DFI/ECA participation, Equator Principles, IFC Performance
Standards or an equivalent project-finance E&S framework actually applies. Do **not** convert it into a generic ESG scorecard.
The financial-model question is whether environmental and social obligations can change **licence to build/operate, timing,
capital cost, operating cost, access to finance, drawability, service availability, remediation, closure or termination economics**.

### Categorisation determines diligence depth, not project value

Where a lender categorises the project by E&S risk, identify the applicable category and the resulting diligence/monitoring scope.
A category is not a valuation haircut and should not be translated into an arbitrary risk premium. Instead trace the actual work
required by the lender/authority, such as:

`screening / categorisation -> ESIA / specialist studies -> management plans / action plan -> consultation / disclosure -> permits / lender conditions -> construction & operations controls -> monitoring / reporting -> corrective action / remedy`

Review whether the model reflects material costs and timing from this chain.

### ESIA / ESMS / action-plan obligations must reach the forecast

For each material environmental or social commitment, identify:

- responsible party and legal/financing source of the obligation;
- completion date or milestone;
- capital and recurring operating cost;
- land/access/resettlement dependency;
- monitoring and independent-verification cost;
- whether compliance is a condition precedent, drawstop, completion test or continuing covenant;
- consequences of non-compliance, including cure, suspension, remediation, compensation, permit risk or lender default; and
- decommissioning/closure or restoration obligation where relevant.

A signed action plan with no budget or schedule link is not financially implemented.

### Stakeholder, resettlement and grievance mechanisms are cash-flow relevant where they alter access or remedy

Where consultation, indigenous/community engagement, livelihood restoration, resettlement, compensation or grievance handling is
material, test whether the project has budgeted the **actual** acquisition/compensation/programme costs and the schedule needed to
secure lawful site access. Avoid two opposite errors:

- assuming every grievance becomes a cash claim; and
- assuming unresolved community/access issues have no schedule or financing consequence.

### Climate assessment under lender E&S standards is not the same as the project climate scenario

Where lenders require physical and/or transition climate assessment, preserve the distinction between:

1. disclosure/diligence compliance; and
2. the actual project cash-flow effect of physical hazards, adaptation measures, carbon/energy rules or operating constraints.

The financial model should reflect only the second where economically material, while still recognising that failure to complete the
first can affect financing availability.

### Independent review and monitoring can be financing conditions

If an independent environmental/social consultant or lender adviser must certify action-plan completion, construction compliance or
ongoing performance, reflect the fee and the timing dependency. A lender sign-off requirement that can delay drawdown or completion
must not be represented as an informational report only.

### E&S covenant breach must be traced through finance remedies

Where a material E&S breach can cause draw suspension, distribution lock-up, mandatory remediation, increased monitoring,
acceleration or termination, test that the downside model follows the actual cure path. Do not assume either immediate lender
enforcement or automatic waiver.

## Brownfield workforce, social-service and local-participation obligations — conditional

Brownfield utilities and service concessions can inherit obligations that do not appear in a greenfield engineering cost
plan. Where the transaction or legal framework makes them material, reconcile:

- employee transfer, retention or redeployment;
- redundancy/severance and transition cost;
- pension, leave or legacy employment obligations that transfer to the project/operator;
- wage harmonisation, collective-bargaining or staffing-minimum commitments;
- training / localisation obligations;
- local ownership, local-partner or domestic-content requirements where they change eligible investors, procurement,
  capex/opex or financing; and
- social-service or universal-service obligations that constrain customer selection, tariff or service standard.

Do not assume the operator can immediately optimise headcount or outsource functions if the concession, labour law or
transition agreement restricts it. Conversely, do not burden the project with public-sector legacy obligations that remain
with the authority.

Stakeholder and social-acceptance issues belong in the financial model only through credible economic pathways — for
example tariff affordability, willingness to pay, connection take-up, land/resettlement delay, service-scope commitments,
permit conditions or compensation. Do not add a generic "stakeholder risk" haircut with no causal link.



## Early PPP scoping, alternatives and reverse revenue requirement — conditional authority/development module

Before a full procurement or financing model exists, an authority or developer may use a deliberately simplified project
model to test whether the proposed service can plausibly support private participation. Treat this as **scoping**, not as a
bankable financing case. The model should expose the size of the commercial gap rather than create false precision.

### Start with the service need, output and do-minimum alternative

An early PPP case should be anchored in the service problem and the minimum performance/output actually required. Where
material, identify:

- the service need and users/beneficiaries;
- minimum performance standards, capacity and quality;
- alternative sites or technical configurations;
- the **do-minimum / defer / rehabilitate / use-existing-capacity** alternative;
- interfaces with existing public assets and services; and
- whether the proposed private scope is genuinely incremental to what government must fund anyway.

A project should not become commercially necessary merely because the preferred technical solution has already been
selected. If a lower-cost alternative can meet the required service for a material period, the financial model should preserve
that comparison at the appropriate appraisal stage.

### Reverse revenue-requirement scoping

A useful early diagnostic can work **backwards** from the project's minimum private-finance cash requirement:

`credible operating cost + lifecycle / essential reserves + debt service + required sponsor cash return + taxes / concession obligations`
`→ approximate required project revenue`
`→ feasible user charges / government payments / ancillary revenue`
`→ residual support or scope gap`

This is not permission to plug revenue to a target IRR. It is a way to reveal whether the service concept is plausibly
financeable and which risk-allocation/payment architecture may be required. The subsequent feasibility and bid models must
replace rough inputs with the appropriate demand study, technical cost, financing evidence and contract mechanics.

Where the revenue requirement exceeds feasible user charges, expose the choice rather than hiding the gap. Depending on the
actual policy and project structure, the solution could involve:

- different scope or service specification;
- a public capital contribution / VGF;
- an availability or performance payment;
- a minimum-revenue or usage support mechanism;
- a longer contract term;
- phased capacity;
- ancillary revenue; or
- a decision not to procure the project as proposed.

Do not import the illustrative equity ratios, return thresholds, construction S-curves or debt tenors in historical scoping
manuals as current finance assumptions.

### Enabling, intermodal and retained public works

A project company's perimeter may exclude access roads, utility relocation, grid/network reinforcement, public land works,
intermodal connections, hospital referral networks, dredging, resettlement works or other enabling infrastructure that is
nonetheless essential to demand, completion or service commencement.

For each material enabling work identify:

`scope / owner → funding source → delivery date → dependency on project completion or demand → remedy/support if late`

The private project should not be shown as fully funded if a critical retained-public work has no funded delivery path.
Conversely, do not charge the SPV for enabling works that remain an authority obligation unless the project agreement
actually transfers them. Where government support funds enabling works rather than the SPV itself, keep that support outside
project revenue unless the financing structure says otherwise.

## Environmental, social, community and consent obligations — financial-model bridge only

Environmental and social material belongs in this skill only where it changes **project economics, schedule, legal
operability or lender requirements**. Do not turn the financial-model audit into a generic ESG checklist. For a material
jurisdictional, financing or contractual obligation, trace:

`applicable requirement / safeguard → assessment / consultation / consent / permit → mitigation or compensation plan`
`→ capex / opex / land / resettlement / schedule / monitoring cost → financing condition / service start / operating obligation`

Examples can include environmental approvals, resettlement/land-access plans, community agreements, Indigenous or
traditional-owner consent/consultation processes where legally or lender-required, cultural-heritage protection, gender or
access commitments, climate/disaster-risk measures, grievance mechanisms and ongoing monitoring.

High-value audit questions include:

- has the model included the **cost and timing** of mandatory mitigation, compensation, relocation, monitoring or community
  commitments that sit with the project;
- are permits/consents a condition to land access, construction, drawdown or operation, and does the programme reflect the
  credible approval path;
- does a change in design/site avoid one impact while creating a different cost or demand consequence;
- is the model relying on a consent or approval that remains unresolved as if it were certain and immediate;
- are lender environmental/social conditions or remedial actions treated as financing conditions where they affect drawability;
  and
- are statutory or lender-specific requirements clearly distinguished from generic good-practice aspirations.

Do not import the Philippine thresholds or classifications from the 2014 draft manual into another jurisdiction. The actual
law, permit, lender standard, project agreement and current project evidence control.

# Common infrastructure economics

## Revenue regime must match the actual risk allocation

The model should first answer **who ultimately pays the project, for what, and subject to what condition**.

For each material revenue stream identify:

- payer: end user, government/authority, regulated customer, access user, contracted shipper/operator or
  ancillary customer;
- unit: vehicle, passenger, tonne, TEU, train path, connection, m3, kWh, capacity unit, floor/service unit or
  fixed availability amount;
- price-setting basis: contract, regulator, tariff law, concession, index formula, market contract or bid price;
- volume/availability basis;
- performance/quality conditions;
- indexation or reopener mechanism;
- collection timing and credit exposure; and
- expiry, termination or reset conditions.

A model that labels revenue “availability payment” but links it to passenger/traffic volume is economically
different from a genuine availability structure. Likewise a real-toll road is not equivalent to a shadow-toll
road or an availability road.

### Demand risk versus revenue risk

For user-pays infrastructure:

`addressable demand → captured demand / utilisation → chargeable units → tariff / price → gross billed revenue → collection → cash revenue`

These links are distinct. Revenue can miss forecast because of:

- fewer users/volumes;
- lower mix or lower-value usage;
- lower approved tariff;
- elasticity response to price;
- leakage/evasion/unbilled use;
- customer credit/default;
- contractual deductions; or
- timing differences.

Do not use “traffic risk” as a catch-all for every revenue risk.

For government-pays infrastructure, end-user volumes may still affect wear, variable operating costs,
consumables, utilities or service intensity even when they do **not** determine revenue. The model should not
silently transfer demand risk to the private party merely because operating costs are volume-sensitive.

### User-pay tariff–service–affordability–support loop

For a material user-pay concession or utility, tariff should not be treated as an independent plug. The economic loop is
usually closer to:

`service scope / quality + capex + O&M + lifecycle + financing → revenue requirement → tariff / charge → affordability and willingness to pay → demand / collection → cash revenue → residual funding gap or surplus`

Where the revenue requirement cannot be reconciled to a tariff that is legally, politically and commercially feasible, the
solution may require a different scope/service level, capital contribution/VGF, recurring subsidy, hybrid payment structure,
contract term or financing structure. The model should expose that gap rather than hiding it in an unsupported tariff uplift.

Where public support is used to close the gap, preserve its actual timing and conditions. An upfront capital contribution
reduces funding needs differently from an output-based or operating subsidy paid only after service or connections are
delivered.

### Tariff structure, reset and subsidy architecture — conditional user-pay / regulated module

For utilities and user-pay concessions, the tariff is often a **designed economic mechanism**, not merely a price input.
Where applicable, test four linked objectives:

1. cost recovery / sustainable asset service;
2. incentives for efficient investment and operations;
3. fairness / affordability across customer classes; and
4. sufficient simplicity for customers and administrators to understand and apply the tariff.

The executed tariff regime may differentiate by customer class, usage block, location, time/peak period, connection
status, service quality or other cost driver. Reconcile the tariff design to the actual cost-to-serve and policy framework.
Where price elasticity is material, a tariff increase should feed back to demand/usage rather than increase revenue
mechanically.

#### Tariff adjustment hierarchy

Long-lived infrastructure rarely relies on one unchanging nominal tariff. Distinguish the contractual/regulatory mechanism:

- **cost pass-through** — selected uncontrollable costs are passed through, sometimes with efficiency limits;
- **indexation** — tariff changes by CPI or a project-specific basket, with defined base date, lag and frequency;
- **periodic reset/review** — tariff is recalibrated using an agreed methodology such as efficient cost, allowed return,
  benchmark or revenue requirement; and
- **extraordinary review** — a specified exceptional event can trigger a special reset.

For a reset, identify the objective, methodology, trigger, review window, evidence standard, lag to implementation,
true-up/backdating and any cap/floor. Do not treat a scheduled review as an automatic increase to the operator's desired
return. Conversely, do not hold tariffs fixed through an event for which the contract/regulator explicitly provides a
reopener.

A pass-through can create weak efficiency incentives if the operator controls quantity or technology. Preserve the
operator-controlled portion of the exposure unless the actual contract transfers it.

#### Subsidy and cross-subsidy design

Where tariffs cannot simultaneously achieve required service, affordability and commercial sustainability, classify the
support mechanism rather than inserting an unexplained revenue plug. Relevant forms can include:

- general operating subsidy;
- targeted subsidy for eligible customer classes;
- connection/access subsidy;
- consumption subsidy;
- cross-subsidy between customer classes or usage blocks;
- capital grant / VGF;
- concessional debt, guarantee or FX support that lowers financing cost;
- in-kind land/resource rights; or
- tax/fee relief where legally available.

Cross-subsidies are funded by other customers rather than by government; they must therefore reconcile across tariff
classes and volumes. A subsidy intended to protect low-income users should not automatically accrue to all customers or
become an uncapped operator cost reimbursement.

Where a general operating subsidy is intended to bridge a transition to cost recovery, test any pre-agreed phase-down
against tariff increases and efficiency improvements. An open-ended reimbursement of actual cost can remove the operator's
incentive to control cost if the contract does not preserve efficiency risk.

#### Connection affordability, service tiers and collection design — conditional

For network services, the principal affordability barrier may be the **connection/access charge** rather than the recurring tariff. Where
relevant, separate connection take-up from ongoing consumption and test whether connection subsidy, instalment plans or other approved
mechanisms change customer adoption and cash collection.

If the contract permits lower-cost service standards or technology for defined customer groups/areas, model the actual scope, capex, opex,
quality and upgrade path. Do not assume one uniform service cost if the PPP deliberately uses differentiated service solutions.

Collection design can also change economics. Prepaid meters, additional payment points, more frequent billing or similar mechanisms may
reduce receivables/default but can require capex and operating cost. Informal/small-scale providers may remain part of the market during a
transition and therefore reduce addressable demand for the concession. Include these effects only where supported by the actual service
strategy.

#### Output-based aid / performance-based subsidy — conditional

Where subsidy is paid only after specified outputs are delivered, trace:

`eligible customer / service area → verified output or connection → performance / service-quality condition → certified subsidy → collection timing`

Output-based support may be:

- one-time, such as a connection subsidy;
- transitional, while tariffs move toward cost recovery; or
- ongoing, supported by a sustainable budget, levy or cross-subsidy.

Do not recognise output-based support on award or construction spend if the contract requires verified service delivery.
Model verification/certification lag, disallowance risk and any continuing performance condition. Where a flat connection
subsidy could incentivise only the cheapest customers to serve, reflect the actual geographic/customer eligibility rules
rather than assuming universal take-up.

### Concession fee / franchise premium — conditional user-pay concession

Some user-pay concessions or franchises require the private party to pay the authority for the right to operate the asset or
collect project revenue. The economic form can include an upfront concession/franchise premium, fixed periodic fee, permit
fee, percentage of revenue, threshold-based sharing or another bid consideration. Where present, treat it as a real project
obligation rather than as an authority-side memorandum item.

Trace, as applicable:

`concession/franchise right → upfront or recurring fee formula → project funding / operating cash flow → tariff and leverage capacity → investor return`

Key modelling questions include:

- whether an upfront premium is funded by equity, senior/subordinated debt or another committed source at close;
- whether a recurring fee is fixed, indexed, revenue-linked, profit-linked or threshold-based;
- whether the fee is senior to distributions or otherwise constrained by the finance waterfall;
- whether the authority payment changes tariff affordability, debt sizing or minimum equity; and
- whether a concession fee, revenue share and refinancing/benefit-sharing payment are genuinely separate obligations rather
  than duplicate labels for the same economic transfer.

An authority receipt is not economically free to the project merely because it occurs at award or financial close. Conversely,
do not invent a concession premium in structures where the authority instead provides capital support or makes availability
payments.

### Variable-term / present-value-of-revenue concessions — conditional

Some user-pay concessions deliberately make the **concession term endogenous to realised demand** rather than fixing both
the tariff and expiry date. A least-present-value-of-revenue or analogous mechanism should be modelled from the actual
contract, not forced into a conventional fixed-term toll-road template.

Where such a mechanism exists, trace:

`eligible contractual revenue → contractual indexation / adjustments → contractual discounting → cumulative present-value revenue → revenue target / bid amount → concession expiry`

Key modelling questions include:

- what revenue counts toward the target: gross tolls, net collections, taxes, compensation, subsidies, penalties or other amounts;
- the contractual discount rate or index and its reset convention;
- whether tariff changes alter the target, the collection period or both;
- minimum or maximum term constraints;
- treatment of force majeure, closure, authority-caused disruption and compensation; and
- how the resulting moving expiry date changes debt maturity/refinancing, lifecycle work, handback, shareholder distributions
  and terminal economics.

Do not hard-code the original expected expiry date if the contract makes expiry a function of cumulative discounted revenue.
Equally, do not introduce a variable-term mechanism into an ordinary fixed-term concession merely because academic literature
finds it useful in some demand-risk settings.

### Revenue floors, caps and sharing are one risk-allocation mechanism — conditional

Where a user-pay PPP contains a minimum-revenue guarantee, revenue support, upside share, revenue cap or similar arrangement,
model the **whole mechanism together**. At minimum identify:

- eligible revenue and measurement period;
- floor / trigger and the exact shortfall funded by government;
- cap or upside-sharing trigger and sharing percentage;
- carry-forward, true-up, clawback or cumulative tests;
- indexation and timing of settlement;
- whether support or sharing changes concession term, tariff reset or termination value; and
- any aggregate cap, expiry or fiscal appropriation condition.

A downside floor and an upside share cannot be modelled as unrelated cash-flow lines if they jointly define the intended demand-
risk allocation. Do not assume that a floor-plus-cap structure is universally optimal; use the executed project mechanism.

## Payment mechanisms and performance deductions — conditional PPP module

Where the project is government-pays or has a performance-linked payment mechanism, the model should
translate the contract into cash economics.

### Service commencement

A government-pays PPP generally should not earn the full operating/service payment before the contractual
service commencement / acceptance condition is achieved. Check the distinction between:

- construction milestone grants;
- capital contributions/VGF;
- pre-service compensation specifically permitted by contract; and
- the recurring service/availability payment.

A financial model that starts the full unitary payment on construction completion while acceptance testing,
commissioning or service commencement occurs later may materially overstate value.

#### Staged, sectional or partial service commencement

Some PPPs permit payment before the whole project reaches final completion because completed facilities, sections, service units or
packages become contractually available in stages. Where that applies, do not force an all-or-nothing revenue start. Reconcile each
partial payment to the **contractual service or completion measure** that earns it, the accepted quantity/area/capacity, the applicable
indexed and fixed payment components, deductions and the date each portion becomes serviceable.

A historical PFI model may use completed-capex percentage as a proxy for partial payment, but that proxy is not a universal rule. A
current model should use capital-progress percentage only if the executed payment mechanism actually makes payment depend on that
measure. Otherwise physical or certified completion should not be replaced by spend merely because spend is easier to model.

### Availability and performance

Where applicable, model:

`maximum payment → availability deductions → performance/quality deductions → other contractual adjustments → net service payment`

The exact mechanism is contract-specific. Relevant features can include:

- unavailable area/asset/service unit;
- criticality weighting;
- duration;
- time-of-day or service-period weighting;
- repeated-failure multipliers;
- rectification/cure periods;
- performance points;
- caps or no caps;
- relief/excusing events;
- government-caused unavailability;
- bonuses where expressly provided; and
- termination/default thresholds arising from persistent underperformance.

Do not invent deductions that the contract does not contain. Conversely, do not model a full undeducted
availability payment under stress while assuming asset outages or service failure.

### Remedy interaction, no-double-recovery and persistent breach

Payment deductions, performance points, liquidated damages, specific-performance rights, cure/rectification obligations,
default notices and termination rights can coexist but are not necessarily cumulative for the same failure. Where the
contract contains a no-double-remedy or equivalent rule, make sure the model does not charge the same economic loss twice.
Conversely, do not erase a separate contractual remedy merely because a payment deduction also applies.

Repeated non-performance can have a different economic path from a single severe failure. Where persistent-breach/default
mechanics exist, follow the actual sequence of warning, final warning, cure/recurrence period, lender cure/step-in and only
then termination where applicable. A minor recurring breach should not become immediate termination merely because a
termination right exists somewhere in the contract; equally, a model that only applies recurring small deductions may miss
the eventual default consequence of persistent failure.

### Service payment should not silently guarantee financing cost

The project must be financeable, but the payment mechanism and the financing package are different layers of the economic
bargain. Unless the contract expressly protects a financing component, do not ring-fence scheduled debt service, interest or
finance charges from performance deductions merely because lenders require payment certainty. A service-based PPP can
transfer availability/performance risk only if poor service can affect the cash available to service private finance in the way
the contract intends.

### Indexation

Availability/service payments often contain indexation intended to reflect underlying cost exposure. A model
should distinguish:

- indexed versus fixed portions;
- the applicable index or basket;
- reference/base date;
- lag;
- frequency;
- floors/caps if contractual;
- rebasing; and
- pass-through items.

Do not automatically index 100% of the payment at CPI when a material portion of the project's economics is
fixed or separately hedged. Equally, do not hold the payment flat while operating/labour costs are explicitly
inflating if the contract provides indexation.

### Pass-through, market testing and benchmarking

Long-duration infrastructure contracts sometimes treat certain costs differently from the core fixed service
payment. Where provided by contract:

- utilities or consumables outside the operator's control may be pass-through or volume-adjusted;
- soft services such as cleaning/catering/security may be periodically market-tested or benchmarked; and
- service scope may be repriced through a prescribed variation mechanism.

The model should not embed a perpetual fixed margin on a service that is contractually re-market-tested, nor
assume full cost pass-through where the project retains efficiency or price risk.

For **reviewable services**, separate the contractual value-testing method from ordinary inflation. A benchmarking exercise
may refer to the cost and market-consistent profit of an efficient alternative provider with the capacity and financial standing
to deliver the service. A market test may instead rely on competitive bids. Some contracts permit escalation from benchmarking
to market testing where benchmark evidence is weak or agreement cannot be reached. The model should reproduce the actual
reset sequence, timing, transaction/transition cost and sharing rule rather than treating the review date as an automatic CPI-
style price reset.

### Operating invoice-to-cash and payment administration — live PPPs

For an operating government-payment PPP, a correct theoretical payment formula is not enough. Reconcile the contractual calculation to
the actual billing and cash-administration process where material:

`service period → gross entitlement → measured deductions/adjustments → invoice/certificate → disputed amount/credit note → tax → due date → cash receipt → late-payment interest or settlement`

Check that invoice timing, certification, deduction disputes, indexation true-ups, additional payments, tax and late-payment mechanics
match the contract and established operating practice. Where actuals exist, bridge billed, accrued, disputed and collected amounts to the
model and working-capital balances. A model can reproduce the unitary-payment formula correctly yet materially misstate liquidity if it
assumes same-period cash receipt when the contractual/actual payment cycle is slower.

## Risk allocation should be economically executable, not merely labelled

A PPP is not improved by transferring the greatest possible number of risks. The durable principle is that risk
should be allocated to the party best able to control, mitigate or bear it at an efficient cost, subject to the
project's actual legal and commercial framework.

For every material risk that changes cash flow, ask:

1. Who contractually bears it?
2. Who can physically control or mitigate it?
3. Is there relief, compensation, insurance, pass-through, tariff reset, time extension or termination protection?
4. Is the model treatment consistent with that allocation?
5. Is the same risk being charged twice — once through explicit cash flows and again through a generic
   contingency/risk premium?

Common infrastructure-specific risk classes include:

- land/site/right-of-way availability;
- design and construction;
- geotechnical/interface risk;
- commissioning and acceptance;
- demand / utilisation;
- tariff / price setting;
- collection;
- service availability / quality;
- lifecycle and asset condition;
- utility/input cost;
- environmental/permit obligations;
- climate/physical resilience;
- change in law;
- force majeure/relief events;
- government action / political risk;
- regulatory reset;
- residual value / handback; and
- termination.

Do not hard-code a universal public/private allocation matrix. Different sectors and jurisdictions allocate the
same risk differently.
