### Risk allocation must align with decision rights

For material risks, also ask **who controls the decisions that determine the project's exposure to that risk**. Efficient
risk allocation is not just about who can influence the external risk factor; it also depends on who can alter the project's
sensitivity to the factor, respond to it, and absorb the consequence.

Examples:

- if government guarantees foreign-exchange losses while sponsors and lenders freely choose the amount and currency of
  borrowing, the protected parties may be able to increase the guaranteed exposure without bearing the incremental risk;
- if an input-cost pass-through exists while the operator controls efficiency/technology, the model should preserve the
  operator-controlled efficiency risk unless the contract expressly transfers it; and
- if demand support protects a floor while the operator controls pricing, service or marketing, the support formula and
  operating decision rights must be read together.

Where one party bears the financial consequence but another controls the exposure, identify contractual caps, approvals,
sharing mechanisms or other controls that prevent the model from assuming economically free risk transfer.

### Whole-life design–build–maintain incentive must actually exist

A DBFM/DBFOM structure may create a whole-life incentive because the private party that chooses design/construction solutions
also bears later maintenance, availability and handback consequences. Do not credit this benefit automatically from the PPP
label. Test whether the economic exposure and decision rights are actually aligned.

For a material value-engineering or capex-saving assumption, bridge:

`design / specification change → construction saving → operating efficiency / reliability → routine maintenance → lifecycle renewals → service deductions / availability → handback condition`

If a D&C contractor captures the construction saving while lifecycle/service downside remains with the authority, another
subcontractor or an uncapped public support mechanism, the project company may not internalise the whole-life consequence.
Likewise, a design choice that reduces upfront capex but increases lifecycle cost should not be presented as a net PPP efficiency
without the downstream effect.

## PPP financial model as contractual economic engine — conditional core module

For a material privately financed PPP, the financial model is not merely a valuation spreadsheet. In many
procurements the preferred bidder's model becomes the **base case financial model** used after financial close to
administer the commercial bargain. It may be used to quantify variations, compensation events, payment resets,
refinancing gains, termination amounts, lifecycle funding and other contractual adjustments. That role materially
changes what an infrastructure reviewer should demand from the model.

Distinguish the following model purposes where they exist:

- **authority / feasibility model** — tests affordability, commercial feasibility, potential government support and
  alternative structures before procurement;
- **public-sector comparator / reference case** — conditional jurisdiction-specific comparison of conventional
  procurement with PPP delivery;
- **bid model** — prices the bidder's actual technical, operating, funding and risk proposition;
- **financial-close model** — reflects the executed finance package, hedging, final construction and O&M contracts,
  accepted payment mechanism and conditions precedent;
- **contractual base case financial model** — agreed economic baseline used for contract adjustments;
- **current forecast / operating model** — incorporates actual performance and current expectations without silently
  rewriting the original contractual base case; and
- **change / refinancing / termination calculation model** — a controlled derivative or update of the agreed base case
  where the contract prescribes such a process.

Do not assume these are interchangeable. A government shadow model may deliberately use hypothetical funding
terms; a bidder model should reflect the bidder's actual solution; an operating forecast may legitimately diverge
from the original base case; and a contractual compensation calculation may be required to preserve a defined
financial position rather than simply use today's unconstrained forecast.

### Project-finance timing resolution follows the economic event frequency

Project finance has distinct development, construction, commissioning, operating, debt-service and expiry phases. The time resolution
used for a material schedule should be fine enough to reproduce the **actual economic timing** of the relevant contract or financing.
Construction funding may require monthly or other short-period draws while operating debt service may be quarterly, semi-annual or another
contractual frequency; taxes, tariff resets and reserve tests can have still different timing.

Do not impose a universal monthly/semi-annual template. Instead, confirm that aggregation does not materially distort:

- construction S-curve and funding need;
- IDC/commitment fees and debt draw timing;
- service commencement and partial-period payment;
- traffic/resource seasonality and outages;
- tariff/index reset dates;
- debt service, reserve funding and covenant measurement; or
- tax/payment timing that changes CFADS or distributions.

An annual-only project model can be adequate at very early pre-feasibility, but it is weak evidence for a financing or contractual decision
where intra-year timing determines liquidity, debt service or payment rights.

### Procurement common assumptions versus bidder-owned risk

A procurement may prescribe common macroeconomic, tax, index, timing or formatting assumptions so competing bids can be
compared on a consistent basis. Distinguish these **authority-mandated common assumptions** from assumptions that remain
the bidder's commercial risk, such as construction productivity, O&M efficiency, lifecycle strategy, financing margin or
demand response unless the RFP expressly standardises them.

Do not infer that an assumption is protected or compensable merely because all bidders were instructed to use the same
value for bid evaluation. At financial close, establish whether that assumption remains contractual, is reset by an agreed
mechanism, or has been replaced by executed market terms.

### Contract-to-model mapping

For PPPs where the model is part of the commercial framework, trace the economic schedules and clauses into the
model. Depending on the contract, this can include:

- base date and price date;
- construction programme and milestone dates;
- service commencement / availability commencement;
- maximum unitary or availability payment;
- payment-mechanism formulas and deduction rules;
- indexation formula, proportions, lag and rebasing;
- pass-through costs;
- change / variation compensation principles;
- relief and compensation events;
- force majeure treatment;
- insurance cost-sharing or premium-review mechanisms;
- lifecycle / renewal obligations;
- benchmarking and market testing;
- refinancing-gain sharing;
- revenue/profit sharing where applicable;
- termination-compensation schedules;
- handback requirements; and
- concession expiry or extension rights.

The key review question is not whether a clause has a similarly named line in the workbook. It is whether the
**economic consequence defined by the executed contract is actually reproduced by the model**.

### Representative contract-event testing

For economically material contractual mechanisms, test at least one **worked event** that is realistic for the project
rather than relying only on a zero-event base case. Depending on the contract this can include:

- a defined availability/performance failure;
- a tariff/index review;
- a market-testing or benchmarking reset;
- a qualifying variation/compensation event;
- a refinancing subject to gain sharing;
- a force-majeure/change-in-law event; or
- an early-termination calculation.

The purpose is domain-specific: prove that the contract's thresholds, lags, caps, sharing, cure periods and economic
rebalancing behave as the parties' bargain requires under a real scenario. A mechanism that produces the right answer only
when every event input is zero has not demonstrated that it models the concession correctly.

### Base case versus actuals and forecast

Post-close PPP models should preserve the distinction between:

`contractual base case → actual historic outturn → current forecast → contractual adjustment calculation`

Actual performance should inform current forecasting and contract management, but should not automatically rewrite
historic base-case assumptions where the base case is needed to calculate compensation, refinancing gains or other
contractual economic adjustments. Conversely, a stale contractual base case should not be presented as the best
estimate of future operating performance merely because it remains legally relevant.

Where a contract requires base-case updates, establish what is permitted to change: actual historical cash flows,
forecast assumptions, financing terms, formula structure, contract changes or only specified items. Do not assume a
model can be freely rebased after every event.

### Post-close operating-model conversion and financial-close-equivalent calibration

A live PPP operating model may be structurally different from the transaction-closing model because it must incorporate actual
accounts, current forecasts and management/lender reporting. Before relying on that new structure, establish a controlled
**financial-close-equivalent calibration state**:

`financial-close inputs / opening position → operating-model logic → financial-close-equivalent outputs → explained conversion differences`

Where practicable, load the applicable financial-close assumptions into the operating-model structure and reconcile the material P&L,
cash flow, balance sheet, debt, lender ratios and shareholder outputs to the agreed financial-close model. Differences caused by
deliberate structural improvements, timing granularity or changed accounting/reporting presentation should be explained rather than
plugged. Only after the conversion bridge is understood should actual outturn and current reforecast assumptions be treated as the
source of economic divergence.

This calibration is not a generic requirement that every new workbook reproduce every old cell. Its purpose is PPP-specific: prove that
moving the concession from a closing model into a long-lived operating model has not silently changed the contractual economics.

### Latest actual position is the opening point for a current operating forecast

Once a concession is live, the current forecast should normally open from the latest reliable actual financial position rather than the
financial-close balance sheet that would have existed if every original forecast had occurred exactly as planned. In particular, actual
cash, senior and subordinated debt, reserves, receivables/payables, tax balances and other material working-capital positions can differ
substantially from the old base case.

For a live operating model, reconcile:

`latest actual balance sheet + actual cash-flow history → forecast opening balances → future contractual/operating cash flows`

Do not overwrite the contractual base case where it remains legally relevant. Maintain both views where necessary: the contractual
baseline for contract calculations and the actual opening position for the best-estimate forecast.

### Actual-account mapping and working-capital unwind

Actual P&L, cash-flow and balance-sheet lines can need different mappings for statutory accounts, financial-close/base-case reporting,
lender CFADS and internal management reporting. For material items, preserve a documented economic classification so that the same
actual cash flow is not omitted, duplicated or moved into a more favourable lender category merely because report formats differ.

Pay particular attention to opening receivables, payables, accruals, prepayments and other current assets/liabilities. Their future cash
unwind should follow the underlying invoice/service/payment timing. An opening accrual should not disappear at the cut-over date, and
a prepayment should not generate an arbitrary first- or final-period cash flow merely because the operating forecast changed period
granularity.

### Actual-to-reforecast and total-contract bridge

A mature operating PPP should be able to explain the relationship between:

`financial-close/base-case expectation → actual outturn to reporting date → current remaining forecast → total-contract current outcome`

Use this bridge for the key stakeholder outputs where material: service/unitary payments, operating and lifecycle costs, liquidity, debt,
lender ratios and shareholder cash flows/returns. Variance should be attributable to actual performance, timing, approved contract
changes, financing events and current forecast assumptions rather than an unexplained model conversion difference.

### Actual shareholder and lender history must remain history

For current sponsor-return analysis, preserve the dated equity contributions, shareholder/subordinated-loan advances and repayments,
interest/fees and distributions that actually occurred from inception to the reporting date, then append the future forecast. Where
sponsor ownership or instruments differ, maintain sponsor-specific cash flows when that difference affects return or sale economics. Do
not recalculate historical sponsor returns using today's forecast timing.

Likewise, live lender analysis should preserve actual historical CFADS, debt service, debt balances, reserve movements and covenant
measurements according to the finance documents, then connect them to the forward reforecast. A current reforecast should not rewrite a
historic debt-service period that has already occurred.

### Periodic lender-update bridge

Where lenders receive recurring operating-model updates, bridge the current submission to the prior agreed/audited operating version or
other lender-recognised baseline. At minimum explain material changes in actual financial position, forecast assumptions, contractual
events, model logic where changed, financial statements and lender ratios. This is distinct from generic file change control: the domain
question is whether a lender can understand **why the concession's credit forecast changed** between reporting dates.

### Operating model to management-reporting interface — conditional

A PPP may use a separate management-reporting layer with more granular monthly actual/budget/reforecast views than the contractual or
lender model. If that layer is relied on for board or concession management decisions, reconcile its material actual, base/budget and
reforecast figures to the same operating model and financial accounts. Different report classifications are acceptable; unexplained
economic differences are not.


## Operational PPP financial model and contract-life management — conditional authority module

A long-term PPP does not become economically self-managing once the contract is signed. Where the authority is responsible for
administering a live PPP, the operational financial model should be capable of reproducing the **current contractual payment and
financial consequences** throughout construction, service delivery, change and exit. This is a domain requirement created by the
PPP bargain, not a generic workbook-design preference.

### Operational payment administration

For an authority-pays PPP, the operating model should be able to calculate the periodic amount actually payable after applying the
contract's current mechanics. Depending on the contract, bridge:

`base contractual payment → indexation/rebasing → certified availability/service → performance deductions/bonuses → pass-throughs/true-ups → compensation events → invoice/payment timing → cash paid`

The model should distinguish recurring adjustments from ad hoc compensation. Examples include:

- monthly/quarterly inflation indexation;
- availability and performance deductions;
- bonuses or service credits where applicable;
- utility or other pass-through true-ups;
- qualifying delay/additional-cost relief;
- force-majeure/change-in-law compensation; and
- prior-period corrections or disputed amounts.

Do not rely on the original bid payment when the live contract requires regular indexation or performance adjustment. Conversely,
do not treat every adverse event as authority compensation merely because the model contains a generic change input.

### User-pay concession monitoring

For a user-pay PPP, the authority's operating model may need to monitor more than the SPV's ability to service debt. Where the contract
contains tariff-reset, profit-sharing, profit-capping, revenue-sharing or financial-equilibrium provisions, the current forecast should
reproduce those mechanisms and test long-run commercial sustainability under actual demand and tariff outcomes.

Where a tariff is periodically reset, distinguish:

- indexation that is formulaic;
- demand/revenue true-up;
- regulated or contractual review;
- profit/revenue sharing;
- cap/floor or extraordinary rebalancing; and
- adjustments requiring approval rather than automatic entitlement.

### Operational-model use for changes, refinancing and termination

A live authority model should be capable, where relevant, of quantifying:

- additional capacity or service scope;
- authority-requested changes and private-partner proposals;
- change financing and revised payment profile;
- refinancing and any contractual gain sharing;
- revised debt/equity gearing where the refinancing definition captures it;
- compensation-event payments;
- current lender/financial indicators; and
- early-termination compensation under the applicable termination cause.

The model should not price only the direct construction cost of a change. Trace the full knock-on effect across service commencement,
operating costs, lifecycle, tax where contractually relevant, financing, insurance, payment, deductions, handback and termination.

### Actuals, historic performance and the shared operating baseline

For operational-management purposes, the model should support the transition from forecast to actual while retaining the contractual
baseline needed for the bargain. In addition to the actual-to-reforecast principles above, preserve enough historic financial and service-
performance data to explain:

`contract baseline → actual performance/payment → current forecast → current contract value / future authority commitment`

EPEC contract-life guidance identifies a single shared operational model as a useful way to reduce recurring reconciliation disputes while
allowing each party to retain its own assumptions. Apply that only where the contract/governance supports it. If two economic models are
maintained, reconcile the material contractual outputs rather than allowing separate authority and private-partner models to create two
inconsistent versions of the payment mechanism.

Where the contract requires model updates, external review/audit or transparent model sharing, treat those provisions as part of the
commercial administration of the PPP. Do not convert this into a generic requirement that every infrastructure workbook be externally
audited or shared.

### Periodic PPP health checks and savings — conditional

Long-duration PPPs should be reviewed periodically or when a material trigger arises if the governing contract/authority process provides
for such review. A useful review can combine technical, financial and legal evidence to determine whether the project remains fit for
purpose, whether required services are being delivered and whether the economic bargain has materially changed.

Potential savings can arise from:

- improved delivery efficiency;
- lower market prices for contestable services;
- changed authority requirements;
- changed financing conditions;
- changed insurance pricing/availability;
- technology or energy-efficiency improvements; or
- other contractually shareable gains.

Do not create short-term savings by stripping maintenance, lifecycle or service resources that are required to sustain availability,
handback condition or long-run value for money. A lower current-year O&M line is not a genuine saving if it merely defers a larger lifecycle,
deduction or handback cost.

Where the contract uses **market testing** or **benchmarking** for soft services or other contestable inputs, apply it only where a credible
comparison market exists and use the contract's reset mechanism. Do not infer a market reset for monopoly-like, highly bespoke or non-
contestable services merely because a review date occurs.

### Refinancing review during the operating phase

A refinancing opportunity can arise because initial debt was shorter than the concession, a mini-perm requires take-out, project risk has
fallen, or financing markets have improved. Where the contract regulates refinancing, trace:

`refinancing proposal → authority notification/consent → new debt/equity structure → fees/hedge break costs → refinancing gain → contractual sharing → revised authority payment or cash settlement`

Do not treat a refinancing as an SPV-only event if the contract grants the authority consent rights or a share of gain. Conversely, do not
assume the authority can compel a refinancing unless the contract provides such a right.

### Insurance review during contract life

Operational insurance should be tested against the **current** project scope and market, not only the financial-close schedule. Where
material, reconcile:

- required coverage and deductibles;
- changes in services/assets;
- policy expiry/renewal;
- market availability and uninsurability provisions;
- overlap or gaps between authority and private-party cover;
- alternative risk mitigation when cover becomes unavailable; and
- contractual insurance-cost sharing/savings mechanisms.

A fall in insurance premium is not automatically distributable savings, and loss of cover is not automatically an authority risk. Follow
the contract's allocation.

### Cumulative change control and future-change financing

PPP change should be assessed cumulatively from contract signature. A series of individually small amendments can materially alter service
scope, risk allocation, payment economics, procurement perimeter or value for money.

For any material change, identify:

- initiator and contractual change route;
- approval and effective date;
- capex/opex/lifecycle effect;
- financing source and obligation to finance;
- payment/tariff adjustment;
- effects on insurance and performance standards;
- interaction with prior amendments; and
- downstream termination/handback consequences.

Where future changes require capital expenditure, do not assume the original financing package can fund them. Apply the agreed change-
financing rules, including any limit on the private partner's obligation to arrange incremental finance.

### End-of-contract operational management

Natural expiry and early termination require an authority service-continuity and financial transition plan. Where relevant, the operating
model should capture:

- continued service through the transition period;
- technical audit and revised maintenance/remedial plan;
- data/information transfer needed for retendering or in-house operation;
- staff-transfer obligations where applicable;
- residual lifecycle/handback/remedial expenditure;
- cash reserve, bond or other security supporting late-life liabilities;
- termination/expiry settlement amounts; and
- final accounting/cash settlement between the parties.

A key late-life risk is that remedial/handback obligations can exceed the remaining project cash flow. Where the authority relies on a
handback reserve, escrow, performance bond or other security, reconcile the amount, funding build-up, expiry and claim conditions to the
actual remedial exposure. Do not assume the private partner will self-fund a large final-year obligation from cash that no longer exists.


## Operating PFI / PPP project-company financial stress — conditional authority and lender-monitoring module

Apply this module to an operating availability/PFI-style project where performance failures, deductions, defects, lifecycle obligations,
subcontractor distress or financing weakness can threaten the SPV's solvency or ability to deliver service. The UK NISTA guidance is a
current legacy-PFI operating reference; apply its principles through the actual project agreement, finance documents and local insolvency
framework rather than treating UK historical funding ratios or liability periods as universal.

### Limited recourse means the project may have no automatic source of rescue capital

A project company can be highly leveraged yet have very little obligation from shareholders or lenders to inject new money beyond their
committed funding. In distress, identify:

- legally committed undrawn equity/support;
- remaining shareholder/subordinated-loan obligations;
- reserve and liquidity availability;
- contractor/service-provider recoveries;
- insurance and guarantees;
- additional-debt capacity and lender consent;
- voluntary sponsor rescue appetite; and
- the point at which the project runs out of cash.

Do not model an automatic equity plug merely because shareholders would prefer to avoid insolvency. Sponsor rescue is a scenario only if it
is contractually committed or deliberately assumed and clearly labelled.

### Performance deductions can become a solvency event

In an availability/payment-mechanism PPP, operational failure can create a circular deterioration:

`service failure / defect → payment deductions → lower project cash → reduced ability to fund rectification/lifecycle →
further failure/deductions → covenant/reserve breach → financial distress`

Where material, test the **cash timing** of both deduction and remediation. The project may have to fund repairs before responsibility,
insurance, subcontract recovery or dispute settlement is resolved.

An authority should model the consequences of contractual remedies as well as the entitlement itself in a serious distress case. This does
not mean waiving valid deductions by default. Where the authority considers temporary suspension, settlement or sequencing of remedies to
protect service continuity or achieve rectification, preserve the accrued contractual entitlement and model the alternative strategies and
termination consequences explicitly.

### Risk pass-down is limited by subcontract terms

Do not assume project-company deductions are perfectly passed to the responsible subcontractor. Compare the project agreement and each
relevant subcontract for:

- scope and performance standard;
- causation/attribution;
- payment-deduction pass-through;
- liability cap and sub-cap;
- exclusion of consequential loss;
- cure/rectification obligation;
- warranty/defects period;
- termination liability;
- security/guarantee;
- insolvency;
- dispute resolution; and
- recovery timing.

The project company retains the **gap** between authority loss/deduction and recoverable subcontract amount.

### Service-provider liability caps can create retained SPV loss

Where payment deductions can ratchet or accumulate over long periods, compare the gross project-agreement deduction to the relevant
service-provider liability cap. Test:

`authority deduction → attributable subcontract liability → contractual cap / exclusions → collectible amount → residual SPV loss`

Do not import NISTA's historical example percentages as standard caps. Use the actual subcontract. A project can be economically distressed
even when the service provider is technically in breach if the cap has already been exhausted.

### Construction defects can migrate back to the project company over time

For an operating project with latent or compliance defects, identify whether the construction contractor remains liable and whether the
claim is still enforceable. Review:

- defects/warranty limitation period;
- latent-defect rights;
- contractual limitation period;
- parent guarantee/bond expiry;
- design liability;
- exclusions/settlements/waivers; and
- contractor solvency.

A defect discovered after contractor liability/security has expired can become a direct SPV lifecycle/rectification exposure. Do not assume
the original construction risk allocation persists unchanged throughout a 20–30 year concession.

### Disputed deductions create attribution and liquidity risk

A deduction can relate to service performance, maintenance, lifecycle failure, construction defect or another cause. Where responsibility is
disputed:

`gross deduction / remediation cost → provisional attribution → cash paid/withheld now → dispute/expert determination →
ultimate recovery / settlement`

Model the current cash position separately from the ultimate economic entitlement. A project can fail liquidity tests before a later
subcontract recovery is received.

### Lifecycle sufficiency becomes a solvency test near expiry

For a project approaching handback, compare:

`remaining project cash + restricted lifecycle funding + forecast shareholder cash that can legally be retained / redirected +
collectible third-party support`

with:

`credible remaining lifecycle + defect rectification + handback remediation + transition + debt / reserve obligations`

Where the required work exceeds available project resources, the project can be economically insolvent even if lifetime investor IRR still
looks healthy. This late-life test should be informed by condition surveys and current cost estimates rather than the original lifecycle
budget.

### Replacement service-provider risk is a project-company cost

If a key hard/soft FM or specialist service provider fails or is terminated, model:

- procurement and mobilisation delay;
- replacement price;
- changed risk allocation / liability cap;
- temporary service failure and deductions;
- transition/knowledge-transfer cost;
- existing defects and disputed responsibility;
- bond/guarantee/termination recovery; and
- lender/authority consent where required.

Do not assume replacement occurs at the original contract price or instantaneously.

### Contract financial model, lender model and shareholder model can legitimately differ

For a distressed operating project distinguish:

- **financial-close / contract model** — contractual baseline for variation, refinancing, termination or other project-agreement calculations;
- **lender / operational model** — current actuals and forecast used to monitor financing covenants and repayment; and
- **shareholder return model** — sponsor view of remaining value, which may use different permitted assumptions.

Do not use the financial-close model as the sole evidence of current solvency. Conversely, do not let a shareholder model overwrite
contractual economics needed for legal calculations.

Where the authority has information rights to lender reporting/models, use those rights according to the contract. Absence of contractual
access should be identified as an information constraint, not solved by pretending the authority has lender data.

### Current and forward coverage must be read against the actual finance thresholds

Monitor DSCR/ADSCR, LLCR/PLCR and other covenant metrics through the remaining debt life. Distinguish:

- ordinary distribution threshold;
- cash-trap / lock-up threshold;
- default threshold;
- cure rights;
- reserve cure;
- equity cure;
- waiver; and
- acceleration/enforcement.

Do not import illustrative NISTA thresholds. The actual loan agreement controls. A forecast breach several periods ahead can be an early
warning before any current default occurs.

### Remaining shareholder cash is a forward shock-absorption metric

Lifetime IRR can remain high because shareholders received large historic distributions, refinancing proceeds or shareholder-loan payments
before distress arose. Therefore separately calculate or inspect the **remaining sponsor value still inside the project**:

- future dividends;
- shareholder-loan interest;
- shareholder-loan principal;
- other permitted sponsor payments; and
- committed but undrawn sponsor support.

Low remaining distributions can reduce shareholders' economic incentive to rescue the project. Do not use historical/lifetime IRR as a proxy
for future loss-absorption capacity.

Where ownership changed in the secondary market, the original project IRR can be particularly uninformative for the current owner's rescue
decision because its acquisition price and return basis differ.

### Reserve underfunding is both a symptom and an accelerant of distress

Compare required and actual balances for DSRA, lifecycle/MRA and other restricted reserves. For each shortfall identify:

- contractual target;
- funding priority;
- permitted draw;
- replenishment requirement;
- event-of-default consequence;
- current liquidity benefit from not funding; and
- future cash requirement created.

A temporarily higher unrestricted cash balance caused by reserve underfunding is not financial strength.

### Lender reports and waiver activity are forward-looking credit evidence

Where available, review material:

- covenant certificates;
- waiver requests;
- lender default notices;
- material-adverse-effect notices;
- reserve shortfalls;
- disputes/deductions;
- updated operating forecasts;
- restructuring discussions; and
- movement of the exposure from routine portfolio management to workout/restructuring.

A waiver can prevent immediate default while signalling that the unwaived financing case no longer works. Model the waiver terms, expiry,
fees and cure requirements rather than treating "waived" as "resolved".

### Statutory accounts provide complementary distress signals

For an operating SPV, statutory accounts are not a substitute for the lender model, but can reveal:

- going-concern uncertainty;
- material disputes/provisions;
- impairment;
- overdue liabilities;
- auditor emphasis or qualification;
- related-party support;
- covenant issues; and
- unusual filing/reporting delay.

Reconcile accounting disclosures to the project's cash-flow position where material. A profitable P&L does not disprove liquidity distress,
and a healthy lender ratio should be challenged if the accounts disclose material uncertainty not reflected in the model.

### External ratings and market signals — conditional

Where project debt is publicly rated or traded, rating migration and rating-agency commentary can be evidence of deterioration in:

- project performance;
- counterparty risk;
- covenant headroom;
- refinancing;
- structural protection; or
- sovereign/regulatory risk.

Use the rating rationale, not merely the alphanumeric grade. Do not require a rating for bank-funded projects that do not have one.

### Non-model behavioural signals can corroborate financial stress

Where documented and relevant, signs such as late subcontractor payments, more aggressive claims behaviour, unexpected senior-management
changes, workout advisers, large unresolved disputes and increased lender monitoring can corroborate modelled stress. These indicators are
not standalone financial-model errors; use them to challenge whether the current forecast adequately reflects the operating reality.

### Distress strategy should compare service-continuity outcomes, not only contractual recoveries

For an authority facing a materially distressed PPP, compare coherent pathways such as:

- strict enforcement with deductions;
- negotiated rectification plan;
- temporary remedy suspension with preserved rights;
- sponsor/lender cure;
- subcontractor replacement;
- debt restructuring;
- consensual contract change; and
- termination/step-in/reprocurement.

The preferred strategy is contract- and fact-specific. The model should expose authority cash, service continuity, lifecycle/handback,
termination and transition consequences of each scenario rather than assuming the maximum immediate deduction always maximises public value.

## Limited-recourse project-finance economics — conditional PPP / concession module

Generic debt arithmetic belongs to the core audit framework. This section addresses the **infrastructure-specific
cash-flow and contractual relationships created by limited-recourse project finance**.

A project-financed SPV is expected to service debt primarily from project cash flows. Therefore funding capacity is
constrained by the project's contractual and physical ability to generate cash, not merely by a sponsor's corporate
balance sheet.

The model should distinguish at least:

`project operating cash flow → CFADS / debt-service cash flow → senior debt service → reserve funding / release → permitted subordinated payments → permitted equity distributions`

The exact waterfall is finance-document specific. Do not impose a generic ordering if the executed finance documents
specify another one.



### Specialised-lending / lender rating lens — conditional

Where a bank applies the Basel specialised-lending framework or an equivalent internal project/IPRE rating methodology, do not reduce the
credit case to one DSCR or LTV. Reconcile the lender's qualitative grade to the actual project evidence across the main categories used in
specialised lending, including:

- financial strength and stress resilience;
- political/legal/regulatory environment;
- transaction characteristics, maturity and refinancing exposure;
- construction/operating/market risk;
- strength, experience and financial capacity of sponsor/developer and key counterparties; and
- security package, controls, assignments, reserves and lender step-in/recovery rights.

For income-producing real estate, include market conditions, property quality/location, lease/tenant profile and sponsor/developer strength.
For project finance, include completion risk, operating performance, offtake/demand risk and contract/security structure.

The supervisory 'strong/good/satisfactory/weak' labels, where applicable, are **credit-category outputs**, not financial-model assumptions.
Do not reverse-engineer a stronger category by changing the model case. Instead use the model to evidence whether the assigned category is
credible under the lender's current methodology.

### Structuring model versus risk-analysis model

A project-finance model can serve two different infrastructure credit purposes and the reviewer should not confuse them.

- In a **structuring model**, debt quantum, tenor, amortisation/sculpting, pricing, reserve package and other protections
  are variables chosen in response to the project's physical, contractual and downside cash-flow risk.
- In a **risk-analysis / monitoring model**, the executed debt package is generally a fact; the primary question is whether
  the project can perform under base and downside cases and what contractual remedies follow if it cannot.

Do not "improve" an executed financing in a risk review by silently re-sculpting it to pass coverage. Conversely, do not
assume a proposed financing is bankable merely because a fixed debt amount can be inserted into the model.

### Financing structure can affect the PPP bid/payment requirement — conditional

For capital-intensive infrastructure, financing terms can materially change the tariff, availability payment or bid price
required to achieve a given sponsor return. Where the model is used to price or compare bids, trace the effect of material
financing choices — debt size, funding sequence, tenor, amortisation, rate/fees, hedging and reserves — into the required
project payment.

This does **not** mean financing can rescue an uneconomic project. Covenants, reserves, subordinated debt and cash sweeps
can redistribute timing and reduce lender loss in some states; they cannot create missing operating cash flow or repair a
fundamentally uncompetitive technology/cost structure.

### Sources and uses through construction

For a greenfield or major expansion case, reconcile the complete funding requirement to the funding package. Sources
may include:

- sponsor equity;
- shareholder/subordinated loans;
- senior bank debt;
- project bonds;
- multilateral / development-bank facilities;
- government capital contributions, grants or viability-gap funding;
- standby / contingency facilities;
- working-capital or VAT/GST facilities where relevant; and
- interest income or other permitted funding sources.

Uses may include:

- design and construction;
- development / bid / transaction costs where project-borne;
- land or concession payments;
- financing fees;
- commitment / availability fees on undrawn debt;
- interest during construction;
- hedging premiums or break/funding costs where applicable;
- reserve-account prefunding;
- taxes on construction-phase transactions where project-borne; and
- contingency.

Check the **timing** of funding, not just the total sources-and-uses equality. A facility that is contractually
available only after conditions precedent cannot fund earlier expenditure. A grant paid at service commencement does
not remove the need for bridge funding during construction unless a bridge facility is actually modelled.

### Deemed equity, pre-COD income and non-cash project-cost items — conditional

Project-finance documents can define "equity", "project cost" and sponsor funding differently from the cash actually paid
by shareholders. Where material, distinguish:

- actual sponsor cash equity;
- shareholder/subordinated loan funding;
- pre-COD operating income retained in the project;
- development fees or owner costs that are eligible project costs;
- capitalised financing costs; and
- grants, credits or other support counted toward project funding.

A loan agreement may credit some of these items toward an equity or project-cost requirement. That does not make them
identical to dated sponsor cash contributions for equity-return calculations. Reconcile both views explicitly.

Distinguish **interest added to the debt balance** from **interest capitalised into accounting/tax asset basis**. The same
word "capitalised interest" is often used for both, but the cash, debt and tax consequences can differ.

Internal development fees/owner costs can be legitimate project costs, but where they are paid to or recognised by a
sponsor affiliate they can create a gain/tax consequence outside the SPV while not representing an equivalent third-party
cash outflow. Do not count the same amount as external construction spend, sponsor cash equity and sponsor income. Apply
the current jurisdiction-specific tax treatment rather than historical examples.

### Equity timing and completion support

The model should reflect the agreed funding sequence: equity first, pro rata, milestone-based, minimum-equity-first,
equity bridge or another structure. Do not assume shareholder funds can be deferred until after senior debt has funded
construction if finance documents require earlier equity support.

Where sponsors or contractors provide completion guarantees, contingent equity, cost-overrun support or standby
facilities during construction, distinguish that **committed support** from optional future sponsor generosity.
Likewise, support that expires at completion should not be treated as permanent operating liquidity.

Where the concession, finance documents or shareholder arrangements restrict sponsor sell-down or require a strategic
operator/sponsor to retain a minimum interest through construction, commissioning or an operating seasoning period, reflect
that restriction in equity-transfer/refinancing scenarios. The economic quality of sponsor support can change materially once
the party providing construction, operating or technical capability is permitted to exit. Do not assume pre-completion
sponsor support remains available after an allowed transfer unless the relevant obligation survives the transfer.

#### Completion tests and release of sponsor / completion support

Physical or contractual practical completion, service commencement and the **finance-defined completion test** can occur on
different dates. Where lenders or sponsors condition the release of guarantees, contingent equity, cost-overrun support or
other completion undertakings on a finance-defined completion test, model that test rather than automatically releasing support
at PC or COD.

Depending on the executed documents, release may require some combination of:

- independent engineer/certifier confirmation of substantial or final completion;
- objective technical and performance-capacity tests;
- reliability or minimum operating-period/seasoning requirements;
- all material punch-list, defect and remaining cost-to-complete items to be fully funded;
- required reserve accounts to be funded;
- no unresolved completion condition that prevents the project from delivering the contracted service; and
- specified financial ratios or operating performance thresholds where the finance package expressly requires them.

Do not manufacture a generic completion checklist. The project agreement, construction contract, technical certification
regime and finance documents determine the actual release conditions. The high-value model question is whether support is
released **before the financing package says the high-risk completion period has actually ended**.

### Public capital contributions must preserve the intended private capital at risk

Where government makes a construction-period capital contribution, grant or milestone payment, assess more than the total
public/private funding split. The contract or procurement may require the public contribution to follow **actual achieved
milestones** and to preserve a defined base-case relationship between public funding and private debt/equity drawn. This can
matter if construction or private-finance drawdowns are delayed.

Where relevant, test that:

- public cash is not paid purely because a scheduled date arrived when the qualifying work/risk reduction did not;
- delayed or reduced private funding does not cause public funding to move ahead of the agreed risk-sharing position;
- an equity bridge changes timing without eliminating the sponsor equity amount that is meant to remain at risk; and
- grants, public equity, guarantees and other government support are considered together when judging the residual private
  downside.

Do not impose a universal private/public funding ratio. Apply the project agreement, funding deed and procurement terms.

### Public support classification: construction funding versus operating revenue

Infrastructure PPPs can receive public support in economically different forms. A construction-period grant, viability-gap
funding payment or milestone capital contribution is not automatically the same thing as an availability/service payment,
operating subsidy, revenue-support payment or contingent guarantee. Classify each support instrument according to the project
agreement, funding deed, finance documents and lender cash-flow definition.

A useful bridge is:

`support instrument → contractual trigger → eligible use → timing → accounting/cash treatment → CFADS/debt-sizing treatment → repayment/clawback if any`

Particular care is required where the same public cash is shown in more than one model layer. A capital contribution can reduce
the construction funding requirement; it should not also inflate recurring operating CFADS or debt capacity unless the financing
documents expressly permit that treatment. Conversely, an operating/service payment should not be treated as permanent equity
or an unconditional construction grant merely because the payer is government.

Hybrid structures are possible. The point is not the label but the **economic perimeter and timing** of the public support.

### CFADS definition must follow the financing package

CFADS is a financing concept, not a universal accounting subtotal. Confirm the project's actual definition and the
cash-flow perimeter used by lenders. Material differences can arise from treatment of:

- taxes;
- working capital;
- lifecycle / major maintenance;
- reserve contributions and releases;
- insurance proceeds;
- government support;
- compensation-event receipts;
- subordinated debt service;
- exceptional operating costs; and
- permitted ancillary cash flows.

A model can calculate DSCR correctly from a mathematical perspective and still misstate bankability if the numerator
is not the CFADS definition required by the finance documents.

### Debt sizing, sculpting and debt tail

Where debt is sized or amortised by project cash flow, test the economic relationship rather than forcing a particular
technique. Debt capacity may be constrained by some combination of:

- target/minimum DSCR;
- LLCR / PLCR;
- maximum leverage or minimum equity;
- debt maturity;
- concession / contract maturity;
- minimum debt tail between final repayment and concession expiry;
- lender downside cases;
- reserve requirements;
- construction-completion exposure; and
- market debt quantum / tenor.

A sculpted repayment profile should follow the finance definition of available debt-service cash flow and the stated
coverage target. Equal amortisation, annuity or bullet/mini-perm structures may also be appropriate where that is the
actual financing.

Do **not** import generic DSCR, LLCR, gearing or tail thresholds. The relevant thresholds are transaction-specific.

A project that repays debt exactly on concession expiry with no contractual or practical tail is not automatically
wrong, but the absence of tail should be understood because it removes a common lender buffer against operating
underperformance or delayed cash generation.

#### Coverage horizon and break-even resilience

DSCR, LLCR and PLCR answer different infrastructure-credit questions:

- **DSCR** tests cash coverage over the relevant debt-service period and is sensitive to short-term liquidity/default risk;
- **LLCR** tests the present value of lender-defined cash flow over the remaining loan life against debt outstanding; and
- **PLCR** extends the cash-flow horizon through the project/concession life and therefore captures the value of the tail
  beyond debt maturity where that tail is economically available.

A strong PLCR cannot cure a near-term liquidity default if cash cannot bridge to the tail. Conversely, a weak single-period
DSCR does not necessarily imply ultimate principal loss if reserves, cure rights and later project cash are available.
Interpret the ratios with the contractual cash waterfall and timing.

Under a simplified proportional-cash-flow case, a useful diagnostic is:

`break-even cash-flow reduction ≈ (coverage ratio - 1) / coverage ratio`

The same intuition can be applied to DSCR, LLCR or PLCR over the matching horizon. Treat this only as a screening tool.
Fixed costs, taxes, volume-dependent costs, reserve draws, non-linear tariffs, deductions and default mechanics can make the
true break-even materially different; use the full project model for the actual downside.

Corporate leverage ratios such as Debt/EBITDA can be useful contextual metrics for some operating assets, but they are not substitutes for
finite-life project cash-flow coverage. Interest, taxes, lifecycle capex, working capital, reserve requirements and concession maturity can
make EBITDA a poor measure of how quickly project debt can actually be repaid.

#### Multi-tranche sculpting and competing debt constraints

Where more than one material debt issue is sculpted or cash-flow sized, do not allocate aggregate debt service pro rata if
facilities have different tenors, interest rates, fees, currencies, cash-capture rights or repayment priorities. Reconcile the
tranche-specific economics, including where relevant:

- debt-service capture / non-capture periods;
- separate maturity/tail constraints;
- tranche-specific discount/effective rates for LLCR or sizing;
- amortisation priority and intercreditor rules;
- average life / weighted principal-repayment timing;
- balloon or bullet amounts;
- withholding tax, political-risk insurance or guarantee premia;
- ongoing commitment/agency/guarantee fees; and
- reserve and hedge requirements by tranche.

A balloon can be economically equivalent to an additional debt issue with a different repayment date; test it as such rather
than hiding it in the final period.

Debt capacity can be constrained simultaneously by maximum gearing/minimum equity, minimum DSCR/LLCR, debt tenor/tail,
average-life limits, lender downside cases and market quantum. The binding constraint can change by scenario. Do not force
one constraint to be binding in every case.

Where the financing documents use a non-flat or "curved" coverage profile, reproduce the actual curve and its rationale.
Do not optimise an arbitrary DSCR curve merely to maximise equity IRR. Likewise, use P50/P90/P99 or other probabilistic
resource cases for debt sizing only where the financing package or lender methodology actually references them.

Taxes, NOL utilisation, interest deductions, financing fees, DSRA movements and refinancing can alter CFADS and therefore
cash-flow-sized debt. Where those items are in the lender CFADS perimeter, the sizing/sculpting case must include their actual
interaction; do not solve debt from a pre-tax or pre-reserve cash flow and then assume the same coverage survives after tax and
reserve consequences.

### Reserve accounts and trapped cash

Where required by the financing, model each reserve according to its actual purpose rather than as a generic cash
balance. Examples can include:

- debt-service reserve account (DSRA);
- major maintenance / lifecycle reserve;
- handback reserve;
- operating reserve;
- tax reserve; and
- restricted compensation / insurance proceeds.

For each material reserve, check required balance, funding source, permitted draw, replenishment priority, interest on
balances, release conditions and treatment at refinancing, termination and concession expiry.

Cash in a restricted reserve is not freely distributable equity cash. Conversely, a reserve should not be layered on
top of an identical cash requirement already embedded elsewhere unless the documents genuinely require both.

#### DSRA cash versus letter-of-credit support — conditional

A DSRA is a liquidity protection, not free project cash. Where the finance package permits a cash-funded reserve or a
letter-of-credit / reserve facility alternative, compare the actual economics and credit support:

- required amount and measurement basis;
- cash funding source and timing;
- LC/facility provider credit quality and any parent/sovereign support;
- commitment/LC fee and collateral requirements;
- draw conditions and expiry;
- reinstatement/replenishment priority after a draw; and
- release/refinancing/termination treatment.

A cash reserve can impose a material opportunity cost on equity; an LC can reduce trapped cash but introduces provider,
renewal and fee risk. Do not assume one is universally preferable.

Some financings permit DSRA cash to be applied against the final debt service or to influence sculpting. Apply that treatment
only if the finance documents allow it and ensure the same reserve is not simultaneously counted as freely distributable cash.

#### Major-maintenance reserve is not the maintenance expense

Where an MRA/lifecycle reserve exists, keep the **reserve funding/release mechanism** distinct from the underlying physical
maintenance or replacement expenditure. The reserve may be funded earlier than the work, earn interest, be restricted, and
have different tax/CFADS treatment. Do not model the reserve deposit as if the maintenance work has already occurred, or count
both the reserve deposit and the eventual maintenance cash spend as two separate economic costs unless that is genuinely the
finance-document cash-flow treatment.

### Distribution tests, lock-up and cash sweep

Equity distributions in project finance commonly occur only after operating costs, taxes, senior debt service and
required reserves are satisfied, and may be subject to backward-looking or forward-looking coverage tests, default
conditions, minimum balances or other lock-ups.

Where such restrictions exist, the model should prevent distributions when the contractual test fails and should show
what happens to trapped cash: retained, swept to debt, reserved, released later or otherwise applied. Do not equate
positive accounting profit with distributable cash.

### Cash sweep versus lock-up — use the mechanism for the risk it is designed to address

A distribution lock-up/cash trap is usually a **stress-period** protection: weak historic/prospective coverage stops cash
leaving the project. A cash sweep can instead capture excess cash in stronger periods and reduce debt before a later downside.
The two mechanisms should not be treated as interchangeable.

The value of a sweep depends on project risk. It can be useful where cash flow is volatile or exposed to a plausible downward
trend and strong early cash might otherwise be distributed before a later default. If cash is persistently weak there is little
to sweep; if cash is highly stable and comfortably above debt service, the incremental credit benefit may be small. Evaluate
the actual sweep with project-specific downside paths rather than crediting it automatically as a fixed amount of protection.

### Debt-service shortfall, reserve use and default arrears — conditional

When project cash is insufficient to meet scheduled senior debt service, do not make the shortfall disappear by setting debt
service equal to cash available. Apply the finance-document sequence, which may include:

`CFADS → permitted reserve/liquidity draw → unpaid debt service / default amount → default interest / cure period → sponsor cure / waiver → acceleration or enforcement`

Track whether unpaid principal/interest remains outstanding, capitalises, accrues default interest or triggers a mandatory
remedy. A later cash recovery should repay the actual arrears according to the waterfall; it should not be treated as a fresh
distribution merely because the original due date has passed.



### Financing-package selection and constrained gearing — not simply maximum debt

For a project whose financing is still being structured, the relevant question is not "what is the highest debt percentage the
spreadsheet can accept?" A feasible capital structure sits inside several simultaneous constraints:

`project cash-flow resilience + coverage tests + maturity / tail + completion exposure + security / reserves`
`+ lender / bond-market capacity + currency / hedge availability + sponsor support + contract term`
`→ feasible financing set`

Within that feasible set, different leverage levels and debt forms can change service-payment requirements, refinancing risk,
shareholder cash timing and downside robustness. A nominally higher equity IRR at greater leverage can simply reflect more
financial risk; it is not evidence of an economically superior project.

Where an "optimal" or maximum gearing calculation is presented, identify the **binding constraint** in each case. It may be
DSCR, LLCR, debt maturity, debt quantum, completion support, minimum equity, rating, market capacity, reserve funding or
another executed/market condition. Do not import the model-specific leverage percentages from historical research.

### Bank debt, fixed-rate project bonds and index-linked debt — cash-flow form matters

Debt instrument choice changes more than the headline coupon. Where alternative financing packages are genuinely in scope,
compare the whole project-finance cash-flow profile, including where relevant:

- construction-period draw versus bond proceeds timing;
- commitment / negative-carry / prefunding cost;
- interest or coupon during construction and whether it is paid, capitalised or reserve-funded;
- amortisation flexibility and sculpting constraints;
- maturity and debt tail;
- reserve and covenant regime;
- rating, trustee and capital-markets requirements;
- prepayment / make-whole / hedge or bond break costs;
- refinancing flexibility; and
- termination or change-compensation treatment.

A bond is not automatically cheaper because it has a lower quoted margin, and a bank facility is not automatically more
flexible if its executed covenants and breakage terms say otherwise.

#### Index-linked debt is not automatically hedged by indexed project revenue

An inflation-linked service payment or tariff can make index-linked debt economically attractive, but "both are indexed" is
not a hedge proof. Reconcile:

`revenue index + base date + reset lag + cap/floor + payment timing`
`versus`
`debt index + principal accretion + coupon basis + reset timing + amortisation / maturity`

Then test the interaction with O&M/lifecycle inflation and distributions. High inflation can increase nominal project revenue
while still weakening debt repayment if debt principal accretes faster, the indices differ, the revenue reset lags, a cap/floor
binds, or distributions are released before full debt retirement. Confirm that the final debt balance is actually repaid in the
model under the relevant inflation/downside cases.

Do not assume index-linked debt is superior to fixed debt universally. The value depends on the project's revenue index,
concession tenor, inflation basis, investor market, break-cost profile and contract allocation.

### Credit wraps, guarantees and bond insurance — conditional

A third-party guarantee or credit wrap can improve rating, tenor, lender/investor access or pricing, but it is not free credit
quality. Where a wrapped or guaranteed structure is modelled, bridge:

`unwrapped project credit / pricing / market access`
`→ guarantee scope + guarantor credit + call conditions + fee / collateral / covenants`
`→ wrapped rating / pricing / tenor / liquidity`
`→ all-in project and authority/sponsor economics`

Check whether the guarantee covers principal, interest, specified periods, specified political/contract risks or all non-payment.
A partial risk guarantee, partial credit guarantee, political-risk policy and full financial guarantee are not interchangeable.
Where the support requires a sovereign counter-guarantee, procurement condition, tied supplier, environmental/social
standard or other obligation, include the corresponding economic consequence where material.

A wrap that raises fees and complexity without a demonstrable improvement in debt capacity, tenor, pricing or risk allocation
should not be credited with automatic project value. Equally, do not assume a guarantor will pay immediately if claim,
arbitration, assignment or waiting conditions apply.

### Sharia-compliant / asset-based project finance — conditional

Some infrastructure projects use Islamic or other asset-/profit-based financing rather than conventional interest-bearing debt.
Only apply this module when the financing actually uses such instruments. Identify the executed legal/economic form — for
example partnership/profit-sharing, cost-plus asset purchase and resale, leasing/ijara, construction/istisna or sukuk-type
capital-market structures — and model the cash flows from those rights rather than relabelling a conventional interest line.

Reconcile where material:

- who legally owns or purchases the asset during each period;
- construction and completion risk before the asset is usable;
- rental, profit-share, purchase-price or redemption cash flows;
- ranking/security and intercreditor treatment alongside conventional debt;
- tax / duty / transfer-cost consequences where project-borne;
- early termination and asset-purchase consequences; and
- Sharia or other required approvals as a financing condition.

The historical thesis's descriptions of particular Islamic products are not a current documentation standard. The executed
facility and current legal/tax advice control.

### Project lease / asset-ownership financing — conditional

Where an infrastructure financing structure separates legal asset ownership from the operating concession through a lease or
asset-finance vehicle, distinguish **financing lease economics** from an operating/concession lease. Map:

`asset owner / lessor funding → asset purchase/construction → lease/rental cash → purchase option / residual value / transfer`

against the project agreement and handback. Do not book both a terminal sale/purchase value and a contractual handback of the
same asset unless the structure genuinely supports both. Confirm that lender/lessor rights, insurance, maintenance, tax and
termination compensation align with the legal owner and the party bearing the economic risk.

### Blended sponsor capital and the apparent equity-return effect

Sponsor funding can combine ordinary equity, shareholder/subordinated debt, preferred instruments or other junior capital.
Separate these cash flows when their ranking, interest/profit, repayment or tax treatment differs. The sponsor return should be
measured from the **actual instrument package**, not from ordinary dividends alone.

A restructuring of sponsor funding can make headline equity IRR rise because less ordinary equity is outstanding, because
junior interest is paid earlier, or because the required project payment/revenue has also changed. Before describing the new
structure as more efficient, bridge:

`project cash requirement → senior debt service → junior instrument service/redemption → ordinary distributions → sponsor total return`

and confirm that the improvement is not simply a transfer of value or risk from the authority, lenders, customers or another
capital class.

## Financing-market and hedging interface — conditional

### Financing-market depth and lender capacity

Bankability is not only a ratio calculation. A project can show acceptable modelled coverage while lacking enough credible lenders or
capital-market capacity to provide the required amount, tenor, currency or risk appetite at the relevant stage. Where financing remains
uncommitted or is being procured, reconcile the funding plan to current, project-relevant market evidence such as lender market
sounding, adviser debt-market feedback, indicative terms or binding commitments as maturity increases.

Consider whether the assumed financing requires:

- a depth of lender competition that does not exist for the jurisdiction, sector or ticket size;
- tenor beyond what credible lenders are offering;
- local- or hard-currency capacity not available in the required amount;
- construction or demand risk that prospective financiers have said they will not accept;
- refinancing or institutional take-out that depends on a market not yet demonstrably available; or
- multiple lenders whose aggregate capacity has been assumed without checking concentration/hold limits.

Market sounding is evidence of potential appetite, not a financing commitment. Conversely, a single lender's conservative view should
not automatically be treated as the whole market. As the project matures, replace generic market assumptions with the most mature
applicable financing evidence.

### Bid-to-financial-close interest-rate movement

PPP bids can be priced before debt is finally locked. Where the procurement framework provides a base-rate adjustment
at financial close, distinguish:

- reference/base interest rate movement;
- lender credit margin;
- hedging cost;
- commitment and arrangement fees; and
- any bidder-retained financing risk.

Do not assume that every financing-cost movement between bid and close is compensated by government. Equally, do
not charge the bidder with a risk the procurement explicitly neutralises through a financial-close adjustment.

### Interest-rate and inflation hedging

Where swaps or other hedges are part of the funding package, the model should reconcile:

- hedged notional to projected debt balance;
- hedge start/end dates to debt exposure;
- fixed versus floating debt portions;
- base rate and credit margin;
- inflation-linked revenue exposure versus nominal debt/cost exposure; and
- hedge breakage or termination economics where contractually relevant.

A hedge is not simply a fixed interest-rate assumption. If the project terminates or refinances early, hedge close-out
costs or gains may become economically material and may interact with the PPP termination/refinancing provisions.

#### Hedge profile, average debt life and extension/contraction risk

For amortising infrastructure debt, hedge economics depend on the **timing of debt outstanding**, not only the final maturity.
Average debt life can be a useful summary of principal-repayment timing, but it is not a substitute for the actual hedge schedule.
Reconcile, where material:

- expected draw profile during construction;
- amortisation and cash-sweep/prepayment profile;
- hedge notional by period;
- hedge maturity versus debt maturity;
- extension risk if debt remains outstanding longer than expected; and
- contraction/prepayment risk if debt is repaid earlier than the hedge.

A single swap tenor based on average life may be a rough pricing approximation, not necessarily the executed hedge. Early
refinancing, termination or cash-sweep prepayment can leave an over-hedged position and create break cost/gain; construction
delay can create an under-hedged or extended exposure.

#### Step-up margins and refinancing incentive — conditional

Where debt margins contractually step up over time, determine whether the structure is intended to encourage refinancing or
compensate lenders for extension risk. The executed step-up remains part of the no-refinancing cash flow. If the investment
case assumes refinancing before the step-up, show the refinancing case separately with availability, fees, hedge breakage,
reserve changes and any PPP gain-sharing rather than deleting the higher margin from the base financing terms.

### Financing certainty versus flexibility

Long-tenor fixed-rate or heavily hedged financing can reduce annual debt-service volatility and improve affordability, but it
can also make the project expensive to change, prepay, refinance, sell or terminate early. Model this trade-off where
material. Relevant economic consequences can include:

- debt prepayment premium or make-whole amount;
- hedge close-out/break cost or gain;
- loss of committed but undrawn facilities;
- refinancing restrictions/consent costs; and
- treatment of those costs under change and termination compensation.

Do not compare financing structures using headline coupon or DSCR alone when one structure embeds materially greater
future breakage or refinancing exposure.

### Refinancing and gain sharing

Refinancing can change debt margin, tenor, leverage, amortisation profile, reserve requirements and shareholder
cash extraction after project risk has reduced. Where the PPP contract contains refinancing-gain sharing:

1. establish the contractual definition of a refinancing;
2. compare the agreed pre-refinancing base case with the post-refinancing economics using the prescribed method;
3. include fees, hedge breakage, reserve effects, tax/interest-deduction consequences and incremental financing costs where the contract requires;
4. identify whether value is shared through a lump sum, reduced future service payment, additional service or another
   contractual mechanism; and
5. do not count the full gross refinancing benefit as shareholder value before the authority share and any financing
   costs are deducted.

Where refinancing is assumed as necessary because initial debt tenor is shorter than the concession, explicitly model
refinancing availability, timing, cost and downside. A mini-perm that silently rolls into identical long-term debt is
not a neutral assumption.



#### Refinancing evidence, model audit and distribution-NPV bridge — conditional

Some PPP contracts define refinancing gain by comparing the current projected sponsor distributions **with** and **without**
the proposed refinancing, using the operating model immediately before the refinancing rather than the stale financial-close
forecast. Where the actual contract uses that or an analogous method:

- preserve the same current operating assumptions in both cases except for the qualifying refinancing effects;
- include only the financing costs, reserve changes, taxes, hedge effects and other items allowed by the contract;
- prevent double counting of shareholder-loan cash flows, equity distributions and transaction costs;
- apply the contractual NPV/discount methodology and valuation date;
- reconcile any reduction in sponsor equity exposure or immediate cash extraction to the contractual sharing rule; and
- reproduce the authority's information, consent and financial-model audit rights where they affect whether the transaction can
  proceed.

A refinancing model supplied to the authority is evidence supporting a contract calculation, not merely a sponsor valuation.
Do not use the sample gain-sharing percentages in historical manuals; the executed project agreement controls the definition,
sharing rate, exempt/qualifying categories and settlement method.

### Distress restructuring is not ordinary refinancing

A distressed PPP restructuring can be a negotiated reallocation of value and risk rather than a simple cheaper-debt transaction. A
credible restructuring case may combine debt rescheduling/resculpting, covenant cure or waiver, sponsor support, operating/lifecycle
efficiencies, project/payment changes and revised shareholder economics. Model the full stakeholder bridge:

`pre-restructuring cash / covenants → debt and operating cure package → authority/project-payment effect → shareholder effect → post-restructuring liquidity and ratios`

Do not assume that resculpting debt alone creates value. A longer amortisation profile can shift risk to lenders or the authority, and a
higher service payment can transfer distress cost to government. Conversely, a lower payment or sponsor injection can transfer value
away from equity. The executed or negotiated terms control; historical target cover ratios are not benchmarks.

### Secondary-market transfer of PPP equity / shareholder instruments

A sale or acquisition of an operating PPP stake should be valued from the **remaining sponsor economic interest**, not merely from the
headline ordinary-equity dividend stream. Depending on the structure, that interest can include shareholder/subordinated loans, interest
and fees, future equity distributions, redemption proceeds and any retained support obligations or contingent exposures.

For a seller, compare the net sale consideration with the present value of the remaining sponsor cash-flow stream under a discount/return
basis consistent with those cash flows. For a buyer, compare purchase consideration and acquisition financing with the future sponsor
cash flows and required return. In both cases, incorporate material transfer consents/lock-ups, sponsor-retention requirements, change of
control, refinancing/hedge effects, tax/transaction costs where in scope, remaining lifecycle/handback exposure and current contract/
operating risks.

Do not import the historical example's required return or sale premium. The durable principle is the incremental **sell versus hold** or
**buy versus required-return** decision using the current post-close forecast and the actual sponsor instrument package.

### Sponsor promote / IRR-flip / hurdle-based equity allocation — conditional

Some infrastructure equity structures allocate project distributions differently once a sponsor, institutional investor or other equity
class reaches specified return hurdles. Where such a promote/flip exists, model the actual contractual waterfall rather than applying one
constant ownership percentage to all distributions.

For each hurdle/tranche identify:

- investor classes and funded capital basis;
- dated contributions and distributions used to measure the hurdle;
- hurdle metric — IRR, preferred return, MOIC or another defined test;
- whether the hurdle is cumulative and whether deficits carry forward;
- incremental allocation percentages after each hurdle;
- treatment of shareholder-loan interest/redemption, refinancing proceeds and sale proceeds; and
- catch-up, clawback or reset mechanics if provided.

A high developer/promote IRR can result from a small capital contribution and asymmetric upside allocation; it is not evidence that the
project itself earns the same return. Test downside and upside cash-flow allocation to each class, especially where the structure is intended
to separate risk between developer and long-term capital.

## Stage-dependent infrastructure risk, project returns and exit value — conditional

Infrastructure risk can change materially across development, construction, commissioning, ramp-up and mature operations.
Do not assume a constant required return or automatic monotonic de-risking. The relevant question at any valuation/sale date is
which risks remain and which have actually been resolved.

Typical risk transitions can include:

- development: permits, land, technical solution, tariff/offtake and financing uncertainty;
- construction: cost, schedule, interface, contractor and completion risk;
- commissioning/ramp-up: acceptance, performance and demand/resource realisation;
- mature operations: operating/lifecycle, counterparty, regulatory, merchant and handback risk; and
- late concession: residual-life, major renewal, refinancing and handback/expiry concentration.

Operating history can reduce uncertainty around some technical, demand or resource assumptions, but it does not eliminate
contract, regulatory, counterparty or lifecycle risk. Use current asset-specific evidence rather than historical claims that all
mature projects deserve one low discount rate.

### Project return, sponsor return and leverage diagnostics

Keep unlevered/project economics distinct from sponsor/equity economics. Financing can redistribute timing and risk between
debt and equity but does not change the underlying physical revenue/cost engine.

As a diagnostic, ordinary positive leverage will often increase sponsor IRR when the all-in debt cost is below the unlevered
project return, but taxes, reserves, fees, sculpting, delayed distributions, subordinated instruments and refinancing can reverse
that intuition. If project and equity returns move in an unexpected direction, investigate the actual financing/timing mechanics
rather than imposing a generic relationship.

For a secondary-market sale, holding-period IRR or value should be based on the remaining sponsor cash flows and the buyer's
required return for the **remaining risk profile**. Do not infer value from a generic EV/EBITDA, yieldco or toll-road multiple
where concession term, lifecycle capex, leverage, shareholder loans or merchant exposure make the cash-flow perimeter different.

## Contract-change economics and funding — conditional PPP module

A PPP model should treat a material variation or compensation event as both a **contract event** and, where relevant,
a **funding event**.

For each material change, separate:

`scope change → capex/opex/lifecycle/schedule effect → incremental funding need → financing cost → payment/tariff/term compensation → residual project/equity effect`

Potential compensation forms include:

- upfront authority payment;
- increase/decrease in unitary payment;
- tariff adjustment;
- extension or reduction of concession term;
- milestone grant;
- compensation over a defined period; or
- another formula set out by the contract.

Do not assume the SPV can fund a major compensation event from existing debt merely because the authority eventually
compensates the cost. Additional debt may be unavailable, restricted by existing lenders or expensive; the contract
