may prescribe how incremental funding is demonstrated or compensated.

### Variation pricing and lender consent

After procurement, a material variation is not exposed to the same competitive tension as the original bid. Where the contract provides
a change-pricing method, reproduce that method rather than inserting an unconstrained management cost estimate. Depending on the
agreement, this can include pre-agreed schedules/rate cards for small works, open-book cost, benchmarked rates, agreed mark-ups,
competitive quotes, independent/expert determination or another prescribed approach.

For a material variation, reconcile:

- defined scope and implementation timetable;
- pricing basis, mark-up/profit and indexation;
- lifecycle and O&M consequences rather than capex alone;
- financing source, incremental financing cost and payment adjustment;
- lender/security-agent consent rights and any drawstop/covenant effect;
- authority approvals, affordability and budget consequences; and
- grant/provider or regulatory approvals where applicable.

Do not assume lender consent is a formality where the variation alters risk allocation, debt capacity, security, payment profile or
termination value. Conversely, do not impose a consent requirement that is absent from the executed finance documents.

### Preserve the contractual financial position where required

Some PPP change mechanisms aim to leave one or both parties in a defined economic position after a qualifying event.
The required metric is contract-specific and may involve project cash flow, senior debt position, shareholder return,
NPV of a payment stream or another agreed test.

Do not automatically use equity IRR as the compensation target. Do not automatically preserve every original return
metric. Apply the mechanism actually specified by the contract.

### D&C and O&M pass-through is not automatically perfect

The SPV often subcontracts construction and operations, but the PPP contract and subcontracts rarely transfer risk on
identical terms. Compare material interfaces such as:

- completion date and testing standard;
- delay liquidated damages versus lost availability/payment;
- liability caps;
- force-majeure definitions;
- change-in-law treatment;
- indexation;
- performance deductions versus O&M abatements;
- lifecycle obligations;
- insurance deductibles; and
- handback obligations.

A model that assumes every PPP loss is perfectly recovered from the D&C or O&M contractor may substantially
understate retained SPV risk where timing, caps, exclusions or scope differ.



### Related-party EPC, O&M, management or supplier arrangements — economic perimeter

A project sponsor may also be the EPC contractor, O&M provider, hospital manager, equipment supplier or other project
counterparty. This can be commercially legitimate, but the model must distinguish **project-company economics** from the
sponsor group's separate contractor/manager return.

Where material, reconcile:

- the SPV's contractual payment to the affiliate and the affiliate's actual scope/risk;
- any shareholder return, development fee, EPC margin, O&M margin or management fee earned outside the SPV;
- related-party liability caps, guarantees and termination rights;
- whether procurement, lender or authority documents require benchmarking, independent approval or arm's-length pricing;
- whether a saving at the SPV is simply a transfer to an affiliate; and
- whether affiliate distress creates correlated sponsor/contractor risk.

Do not count an affiliate contractor margin as both a project cost saving and an additional project return. Conversely, do not
eliminate a real contractual cost merely because the ultimate recipient is a shareholder.

### Conditions precedent, drawstops and funding availability

Signing finance documents is not the same as having usable cash. For each material facility, identify the conditions
that control first draw and later drawdowns where they can affect the programme. These can include:

- project-agreement effectiveness;
- equity contribution or minimum equity funded;
- land/site rights;
- permits and licences;
- executed D&C/O&M/offtake or other key contracts;
- required insurance;
- security perfection;
- hedging;
- legal/technical certificates;
- no-default / representations repeated at draw; and
- lender-approved cost-to-complete or draw certificates.

The model should not use a facility before the relevant funding gate. Likewise, a later drawstop or event of default
may block further debt even when undrawn commitment remains numerically available.

### Multi-tranche financing and intercreditor economics

Where the capital structure contains multiple senior, mezzanine, subordinated, DFI/ECA, bond, liquidity, VAT or
working-capital facilities, do not collapse them into one synthetic debt line if their economics differ materially.
Reconcile, by tranche where relevant:

- currency and index/base rate;
- commitment and availability period;
- draw order;
- maturity and amortisation;
- cash sweep or prepayment priority;
- reserve rights;
- hedging requirement;
- security ranking / payment priority;
- cure rights and voting/control where they affect cash distributions or enforcement; and
- refinancing/termination treatment.

The infrastructure risk is not generic debt arithmetic; it is that one tranche can become unavailable, mature, or
block distributions even when the aggregated debt balance appears supportable.

### Covenant breach, cure, waiver and sponsor support

Do not jump directly from a forecast covenant breach to insolvency or termination. Where finance documents specify
a cure regime, model or scenario the actual path: warning/lock-up, cash trap, reserve cure, sponsor equity cure,
waiver, mandatory prepayment, acceleration or enforcement.

Sponsor support should be included only to the extent legally committed or explicitly assumed for the purpose of the
model. A model that automatically injects unlimited equity to cure every downside is not a limited-recourse project
case.

### Direct agreements, lender cure, step-in and substitution

For material project-financed PPPs, direct agreements can materially change the economics between project default
and termination. Before assuming an immediate termination payment after SPV default, determine whether lenders
have contractual:

- notice of default/termination;
- cure periods;
- step-in rights;
- rights to appoint a substitute entity;
- novation rights; and
- corresponding rights under key D&C, O&M, offtake or other project contracts.

Where these rights are exercisable and economically relevant, distress scenarios should respect the cure/step-in
sequence rather than jumping from first breach to terminal cash flow.

#### Replacement contractor / substitute-provider practicality

A legal substitution right is not the same as a practically executable rescue. For a material contractor/operator distress
case, consider whether the market actually contains a qualified replacement and whether the model captures, where relevant:

- procurement/search and lender/authority approval time;
- mobilisation and transition delay;
- replacement-price premium or changed commercial terms;
- interface/recommissioning cost;
- bond, guarantee, LD or insurance recoveries; and
- service-payment/revenue consequence during the transition.

For very large, specialist or capacity-constrained projects, replacement may be slower or more expensive than the legal
step-in timetable suggests. Do not make substitution economically instantaneous merely because the direct agreement permits it.

### Security and credit-support recoverability

Where completion or operating downside relies on credit support, identify what the project can actually recover,
when, and from whom. Relevant support can include:

- parent-company guarantees;
- performance bonds;
- letters of credit;
- liquidated damages;
- standby equity/debt;
- reserve accounts;
- insurance proceeds;
- revenue assignments; and
- government/offtaker guarantees.

Model the applicable cap, expiry, deductible/threshold, calling condition, collection timing and counterparty credit.
Do not treat contractual rights as immediate cash if recovery can be delayed, capped, disputed or dependent on an
insolvent counterparty.

#### Security value is not book-value collateral recovery

Limited-recourse infrastructure lenders principally underwrite the project's operating cash flows and the contractual rights
that preserve or redirect those cash flows. Physical security over a specialised road, rail asset, plant, network or concession
SPV may provide control and enforcement leverage without making the asset readily saleable for its accounting carrying value or
replacement cost.

Where downside recovery is material, distinguish:

- project cash flow and assigned revenue/accounts;
- security over shares, assets and material project contracts;
- lender cure, step-in, substitution and continuation rights;
- termination compensation and insurance proceeds;
- residual or alternative-use value of the physical asset; and
- any legally enforceable collateral recovery after costs and priority claims.

Do not use PPE book value or replacement cost as a proxy for lender recovery unless the asset can actually be realised on that
basis. For many PPPs the economically valuable collateral is the continuing concession/payment stream and associated contractual
control rights, not the scrap or accounting value of the infrastructure itself.

### Insurance economics — conditional

Infrastructure insurance can affect both operating cash flow and recovery after a major event. Where material,
reconcile required/project insurance to:

- construction versus operating cover;
- material damage/property damage;
- delay in start-up or business interruption;
- liability cover;
- policy limits, deductibles and waiting periods;
- reinstatement versus debt-prepayment use of proceeds;
- co-insured/lender loss-payee/security arrangements;
- exclusions and uninsured risk retained by the project; and
- any contractual premium-sharing/review mechanism.

Do not assume that insured physical damage makes the project economically whole. Deductibles, waiting periods,
revenue interruption, reinstatement timing and contract deductions may remain with the SPV.

#### Reinstatement economics and lender economic tests

For a catastrophic event, insurance proceeds may not always flow automatically to reinstatement. Some project agreements and
finance documents permit an **economic reinstatement test** before large proceeds are committed to rebuilding, particularly
for a single-site asset where total destruction is plausible. Where such a test exists, reproduce its actual metric, timing,
thresholds and use-of-proceeds consequences. A loan-life or whole-project solvency test can produce a different answer from an
annual DSCR snapshot.

Do not invent an economic test merely because lenders have security over insurance proceeds. The executed project agreement,
insurance provisions, intercreditor/security documents and lender arrangements determine whether proceeds reinstate, prepay
debt or follow another route.

#### Uninsurable-risk regime

A contractual declaration that a required risk has become uninsurable can alter both cost and risk allocation. Where the PPP
contains an uninsurability regime, model the actual sequence, which may include:

- suspension of the obligation to buy unavailable cover;
- reduction of the service payment for the premium no longer incurred;
- periodic re-testing of the insurance market;
- re-procurement of cover if it becomes available again; and
- government/authority funding or termination consequences if the formerly insured event occurs.

Do not assume an uninsurable risk is either fully project-retained or fully government-backed without tracing the contract.

### Counterparty credit and concentration

Contracted infrastructure revenue is not risk-free merely because volume is fixed. Identify material reliance on:

- a single authority/offtaker;
- anchor customer or shipper;
- airline/shipping line;
- EPC/D&C contractor;
- O&M provider;
- fuel/commodity supplier;
- insurer/guarantor; or
- grid/network operator.

Where counterparty default can interrupt payment, construction, input supply, performance support or insurance
recovery, the model should not treat the corresponding contractual cash flow as sovereign-like unless the credit
support actually justifies that treatment.

### FX and index-basis risk

Infrastructure often combines local-currency revenue with foreign-currency capex, debt, fuel/equipment cost or
returns. Separate:

- transaction FX exposure;
- debt principal retranslation / debt-service FX;
- contractual FX indexation or pass-through;
- hedge protection; and
- residual basis risk from mismatched indices, weights, reset dates or caps/floors.

A tariff indexed to a broad CPI does not necessarily hedge foreign debt or imported equipment. Likewise, an FX
pass-through may operate with a lag or only on a defined tariff component.

#### FX convertibility, transferability and repatriation

For cross-border project finance, distinguish **exchange-rate risk** from the ability to convert local currency into the required
hard currency and transfer or repatriate that currency outside the host jurisdiction. A project can have sufficient local-currency
cash and a stable modelled exchange rate yet still be unable to service foreign debt, pay hedge amounts or remit distributions if
exchange controls or transfer restrictions intervene.

Where this risk is material, reconcile any political-risk insurance, government convertibility undertaking, offshore reserve or
convertibility facility to:

- covered currency and eligible payments;
- trigger and waiting/claim process;
- cap and tenor/expiry;
- conversion rate or valuation basis where relevant;
- settlement delay and temporary liquidity need; and
- exclusions or amounts that remain exposed.

Do not combine currency depreciation and transfer/inconvertibility into one sensitivity if different parties or instruments bear
them. A hedge against FX movement does not itself guarantee legal access to or transfer of hard currency.

## Authority-side affordability and fiscal cash flows — conditional

Where the model is prepared for a government / procuring authority rather than solely for the SPV, separately model
the public cash-flow obligations created by the PPP. Depending on the structure these may include:

- construction-period capital contribution;
- recurring availability / service payments;
- demand or revenue guarantees;
- operating subsidies;
- retained land / enabling works;
- utility or pass-through costs;
- compensation events and authority changes;
- termination payments;
- guarantees and other contingent support; and
- handback / transition costs retained by government.

Do not treat a PPP as fiscally affordable merely because the SPV is bankable. A commercially financeable project can
still create an unsustainable or highly contingent public payment profile.

Where a Public Sector Comparator or value-for-money analysis is required by the jurisdiction, apply the governing
methodology and risk allocation rather than a generic global formula. Keep procurement-choice analysis distinct from
SPV investor valuation.

### Multi-year authority payment funding and appropriation

A long-term PPP obligation should be connected to the authority's credible funding and budget architecture rather than appearing as an
abstract affordability ratio. Where relevant, reconcile recurring service payments, capital contributions and expected contingent calls
to the identified budget source, appropriation/authorisation mechanism, medium-term expenditure framework or other long-term fiscal
process that supports payment over the concession life.

Distinguish the **contractual obligation to pay** from the administrative mechanism by which budget is appropriated and cash is made
available. If annual appropriation, ring-fenced funds, sovereign support or another mechanism changes payment/credit risk, reflect the
actual structure. Do not assume a 20–30 year PPP commitment is fully funded merely because the next annual budget contains the first
payment.

### Preferred-bid to financial-close affordability recheck

Affordability should be rechecked when material inputs can legitimately change between preferred bidder selection and financial close.
Where procurement rules permit financial-close adjustments, update the relevant model for the specified reference-rate movement,
inflation, timing, scope or other allowed changes and confirm the final contractual payment remains within the authority's approved
funding envelope. Do not preserve the selection-date affordability conclusion after the payment has materially changed.

Equally, the model should not grant an adjustment that the procurement did not permit. Separate authority-retained close adjustment risk
from bidder-retained lender margin, fee, hedge or execution risk according to the actual rules.

### Direct versus contingent public liabilities

For an authority-side model, classify fiscal exposure rather than presenting one undifferentiated government cost.
Distinguish:

- **direct committed payments** — capital contributions, availability/service payments, subsidies and retained
  public operating costs; from
- **contingent payments** — revenue/traffic/FX guarantees, compensation events, debt or payment guarantees,
  termination compensation and other support triggered only in specified circumstances.

For contingent exposure, model the trigger, timing, payment basis, cap, duration and interaction with project cash
flows. A guarantee's face amount alone is not an adequate representation of fiscal risk.

#### Quantify material government-guarantee risk

For a material revenue, debt-service, FX, minimum-payment or other government guarantee, the authority-side model should
quantify the exposure at a level proportionate to the decision. Depending on materiality and available data, this can include:

- maximum contractual loss/exposure;
- expected or probability-weighted loss;
- loss under a severe but coherent downside;
- timing/profile of calls rather than only lifetime total; and
- where proportionate, a market-value or stochastic/option-style estimate of the guarantee.

Do not mandate Monte Carlo or option pricing for every PPP. The method should match data quality, materiality and the
risk driver. However, a large contingent guarantee should not be represented solely by a binary note or its face value when
its probability and downside concentration are central to public affordability.

Where government support covers lender losses, distinguish guaranteed from genuinely at-risk debt. Excessive protection can
change lender/sponsor incentives and should not be treated as equivalent to strong project cash-flow bankability.

#### Forecast cash cost and risk-adjusted support value are different — conditional

For an authority model that **claims to value risk transfer, guarantees or contingent support economically**, distinguish at least:

- maximum contractual exposure;
- expected / probability-weighted cash loss by period;
- severe but plausible fiscal exposure and liquidity timing; and
- risk-adjusted, fair-value or market-consistent value where the governing appraisal method requires such a measure.

A guarantee with little expected near-term cash outflow can still transfer economically valuable downside risk to government.
Conversely, do not impose a fair-value methodology where the jurisdiction's fiscal/accounting/PSC framework requires another
basis. The model should be explicit about which concept of cost it is reporting and should not describe an undiscounted face
value, expected loss and risk-adjusted value as interchangeable.

### PPP financing does not remove the authority's funding obligation

For a government-pays PPP, private financing changes the timing and risk allocation of public cash flows; it does not eliminate
the need for government/user funding over the contract life. Reconcile the service/payment obligation, capital contributions and
contingent support to the authority's long-term budget/affordability case rather than focusing only on the absence of upfront public
capex.

Where a user-pay concession appears fiscally self-supporting, still identify guarantees, minimum-revenue support, termination
undertakings, FX support, land contributions or implicit service-continuity exposure that can return risk to government.

### Authority portfolio / programme fiscal exposure — conditional

If the model is used for a PPP programme, ministry, municipality or other portfolio rather than one project, aggregate project-level
cash flows without losing contingent-risk correlation. At minimum distinguish:

- scheduled capital contributions and availability/service payments;
- expected contingent-support payments;
- contractual maximum exposures where relevant;
- termination or rescue concentration;
- common macro drivers such as inflation, FX, interest rates or demand shocks; and
- annual/medium-term budget and fiscal limits under the governing framework.

A portfolio of individually affordable PPPs can become unaffordable if multiple guarantees or indexed payments crystallise together.
Do not add maximum exposures as if all are certain, but do not assess each project in isolation when the authority's solvency/budget is
shared.

### Municipal / subnational PPP payer credit — conditional

Where the contracting authority is a municipality, state/province, utility or other subnational entity, do not automatically substitute
sovereign credit. Reconcile the payment obligation to the actual revenue base, intergovernmental transfers, borrowing/fiscal limits,
credit enhancement and any legally enforceable higher-government support. A tariff or availability payment can be contractually valid
but still exposed to subnational liquidity or appropriation risk.

### Unsolicited or directly negotiated proposal — market-test discipline

Where an infrastructure proposal is unsolicited or materially negotiated without competitive price tension, do not treat the proponent's
capex, tariff, availability payment, return or risk price as automatically market-tested. If the authority case claims value for money or
commercial reasonableness, use the jurisdiction's permitted benchmark, independent cost/finance evidence, competitive challenge or other
credible market test.

This is not a presumption that unsolicited proposals are uneconomic. The model issue is that absence of competition reduces the evidentiary
weight of the bid price as proof of efficient cost or risk transfer. Preserve any statutory compensation for intellectual property or project
development rights separately from the underlying infrastructure economics.


### Public-infrastructure financial viability and service sustainability — conditional sovereign / public-operator module

Where infrastructure is owned, operated or supported by a ministry, general-government unit, state-owned utility, municipal entity or
other public operator, distinguish three questions that are often incorrectly collapsed into one:

1. **economic viability** — does the investment create sufficient net value for the relevant society/economy under the governing economic
   appraisal method?;
2. **intrinsic project financial viability** — can the project/component generate enough predictable project cash to recover the financial
   costs it is expected to recover without discretionary external support?; and
3. **entity/service financial sustainability** — can the responsible operator and/or government actually fund O&M, lifecycle/replacement,
   capital service and other obligations needed to deliver the required service over the asset's economic life?

A project can be economically valuable but not intrinsically financially viable. It can still be sustainable if a credible long-term subsidy or
budget mechanism exists. Conversely, an individually viable project can sit inside an operator whose wider network is financially weak and
therefore fail to receive the O&M/lifecycle resources required in practice.

#### Identify the actual O&M entity and network perimeter

For public infrastructure, first identify who will **operate and maintain** the asset after completion. The construction/executing agency and
the ultimate O&M agency may differ. If responsibilities are split among several agencies, assess each material funding obligation and the
interface between them.

The relevant sustainability perimeter is normally the **network or service system managed by the responsible O&M entity**, not only the new
project increment. A new road, water plant, school, hospital or network extension competes for recurrent resources with the existing asset
base managed by the same institution.

#### Unconstrained O&M requirement versus budget allocation versus execution

For a budget-funded public operator, distinguish:

`technical / normative network O&M requirement → budget request → approved allocation → cash release → actual execution → maintenance performed → asset condition / service`

The first item is the **unconstrained O&M requirement**: the amount needed to operate and maintain the full network at the service/asset-
condition standard implied by the technical design, asset-management plan and economic life, before fiscal rationing. Evidence can include:

- asset inventory, age and physical condition;
- historic O&M and backlog;
- recommended routine and periodic maintenance;
- planned replacements/renewals;
- staffing, utilities, consumables and establishment costs;
- asset-management-system outputs; and
- engineering norms or maintenance plans appropriate to the asset.

Do not treat the approved budget as proof of technical adequacy. Compare the unconstrained requirement with both **allocation and actual
execution/utilisation**. An authority can under-budget O&M and can also fail to spend the budget that was allocated.

A particularly misleading test is:

`incremental project O&M / total agency budget = small percentage → therefore sustainable`

This can fail when the existing network is already materially underfunded. It is not credible to assume the new project will be perfectly
maintained while the rest of the same network remains chronically underfunded unless there is a ring-fenced or otherwise protected funding
mechanism.

#### Maintenance backlog changes asset life and future capital need

Persistent O&M underfunding is not merely a current-period expense variance. It can cause:

`underfunded maintenance → deferred work/backlog → accelerated deterioration → lower availability/service → larger rehabilitation/renewal → shortened economic life`

Where the base case assumes full design life and stable service despite a material historic or forecast O&M funding gap, require a technical
bridge explaining why. Do not preserve the original asset life as a fixed model fact if the funding path cannot sustain the maintenance regime
on which that life depends.

#### Budget growth should cover the existing network before the project increment is claimed funded

For a growing/aging network, future recurrent funding has at least two distinct demands:

- escalation, ageing and renewal of the **existing** asset base; and
- incremental O&M/lifecycle of the new project.

A nominal budget increase can still represent declining real maintenance capacity if it does not cover inflation, growth in the asset base or
backlog. Test the composition of the increase rather than comparing only headline budget totals.

#### Public utility / corporatised operator sustainability

For a corporatised utility or other market-facing public operator, project-level cash flow is not enough. Review the operating entity's historic
and forecast capacity to fund the **whole network**, including the project. Infrastructure-specific drivers can include:

- tariff regime and actual tariff-reset history;
- billing, collection and receivable ageing;
- technical and commercial losses;
- non-revenue water;
- metering and prepaid-meter penetration;
- demand and price elasticity;
- subsidy entitlement and actual receipt;
- cross-subsidy between profitable and loss-making service segments;
- existing debt/capital obligations;
- planned network capex and replacement;
- climate/resource events affecting service; and
- regulatory or political constraints on price, service or investment.

Historical operating and financial analysis should span a period that is representative of the infrastructure's relevant cycle rather than a
mechanical fixed number of years. Forecasts should extend far enough to capture project ramp-up/full capacity and the point at which the new
asset's recurrent burden becomes established.

A project can pass its own FIRR/NPV or project-finance credit tests while the operator remains unsustainable because of liquidity pressure,
weak capitalisation, loss-making service obligations, large existing liabilities or delayed/non-receipt of government compensation. Surface
that operator-level weakness rather than presenting project viability as proof of service sustainability.

#### Predictable support versus discretionary support

For a project/component described as **intrinsically financially viable**, cost recovery should come from sufficiently predictable project-
related sources. Depending on the governing framework these can include:

- user tariffs/tolls/fees;
- contractually committed availability or offtake payments;
- cost savings/efficiency benefits that actually release cash;
- earmarked or legislated service payments;
- committed viability-gap support under the project contract; or
- other enforceable/predictable support.

Annual appropriations, discretionary grants or politically revocable subsidies are different. A project dependent on them may still be
financially sustainable if the government has credible fiscal capacity and policy commitment, but it should not be labelled self-financing or
intrinsically viable merely because the base case assumes the subsidy always arrives.

Where support depends on the government's fiscal position, analyze the **appropriate level of government** and the actual transfer history,
policy commitment and size of the required support relative to fiscal capacity. Do not substitute sovereign capacity automatically for a
municipal, utility or subnational payer unless there is enforceable higher-government support.

#### Institutional financial-analysis basis — keep real project evaluation separate from nominal operator forecasting

Some development-finance methodologies deliberately use different analytical bases for different questions. Under ADB's sovereign-project
method, for example:

- the **operator/entity financial analysis** is prepared in nominal terms in the entity's presentation/functional operating currency; while
- the **project financial cost-benefit evaluation** is prepared in real terms, after tax, at project/component level.

Treat this as an institutional methodology, not a universal prescription. Where the governing methodology uses this split, do not mix a real
FIRR cash-flow stream with a nominal WACC or use loan currency as though all local revenues and costs moved with the exchange rate.

For foreign-currency project items, state the conversion basis into the relevant presentation currency and preserve the underlying FX exposure.
A one-currency model is a material simplification where debt, capex or operating inputs are in another currency.

#### Incremental project financial evaluation — conditional FIRR/FNPV method

Where the authority/development-finance methodology requires a project financial cost-benefit evaluation, construct the financial cash flow
on an **incremental project basis**:

- expansion/extension: `with-project cash flow − without-project cash flow`;
- new standalone asset: the full project cash flow is incremental; and
- replacement: include the avoided/retained old-system cash flows plus disposal, transition/retraining and replacement consequences as
  applicable.

Project inflows can include user revenue and real cash savings from improved efficiency. Project outflows should include the infrastructure
cash costs required over the evaluation life, including construction, O&M, applicable taxes, working capital where relevant, scheduled
replacement/periodic maintenance and final restoration/closure where applicable.

Under the ADB real project-evaluation convention, base cost including taxes and physical contingencies enters the financial evaluation, while
price contingency and financing charges are excluded from the FIRR/FNPV project cash flow. That is **method-specific**. Do not impose it on
an SPV valuation, lender CFADS model or jurisdiction that defines the financial evaluation differently.

Likewise, residual value should follow the governing methodology and the asset/contract rights. Do not import a generic residual value into a
PPP that hands the asset back for no compensation.

#### Tariff, user-fee and toll achievability

A financially self-supporting public infrastructure case should not assume tariff/toll increases solely because the spreadsheet needs them.
Test material price/revenue assumptions against:

- governing law, policy, concession or regulation;
- who has authority to set/revise the charge;
- the intended objective — partial O&M recovery, full financial cost recovery or commercial return;
- actual service cost/revenue requirement;
- historical revision frequency and magnitude;
- pending policy/regulatory changes;
- affordability and elasticity; and
- the difference between approved charge, billed amount and cash collection.

A long history of below-inflation or irregular tariff revisions is evidence against an automatic full-indexation base case unless there is a
credible structural change. Conversely, a new independent regulator or statutory formula may justify a different forecast from history if the
model documents that change.

#### Project-financing-plan WACC is not sponsor hurdle rate or value for money

Where a project financial evaluation uses WACC as its discount rate, calculate the WACC from the **financing sources actually assigned to the
project evaluation**, not the existing operator's corporate capital structure unless the governing methodology says otherwise.

The economic cost of each financing source can include interest plus relevant fees/charges. Government equity and grants are not necessarily
zero-cost resources: under opportunity-cost methodologies they consume scarce public capital even if no contractual return is paid. For a PPP,
private equity cost should reflect the applicable market opportunity set/risk under the governing method rather than being replaced by a
public borrowing rate without justification.

Do not equate:

- project WACC;
- private sponsor minimum equity return;
- public-sector social discount rate;
- PSC/VfM discount rate; or
- the project's observed FIRR.

They answer different questions and can legitimately differ.

#### Switching values and financial resilience

For a project evaluated using FNPV/FIRR or another explicit viability threshold, supplement arbitrary ±x% sensitivities with **switching
values** where useful. A switching value asks how far a driver can deteriorate before the financial conclusion changes, for example:

- tariff/revenue decline before FNPV reaches zero;
- capex increase before FIRR falls to the required financial threshold;
- demand/offtake decline before viability is lost; or
- O&M increase before the project no longer recovers the required financial cost.

This is conceptually similar to project-finance break-even DSCR/LLCR analysis but the threshold and cash-flow perimeter are different. Do not
apply a FIRR/WACC switching value to lender CFADS or present it as a probability of failure.

Where risk factors are correlated, construct coherent combined scenarios rather than adding independent single-variable sensitivities. Examples
include construction delay with higher cost and delayed service benefits, or tariff increase with reduced demand/beneficiary take-up. Avoid
aggregating so many unrelated shocks that the scenario loses diagnostic value.

#### Financial covenants must be controllable early-warning mechanisms

For public operators/development-finance projects, financial covenants should be selected as **context-specific early-warning/mitigation tools**,
not as a generic checklist. A useful covenant is:

- legally/contractually enforceable in the relevant framework;
- objectively measurable;
- monitorable on the required frequency;
- achievable based on the forecast and operating context; and
- materially within the borrower/operator's control.

Do not covenant an outcome the entity cannot control, such as actual demand or a legislature's future appropriation. Where an independent
regulator sets tariffs, a covenant may require the operator to make a timely tariff application or take specified controllable steps rather than
guarantee the regulator's decision.

Depending on the service and entity, useful infrastructure-linked covenant/early-warning measures can include collection efficiency/receivable
days, cash O&M cost recovery, debt-service coverage, debt limits, debt/equity or internally funded capex. The relevant measure and threshold
must be supported by sector/entity context; do not import ADB example ratios as universal levels.

Distinguish:

- one-time conditions/actions;
- continuing performance covenants; and
- prior-consent limitations on actions such as additional debt.

A forecast breach should trigger analysis and mitigation before legal default where the framework permits. Waiver, deferral or restructuring
should be based on changed circumstances and a revised sustainability case rather than used to make the model pass automatically.

#### Re-evaluate financial sustainability when the project or entity changes

Material changes to design, scope, financing, operating entity, tariff framework, support mechanism or the entity's going-concern/financial
position can invalidate the original financial conclusion. Where the applicable lender/authority process requires it, update the project
financial evaluation and/or operator sustainability analysis and identify corrective actions or additional covenants.

At project completion, the sustainability review should ask whether:

- design/scope/implementation arrangements remained appropriate;
- changes during implementation altered financial viability;
- mitigation actions were actually effective;
- the final O&M entity has resources to operate and maintain the asset;
- residual risks need continuing monitoring; and
- the selected O&M institutional arrangement still matches the project delivered.

Do not carry an appraisal-stage 'financially sustainable' label into operations when material evidence has changed.

### Sector-specific public financial sustainability extensions

#### Electricity utilities

Where a public electricity entity operates generation/transmission/distribution assets, assess the actual sector structure and avoid analysing a
project as though all value/risk sits in one vertically integrated entity when functions are separated. Where material, connect:

- full/partial-cost-recovery or competitive/government-set tariff;
- regulator/ministerial reset authority and timing;
- feed-in tariff or renewable-support obligations;
- government's fiscal capacity to compensate the utility for mandated support;
- technical and commercial losses;
- metering/prepayment;
- billing/collection/receivables; and
- explicit operating subsidies.

A renewable support tariff can make the generator bankable while weakening the purchasing utility if the promised government compensation
is not funded or paid.

#### Water and wastewater public operators

For public water/wastewater entities, bridge partial/full tariff recovery to actual cash O&M. Where tariff does not cover the required O&M,
identify the subsidy arrangement **and actual historic/forecast flow**, not merely the policy entitlement. Link financial sustainability to NRW,
metering, billing/collection and affordability because these operational drivers determine whether the stated tariff translates into cash.

#### Public/toll roads

For a public road entity, determine whether tolls are retained by the road operator or remitted to the treasury/government. If retained, test
whether toll cash is adequate for the required O&M and capital-service obligations. If remitted and the road entity depends on appropriations,
use the network-level unconstrained-O&M/allocation/execution analysis above rather than treating gross toll collections as spendable operator
cash.

#### Social, municipal and urban service infrastructure

For education, health, elderly care, solid waste, urban metro and analogous public services where full user-pay cost recovery is structurally or
politically unrealistic, focus on the recurrent-service funding architecture and government fiscal capacity. High recurrent cost relative to
initial capex can make a 'fully funded construction' project unsustainable in operation.

Where a health/elder-care service depends materially on an insurer or public purchasing agency, assess that payer's capacity and payment
mechanism rather than assuming billed service automatically becomes operator cash.

### Public-sector option / comparator and value for money — conditional authority module

Where the jurisdiction requires a public-sector comparator, reference project or private-finance value-for-money
analysis, ensure the compared options represent an equivalent service/output and an equivalent whole-life asset
condition. Do not create apparent PPP value by assuming:

- lower maintenance in the public option;
- a different service specification;
- different project timing without explanation;
- risks in one option but not the other; or
- tax/financing conventions inconsistent with the governing methodology.

The procurement-choice model is not the same as the SPV model. Apply the jurisdiction's current appraisal and
discounting rules and keep social/economic appraisal distinct from project-company cash-flow valuation.


#### VfM is relative, performance-equivalent and distinct from affordability

Value for money is fundamentally a **comparison of value/performance and whole-life cost between alternatives**, not a standalone return
metric. Compare procurement options at an equivalent required output/service level and asset condition. A lower-cost option that fails the
minimum service requirement is not better VfM, and a higher-cost option can still offer superior VfM if the additional performance/value
justifies the cost under the governing methodology.

Keep three filters separate:

- **minimum service/performance requirement** — options below it are not compliant alternatives;
- **affordability** — options above the authority's credible funding limit may be unavailable even if they offer high value; and
- **VfM** — among feasible/affordable alternatives, which option provides the best relationship between cost, service and risk?

Do not call an unaffordable PPP 'good VfM' as though that solves the funding problem, and do not call the cheapest compliant cash-flow NPV
'best VfM' if material non-valued performance differences are excluded from the comparison.

#### The VfM tool must match the decision stage and purpose

Different PPP frameworks use VfM analysis for different decisions. Depending on the jurisdiction, it can act as:

- an early **screen/awareness tool** to decide whether the PPP option deserves detailed development;
- a **gateway** before procurement;
- a procurement **negotiation/reference tool**;
- a **bid sanity check** against the conventional/reference option; or
- an ex-durante/ex-post review of whether expected PPP benefits actually materialised.

Do not use an early conceptual comparator as though it were a precise benchmark for a final bid, and do not freeze an appraisal-stage PSC
when scope, market pricing, risk allocation or timing has materially changed by procurement. Apply the governing update rules and preserve a
clear date/status for each comparison.

Where a framework treats competitive tension and market quality as evidence of VfM during procurement, assess whether sufficient credible
competition actually existed. A technically compliant single-bid outcome does not carry the same evidentiary weight as a well-contested market
process unless the methodology provides another robust benchmark.

#### PPP 'risk-transfer premium' and whole-life efficiency are alternative/combined VfM lenses

A PPP can cost more in financing and transaction terms than conventional public delivery while still be expected to deliver value if the extra
cost buys economically valuable risk transfer, cost/time certainty, lifecycle integration or service-performance incentives. Another VfM lens
focuses on design-build-operate integration, innovation and whole-life efficiency.

Where the authority case relies on either logic, show the bridge rather than simply asserting a private-sector efficiency percentage:

`incremental PPP transaction/finance cost → risk transferred / delivery certainty / lifecycle efficiency / service improvement → net whole-life authority or societal value`

Do not count the same benefit twice as both a quantified risk adjustment and a separate qualitative 'certainty' benefit without explaining the
methodology.

#### Quantitative and non-valued effects must be integrated, not competing truths

Not every relevant difference can be monetised reliably. Depending on the governing methodology, **non-valued effects** can include
quantitative but non-monetised factors or qualitative factors such as:

- flexibility/adaptability;
- quality of competition/market appetite;
- deliverability and achievability;
- service integration;
- innovation potential;
- earlier service delivery where not already monetised; and
- institutional/contract-management capability.

Record material non-valued effects explicitly and explain how they influence the procurement decision. Do not silently assign arbitrary cash
values merely to force every effect into the NPV, but equally do not ignore a material qualitative disadvantage simply because it lacks a price.
The relative weight of quantitative and qualitative criteria is jurisdiction-specific.

#### Risk treatment and discount rate must not double count the same uncertainty

In VfM analysis, risk can be reflected through cash-flow adjustments, a risk-adjusted discount rate, or a methodology-specific combination.
The reviewer should identify the governing approach and map each material risk once.

High-value challenge:

`risk quantified in PSC/PPP cash flow + same risk premium embedded again in the discount rate = potential double count`

The inverse problem also matters: a risk can be omitted if the methodology assumes it is in the discount rate but the applied rate does not in
fact capture it.

Do not import another country's PSC discount-rate convention. The appropriate rate can depend on whether the assessment is from the narrow
authority-budget perspective or a broader socio-economic/taxpayer perspective, and on how the governing methodology treats risk.

#### VfM risk analysis should be proportionate and evidence-based

Risk analysis can range from qualitative prioritisation to detailed probability analysis. Match effort to project maturity, data quality,
materiality and the governing methodology. Where risks are correlated, avoid mechanically summing standalone expected values if that would
overstate total exposure; construct a coherent combined view or apply the prescribed correlation treatment.

Outside-view or standard adjustments can be useful at early maturity where project-specific risk evidence is weak, but should normally be
replaced/refined by project-specific risk analysis as design, procurement and contractual allocation mature. Do not preserve a broad early
uplift alongside a detailed later quantified risk register if both represent the same risk.

#### Ex-durante and ex-post VfM — conditional

For an operating PPP, an authority may assess whether the expected sources of VfM actually emerged. Relevant evidence can include:

- actual construction cost/time performance relative to the counterfactual/reference expectation;
- service quality and availability;
- maintenance/lifecycle condition;
- deductions and persistent underperformance;
- realised refinancing/insurance/market-testing savings;
- authority contract-management cost;
- material variations/renegotiations; and
- service continuity/handback outcomes.

This is not a retrospective re-run of the original spreadsheet with hindsight inputs. The purpose is to test the **realised procurement and
contract-management outcome** and feed lessons into future projects/programmes.

#### Competitive-neutrality adjustments are jurisdiction-specific cash-flow adjustments

Where the applicable PSC methodology includes competitive neutrality, do not use it as a generic percentage for presumed
public/private efficiency differences. Under the Australian National PPP framework, for example, the adjustment is intended
to remove material advantages/disadvantages arising from government ownership that are not equally available to bidders and
is treated on a **cash-flow** basis. Differences in competitive efficiency are conceptually separate, and non-cash depreciation
is not itself a competitive-neutrality cash flow.

The auditor should therefore identify the governing PSC methodology and trace each adjustment to an actual ownership-related
cash consequence such as taxes, regulatory compliance or equivalent corporate support, rather than embedding an unexplained
'private sector efficiency' uplift or public-sector handicap.

### Authority-side economic appraisal / cost-benefit analysis — conditional

Where the model includes an economic appraisal, EIRR/ENPV, social cost-benefit analysis or similar authority/development-finance case,
keep the **economic/social appraisal perimeter** distinct from the SPV's financial cash flows and from procurement value-for-money.
Economic appraisal asks whether the infrastructure creates sufficient net value for the relevant society/economy; it is not simply the
project company's NPV with a different discount rate.

#### Financial cash flow versus economic resource flow

Apply the governing institutional/jurisdictional methodology to determine which items are real resource costs/benefits and which are
transfers within the appraisal perimeter. Depending on that perimeter and methodology, tolls/tariffs, taxes, subsidies and financing
flows may redistribute value between users, the project company and government without themselves representing an equal incremental
social benefit or resource cost. Conversely, market prices may require shadow-price or externality adjustments where the prescribed
method calls for them.

Do not hard-code EIB, ADB or another institution's discount rate, shadow exchange rate, carbon value or decision threshold into a
universal infrastructure test. These are methodology-, country- and date-specific inputs.

#### With-project versus without-project counterfactual

The economic case should compare the project against a credible **without-project** counterfactual, not against an artificial zero-activity
world. The counterfactual may contain existing infrastructure, maintenance, congestion, losses, service deterioration, alternative
investments or other changes that would occur without the proposed project.

Trace the difference between with- and without-project physical outcomes into incremental economic benefits and costs. Prevent the model
from claiming benefits that would have occurred anyway, ignoring diverted/displaced activity, or omitting costs that remain in the
counterfactual.

#### Benefit attribution and double-counting

Economic benefits should remain tied to the sector's physical service engine and the governing valuation method. Examples can include
travel-time/reliability/safety effects in transport, loss/reliability effects in power, and service reliability/health/time or resource
benefits in water. Do not count two monetisations of the same underlying benefit merely because both can be estimated.

High-value double-counting challenges include, where relevant:

- user willingness-to-pay/consumer-surplus benefit plus the same user tariff revenue as a separate social benefit;
- travel-time/accessibility benefit plus full land-value uplift where the latter largely capitalises the same accessibility effect;
- avoided resource/vehicle/energy cost plus another benefit measure that already embeds that avoided cost; or
- reliability/availability improvement plus downstream output benefit without checking overlap.

The exact treatment depends on the applicable CBA methodology and project context; these are challenge points, not automatic exclusions.

#### Economic viability and financial sustainability are separate

A project can create positive economic value yet lack sufficient cash to maintain the asset or service its obligations, and a financially
profitable concession can still have weak net social value. Reconcile economic appraisal to the infrastructure's long-run financial and
operating sustainability where the decision requires both.

For a utility or user-pay service, this bridge can include:

`economic service benefit → tariff/charge policy → affordability/collection → subsidy/support → O&M/lifecycle funding → sustained service`

Do not assume an EIRR/ENPV conclusion proves the tariff is financeable, the authority can fund the subsidy, or the operator can maintain
service quality. Conversely, a financing shortfall does not by itself erase the underlying economic benefit; it identifies a funding,
tariff, scope or delivery problem that must be solved.

### IFRIC 12 service-concession interface — conditional accounting module

Where statutory financial statements are within scope and IFRS applies, determine whether the service concession
falls within IFRIC 12 before assuming the infrastructure is ordinary operator PPE. The core infrastructure distinction
is:

- a **financial asset** to the extent the operator has an unconditional contractual right to receive cash from the
  grantor;
- an **intangible asset** to the extent the operator receives a right to charge users; or
- a combination where both rights exist.

This classification can materially alter accounting statements and some model outputs, although the underlying
project cash economics remain governed by the concession. Do not force IFRIC 12 where its control/residual-interest
criteria are not met, and do not confuse accounting classification with who economically bears demand risk.

## Construction, commissioning and service commencement

Infrastructure value usually depends on the project becoming an **operational service**, not merely a
physically completed asset.

The model should distinguish where material:

- notice to proceed;
- design approvals;
- land/site access;
- construction start;
- sectional completion;
- systems integration;
- testing;
- trial operations;
- independent certification;
- safety/regulatory approval;
- practical/substantial completion;
- service commencement / commercial operation; and
- final completion.

Revenue, operating obligations, liquidated damages, availability payments and debt/project milestones may be
triggered by different dates. One “completion date” should not be used for every economic consequence unless
that is genuinely how the contract works.

### Interface risk

Large infrastructure projects are systems of interdependent packages. Examples include:

- civil works + signalling + rolling stock + power for rail;
- roadworks + tolling system + operations centre;
- terminal + runway + baggage/security systems at airports;
- quay + cranes + yard + gate + hinterland links at ports;
- source + treatment + pumping + network at water utilities; and
- passive fibre + active electronics + backhaul + customer connections in broadband.

If one package finishes late, the whole asset may be unable to earn revenue. The model should not assume
independent package completion where system acceptance requires all critical interfaces.

### Construction programme-to-model bridge

For a material greenfield/expansion project, reconcile the financial model's dates and spend profile to the latest
credible construction programme. The bridge should capture, where relevant:

- critical path and near-critical path;
- available float and who owns it contractually;
- land/utility/enabling dependencies;
- package-to-package interfaces;
- long-lead equipment;
- seasonal/weather windows;
- factory acceptance testing and delivery;
- systems integration;
- trial operation / reliability run;
- independent certifier or authority acceptance;
- sectional/phased opening; and
- soft-landings or ramp to full service where applicable.

A delay sensitivity should move the costs and revenues that actually depend on the delayed activity. Applying the
same blanket delay to every capex line can overstate or understate IDC, escalation, LDs, resource costs and service
commencement exposure.

### Construction cost, contingency and cost-to-complete stack

Build the funding requirement from the economic cost stack rather than one undifferentiated capex number. Depending
on project stage, distinguish:

- executed/committed D&C price;
- owner/SPV costs outside the D&C contract;
- provisional sums / allowances / undefined scope;
- escalation and FX exposure not fixed by contract;
- interface and enabling works;
- testing/commissioning/spares;
- land/utility/permit costs;
- identified risk-event allowances;
- contingency;
- financing carry/IDC and fees; and
- remaining cost to complete after actual spend/commitments.

Prevent double counting between contractor contingency, owner's contingency, quantified risk allowance, generic
overrun sensitivity and modelled specific risks. For an operating or partly built asset, use **remaining exposure**: a
percentage overrun on original total capex is not a meaningful proxy when most work is already completed or fixed.

Where an independent QS/technical adviser has validated cost and programme, the model should reconcile to the scope,
base date, escalation treatment, contingency perimeter and remaining commitments of that review.

### Construction actuals, remaining cost and remaining finance — live construction projects

Once construction actuals exist, future capex and funding should be based on the **remaining project**, not a fresh rerun of
the original full-life construction curve.

Reconcile at the actual/forecast cut-over:

- certified/actual work completed and cash paid;
- work-in-progress and accrued construction liabilities where material;
- committed but unpaid amounts;
- remaining contract cost and current cost-to-complete;
- actual debt drawn and capitalised interest/fees to date;
- undrawn debt commitment that remains legally available;
- actual sponsor/public funding contributed;
- remaining equity/support obligation; and
- remaining contingency/standby availability.

Future construction spend should be reprofiled over the remaining programme so that actuals plus forecast reconcile to the
current cost-to-complete. Likewise, future debt/equity draws should reflect the remaining committed funding and the finance
package's equity-first/pro-rata/other funding rule. Do not re-scale historic draws or overwrite actual sponsor funding simply
to preserve the original gearing percentage.

Where total debt commitment itself depends on project cost including IDC, fees or reserve funding, remaining-debt and
remaining-cost calculations can be interdependent. The economic requirement is that the post-cutover financing still funds the
current cost-to-complete under the executed financing rules; the generic method used to resolve circularity belongs outside
this domain skill.

#### Separate pure COD delay from delayed construction progress

A "delay" sensitivity can represent different physical events. Distinguish at least:

- **completion/acceptance delay after construction spend substantially occurs** — the asset may sit idle or continue testing,
  delaying revenue while much of the original capex profile is unchanged; and
- **construction-progress delay** — physical work and therefore remaining spend move later, often with additional site,
  escalation, contractor and financing cost.

Do not automatically shift the entire construction S-curve for every COD delay, or hold the spend profile fixed for a genuine
construction-progress delay. The scenario should match the underlying event.

### Delay loss versus liquidated-damages recovery

Where construction delay is material, distinguish the project's **gross economic loss from delay** from the amount contractually
recoverable from the D&C/EPC contractor. Gross delay exposure can include lost or deferred service/toll/offtake revenue, additional IDC
and fees, escalation, extended SPV/adviser/insurance/O&M mobilisation cost, hedge effects and downstream lifecycle/term consequences.

LD recovery should separately reflect the executed rate/formula, eligible delay days, relief/excusable events, cap, exclusivity/remedy
rules, security/guarantee support, contractor credit and collection timing. A cap can leave substantial retained SPV loss; a theoretical
LD entitlement can also create a liquidity gap if cash is disputed or collected later.

Do not assume `delay cost = LD recovery`. Bridge:

`gross project delay loss → contractual LD entitlement → cap/exclusions/relief → recoverability and timing → net retained project loss`

## Physical capacity, throughput and bottlenecks

Infrastructure output is usually constrained by a **system bottleneck**, not a single nameplate number.

For a material capacity model, identify:

`demand → constrained throughput/service → utilisation → degradation/congestion → capacity augmentation trigger`

Capacity may be limited by:

- core asset capacity;
- connecting/hinterland network;
- terminal/station/yard/gate capacity;
- processing/treatment equipment;
- operating hours;
- staffing;
- maintenance outages;
- reliability/availability;
- safety separation/headway rules;
- storage/buffer capacity;
- environmental licence limits; or
- customer/market constraints.

Do not let volume exceed a downstream bottleneck just because the primary asset's nameplate capacity is
higher. Conversely, do not assume every nominal constraint binds simultaneously.

### Capacity expansion and modularity

Where demand approaches capacity, the model should connect demand growth to the actual augmentation path:

- trigger metric;
- design/permitting lead time;
- construction period;
- capex;
- outage/interface effects;
- commissioning;
- resulting incremental capacity; and
- tariff/payment/regulatory recovery if applicable.

A forecast that grows demand indefinitely while leaving infrastructure capex flat is suspect unless spare
capacity is evidenced.

## Lifecycle maintenance, renewals and asset condition

Infrastructure has long-lived assets but many components have lives materially shorter than the concession or
valuation period. Separate:

- routine O&M;
- periodic maintenance;
- lifecycle renewal/replacement;
- capacity augmentation;
- regulatory/compliance upgrades;
- major overhaul; and
- handback remediation.

Major lifecycle spend should be linked to physical drivers such as age, usage, condition, cycles, failures,
inspection results or prescribed replacement intervals. Do not model long-life infrastructure as though all
construction capex lasts unchanged for 25–40 years.

### Lifecycle funding — conditional

Where the contract or financing structure requires a lifecycle/major maintenance reserve or renewal fund,
model the actual contribution, permitted use, release and handback interaction. Do not force a reserve where
none exists, but do not omit a contractual reserve simply because lifecycle capex is visible elsewhere.

### Planned-versus-actual lifecycle and asset-condition reconciliation

For operating infrastructure, do not review lifecycle solely against the original base case. Where actual maintenance,
condition surveys, defects, asset registers or renewal plans exist, reconcile:

`base-case lifecycle plan → actual spend/work completed → current asset condition → remaining renewals → handback /
service obligation`

Lower-than-base-case lifecycle spend is not automatically a saving. It may represent efficiency, deferral, under-
maintenance or transfer of work into a later period. The financial model should reflect the physical reason.

### Post-debt discipline cliff

Senior debt often matures before concession expiry. Once senior debt is repaid, lender controls, reserve requirements
and distribution restrictions can weaken while lifecycle and handback obligations remain. Where material, test the
period from final senior debt repayment to handback separately:

- required lifecycle/remediation spend;
- remaining security/reserves;
- shareholder distribution policy;
- asset-condition obligations;
- authority inspection/remedy rights; and
- residual service-performance exposure.

Do not infer that the project is low risk merely because it is debt free. The final years can contain concentrated
asset-condition and handback obligations with fewer financing disciplines.

### Handback and residual condition

Where assets revert or are transferred at expiry, the model should reflect the actual handback standard rather
than assuming the project can minimise maintenance in the final years.

Relevant features can include:

- required remaining useful/residual life;
- condition/performance standards;
- inspections several years before expiry;
- remedial work plan;
- handback reserve, bond or security;
- final condition survey;
- transition costs;
- spares/documentation/data/IP transfer; and
- staff or operating transition where relevant.

The concession end date is not automatically the end of all cash costs. Conversely, do not create a terminal
asset sale value for an asset that is transferred back to the authority for nil consideration unless the contract
provides compensation.

### Handback retention, escrow and lifecycle-surplus capture — conditional

Handback security is not always a project-funded reserve account. Some PPP contracts permit the authority, following a
pre-expiry condition survey, to **withhold part of the remaining service/unitary payments into an escrow or retention fund**
until identified rectification or maintenance work is completed. The fund may then reimburse compliant work, pay for
self-procured remediation if the private party fails to act, and return any residual balance only after the handback condition
is satisfied. Some structures can also sweep an identified lifecycle surplus into this retention mechanism.

Where such a mechanism exists, distinguish:

- project cash reserved voluntarily/contractually by the SPV;
- service-payment amounts withheld by the authority;
- lifecycle surplus subject to retention;
- actual remediation spend and reimbursement; and
- residual release after satisfactory work.

Do not double count both a full SPV handback reserve and full authority withholding unless both are contractually required.

## Contract expiry, extension and terminal economics

Separate:

- physical useful life;
- concession/lease/licence term;
- debt maturity;
- model forecast horizon; and
- economic valuation horizon.

A long-lived asset may have no private terminal value if the concession ends earlier. A regulated network may
continue beyond the explicit model period. A concession may contain renewal/extension options that are not
certain enough for the base case.

Do not use generic perpetuity terminal value for a finite concession unless the legal/economic rights support it.

### Authority service continuity, transition and reprocurement — conditional

Expiry or early termination can create authority-side costs even when the asset itself is handed back. Where the authority model or
termination/expiry case is intended to capture public cash exposure, assess the practical route to uninterrupted service, including where
relevant:

- interim operator/O&M arrangements;
- retender/reprocurement and adviser cost;
- mobilisation and systems/data transfer;
- staff transfer or transitional workforce cost;
- inventory, spares, licences and permits;
- condition remediation not recovered from project security; and
- temporary service arrangements while a replacement operator is procured.

Do not assume the authority receives an operational asset and a fully functioning replacement service provider at zero transition cost on
the concession end date. Equally, do not add generic transition cost where the existing contract, successor procurement or another party
clearly bears it.

## Change, relief, force majeure and economic rebalancing — conditional

Long infrastructure contracts commonly contain mechanisms for events that were not priced as ordinary
operator risk. Depending on the contract, these may include:

- compensation events;
- relief events;
- authority-caused delay;
- discriminatory or specific change in law;
- general change in law with sharing mechanisms;
- force majeure;
- emergency measures;
- tariff extraordinary review;
- scope variation; and
- extension of time.

Where a scenario assumes such an event, the model should apply the contract's **economic consequence**, not
simply the gross cost. A cost may be compensated, shared, delayed, passed through, capitalised into a tariff
reset, or borne by the project.

Do not add expected compensation to a base case unless the trigger is supportable. Do not ignore relief when
stress-testing an event that the contract expressly protects.

### Live claims, disputes and unsettled change events

Once a PPP is in construction or operations, the current forecast may contain material claims whose **existence, entitlement,
quantum and payment date are different facts**. For each material unsettled event, distinguish where applicable:

`event / notice → claimed entitlement → time relief requested → amount claimed → authority / project-company assessment → certified or agreed amount → disputed balance → settlement / determination → cash receipt or payment`

Also reflect contractual interest/indexation, mitigation, legal/adviser cost, financing carry and incremental funding where these
are economically material and contractually supportable. The project-company forecast should not book the full disputed claim
as certain revenue merely because a notice has been issued. The authority forecast should not ignore a credible pending exposure
merely because it has not yet been settled.

Where claims are resolved through settlement, waiver, amendment, expert determination, arbitration or judgment, update the
current forecast to the resolved economics while preserving the contractual base case where it still has a defined compensation,
refinancing or other contractual role. Delayed claim cash can itself create bridge-funding or covenant consequences.

### Contractual adjustment versus renegotiation

Distinguish a **pre-agreed adjustment mechanism** from a true renegotiation of the concession. Indexation, benchmarking,
market testing, change-in-law relief, compensation events and formulaic financial-equilibrium provisions may change cash
flows while remaining within the original bargain. A renegotiation rewrites material contractual terms because the existing
mechanisms are inadequate or the parties choose a different bargain.

For a material renegotiation/restructuring case:

- freeze and preserve the pre-renegotiation economic baseline;
- identify the event or distress that triggered negotiations;
- separate contractual entitlements from discretionary concessions by each party;
- model debt restructuring, new public support, tariff/payment changes, term changes and equity changes together;
- identify which risks and decision rights move between parties; and
- quantify the transfer of economic value rather than presenting the revised case as a neutral 'updated forecast'.

Do not assume government rescue merely because an infrastructure service is politically essential. Conversely, if the
model explicitly assumes a rescue or restructuring, show the public cash/support and the private/lender loss-sharing that
make the rescue economically coherent.

### Renegotiation should not be an assumed rescue of an uneconomic bid

Academic evidence on concession renegotiation shows why this deserves a specific model-audit challenge. A base case should not
assume that a future discretionary tariff increase, term extension, subsidy, scope relaxation or other renegotiated concession
will restore lender or shareholder economics unless that right is already contractually or legally supportable.

Where project viability or returns depend on an anticipated post-award change:

- identify whether it is an executed entitlement, a permitted review mechanism or merely an expected negotiation;
- compare the original bid / close economics with the economics after the assumed change;
- identify which party funds the value transfer and when;
- challenge especially early-construction assumptions that future scope/payment changes will cure an aggressive bid; and
- do not infer misconduct merely from a later renegotiation — treat this as a diagnostic for unsupported economic reliance.

## Termination economics — conditional

Termination can be a material infrastructure cash flow. Where relevant, distinguish termination due to:

- authority/default or voluntary authority termination;
- private-partner default;
- prolonged force majeure;
- illegality/change in law; or
- other project-specific events.

The compensation basis can differ materially by cause. It may reference outstanding senior debt, market value,
equity compensation, break costs, deductions, insurance proceeds, asset condition, or another contractual
formula.

Do not model one generic “termination value” across all termination causes. Do not assume debt is always made
whole: that depends on the contract and financing package.

### Private-default termination may require a valuation pathway, not one fixed formula

In some PPP forms, private-party default compensation depends on whether a sufficiently **liquid market** exists for the
remaining concession. The authority may retender the unexpired contract and use market proceeds/fair value, or use a no-
retendering/expert-valuation route if there is no reliable market. A model that hard-codes one deterministic default-
termination amount can therefore be wrong even when its arithmetic is perfect.

Where the contract uses this architecture, test:

- how liquid-market/fair-value status is determined;
- whether a retender or no-retender route is selected or mandated;
- costs, timing and continued-service cash flows between termination and settlement;
- deductions, asset condition and insurance/third-party recoveries; and
- whether the valuation basis changes with lender step-in or substitution.

### Debt form and settlement route can change termination cash flow

Termination compensation may distinguish senior bank debt, capital-market/bond debt, subordinated debt that is economically
debt-like, and shareholder/subordinated instruments that are economically equity. In some contracts government may assume or
transfer senior-debt obligations rather than pay them out immediately, for example where doing so avoids economically
inefficient hedge breakage. Model the actual settlement route, mitigation obligations and break gains/costs rather than
assuming every debt instrument is repaid in cash on the termination date.

## Government support and contingent project economics

Infrastructure projects can combine commercial/project revenue with public support. Relevant mechanisms can
include:

- capital grant / VGF;
- milestone contribution;
- availability payment;
- operating subsidy;
- minimum-revenue or demand support;
- tariff subsidy;
- exchange-rate support;
- debt guarantee;
- termination undertaking;
- land/in-kind contribution; and
- public equity contribution.

For the project model, reconcile each mechanism to its actual trigger and timing. Avoid:

- counting a capital grant as both reduced capex and cash receipt;
- counting a minimum-revenue guarantee on top of full unguaranteed base revenue;
- assuming government support continues after its legal period;
- treating contingent support as unconditional cash; or
- ignoring clawbacks/profit-share/upside-sharing where contractually linked.

Government fiscal accounting belongs elsewhere; this domain skill focuses on the project-economic effect of
support and contingent triggers.

## Land, right of way, resettlement and site availability

Infrastructure often depends on linear corridors or large sites. Where material, distinguish:

- land already owned/available;
- land to be acquired by government;
- land to be acquired by project company;
- easements/right of way;
- utility relocation;
- access roads/worksites;
- resettlement/livelihood restoration obligations;
- environmental offsets; and
- staged site availability.

A project can be “financially closed” but unable to build if the construction corridor is not available. Site and
resettlement delay can affect:

- construction sequence;
- capex/compensation;
- contractor claims;
- service commencement;
- revenue start;
- relief/compensation entitlement; and
- financing draw conditions.

Where IFC Performance Standards, Equator Principles or another lender E&S framework actually applies, the
financial model should capture conditions or remediation that materially affect drawdown, schedule, capex or
operations. This is **not** a generic ESG compliance test.

## Fragile, conflict-affected and severe political-risk settings — conditional

Where project economics are materially exposed to conflict, state fragility or weak institutional capacity, translate the risk through
specific project pathways rather than a generic country-risk premium. Depending on the project, relevant pathways can include:

- physical security / sabotage and service interruption;
- inability to access sites or maintain supply chains;
- authority/offtaker payment interruption;
- permit, customs or border disruption;
- currency convertibility/transfer restrictions;
- forced contract change, expropriation or discriminatory action;
- insurance / political-risk cover exclusions, waiting periods and claims timing; and
- lender drawstop, step-in or termination rights.

Where multilateral/DFI support, political-risk insurance, guarantee, escrow, offshore account or other credit enhancement exists,
model its actual eligible risks, cap, tenor, claim process and residual exposure. Do not treat the presence of an international lender as
blanket protection against all political or conflict risk.

## Climate and physical resilience — apply where economically material

Do not add an arbitrary climate haircut. Connect project-specific physical exposure to project economics.

Potential pathways include:

`hazard → physical asset/service effect → downtime/capacity/repair/adaptation → payment/revenue/cost/insurance → lifecycle/handback`

Examples:

- flood damages road, rail, electrical or treatment assets;
- heat affects rail track, road pavement, airport runway, equipment or network capacity;
- drought constrains raw-water supply or hydropower;
- storm surge/wave conditions affect ports/coastal assets;
- wildfire affects corridors or network service;
- extreme rainfall overloads drainage/wastewater systems; and
