- heat/power constraints affect data/digital infrastructure.

Where contractually relevant, test whether climate-related service failures trigger deductions, whether
adaptation capex is recoverable, and whether relief/insurance provisions change the net project exposure.



### Climate-resilience funding and finance — conditional

Physical resilience can create substantial **economic value without creating a direct private revenue stream**. Therefore separate:

`resilience measure -> incremental capex / opex / maintenance -> avoided damage / avoided downtime / service continuity / asset-life benefit -> party receiving benefit -> party able to fund -> financing instrument`

Do not make a resilience investment bankable merely by inserting the avoided social/economic loss into SPV revenue.

Where resilience capex is required, identify which source funds it:

- ordinary project equity/debt;
- authority capital contribution or lifecycle payment;
- dedicated budget/tax/levy;
- concessional or blended finance;
- grant/climate facility;
- guarantee/credit enhancement;
- insurance or risk-transfer proceeds;
- resilience/green/sustainability-linked debt where the financing terms genuinely qualify; or
- retained project cash / lifecycle reserve.

The instrument changes **who bears cost and risk**, not the physical cost of resilience.

#### Incremental adaptation cost should be visible

Where the base technical design and the climate-resilient design differ, bridge:

`base design cost -> adaptation/resilience increment -> financing cost of increment -> operating/lifecycle change -> expected service/repair/insurance effect`

This enables lenders and authorities to distinguish normal asset scope from the incremental resilience package and to test whether
funding is committed.

#### Maintenance is a resilience mechanism

Underfunded routine and periodic maintenance can destroy resilience even where initial climate-hardening capex is sound. For roads,
rail, drainage, ports, power networks and other exposed assets, test whether the model preserves the maintenance standard needed for
the resilience design to work through the asset life.

#### Climate data maturity should increase through project development

Do not hold a concept-stage hazard screen constant through financial close. As the project matures, expect progressively more specific
information on hazard, exposure, vulnerability, adaptation design, residual risk, insurance and contractual allocation. If detailed
engineering later shows a different exposure than the early screen, reforecast the economics.

#### Climate contract mechanics should match controllability

Where climate resilience is embedded into a PPP/PBC, distinguish:

- design/performance requirements controllable by the private party;
- ordinary weather variability priced into the base contract;
- foreseeable adaptation/lifecycle obligations;
- exceptional or supervening climate events allocated under relief/force-majeure/compensation provisions; and
- retained public risks.

A broad force-majeure clause should not be used to transfer ordinary maintenance or known climate adaptation duties back to the
authority.

#### Disaster liquidity is separate from long-run adaptation economics

A project may be resilient in NPV terms and still fail through a short-term liquidity shock. Where material, test emergency cash,
insurance waiting periods/deductibles, reserve liquidity, contingent credit, authority support and debt-service timing after the event.

### Live risk register / contract-management forecast bridge — operating and construction projects

For a live project, the current forecast should reconcile to material known risks and mitigations recorded in the latest project risk
register, contract-management record, technical-adviser report or equivalent current evidence. This does not mean booking every risk at
its maximum exposure. It means that a known material risk with a credible probability/timing/cost pathway should not disappear merely
because it was absent from the original base case.

For material live risks, identify:

`current risk/event → owner → probability/status → mitigation/action → schedule/service/cost effect → contractual recovery/relief → residual financial exposure`

Preserve the contractual base case where it remains legally relevant, but ensure the **current forecast and downside cases** reflect the
latest risk position. Do not double count a risk already embedded in remaining contingency, a claim provision or a specific scenario.

## Infrastructure uncertainty and the outside view

Major infrastructure projects are exposed to systematic optimism risk in cost, schedule and demand forecasts.
Peer-reviewed transport research documents persistent ex-ante forecast error, and current public appraisal
practice such as the UK Green Book 2026 encourages historical forecast-error evidence from similar proposals.

For material greenfield/megaproject cases, use an **outside-view/reference-class challenge** where credible
comparable evidence exists:

- compare forecast construction cost and schedule with relevant completed projects;
- compare traffic/demand ramp-up and long-term usage with relevant outturns;
- identify whether the project-specific forecast lies unusually far in the favourable tail;
- reconcile why this project should differ; and
- use the outside view to challenge, not mechanically replace, bottom-up engineering or traffic analysis.

Do **not** import published average cost overruns, traffic shortfalls or generic optimism-bias percentages as
universal uplifts. Reference classes must be relevant by asset type, jurisdiction, project stage, scope,
complexity, delivery model and base date.

## Risk, uncertainty, scenarios and flexibility

Infrastructure risks are often causally linked. Scenario analysis should preserve those links. Examples:

- construction delay → later revenue → more escalation/financing carry → potentially later refinancing;
- low traffic → lower toll revenue but potentially lower variable O&M and deferred capacity expansion;
- high traffic → more revenue but earlier major maintenance/capacity capex;
- lower water demand → lower sales but potentially reduced treatment/pumping cost;
- service outage → repair cost + availability deduction + possible performance points;
- inflation shock → indexed revenue and indexed operating/lifecycle costs on different lags/bases;
- regulatory capex → RAB growth and later allowed-revenue effects, not necessarily immediate cash recovery.

Avoid “all bad” or “all good” cases that combine mutually inconsistent assumptions merely to produce an
extreme output.

## Correlated downside and causal scenario design

Single-variable sensitivities are useful diagnostics but are not enough for infrastructure risks that share causes or
interact through contract and financing mechanics. Preserve causal dependencies in downside cases. Examples:

- construction delay → later service commencement + extended site costs + IDC + escalation + possible LD/longstop +
  hedge/financing effects;
- traffic shortfall → lower user revenue + possibly lower variable O&M + deferred augmentation + weaker debt cover;
- contractor distress → replacement premium + delay + bond/guarantee recovery timing + lender drawstop;
- physical outage → repair + lost user revenue or payment deduction + insurance deductible/waiting period + lifecycle
  consequences;
- high inflation → different indexed revenue and cost baskets + working-capital/timing effects + debt/index hedge
  basis risk; and
- change event → incremental scope + funding requirement + compensation entitlement + timing gap.

Where probabilistic analysis is used, correlations and dependency logic should have an economic/technical basis. Do
not require Monte Carlo for every model, and do not assume isolated triangular distributions are automatically more
credible than well-designed deterministic cases.


# Industrial / emerging-technology project finance — conditional module

Apply this module where the infrastructure project depends materially on a technology, process configuration or commercial
deployment that has limited operating history at the proposed scale. It is particularly relevant to FOAK / early-NOAK industrial,
energy, waste, storage and process-infrastructure projects. Do not apply the same maturity requirements mechanically to conventional
civil infrastructure or well-proven equipment deployed in a standard configuration.

## Stand-alone project credit is a contract-and-performance outcome, not a sponsor label

A limited-recourse technology project should be analysed as a stand-alone economic system whose ability to service debt depends on
the project company's rights, assets, performance and contractual cash flows. The sponsor's reputation and balance sheet can matter
through completion support, guarantees, affiliate contracts, equity commitments or practical execution capability, but the project
should not be called "bankable" merely because the sponsor is strong.

A useful qualitative review frame is:

`project entity + separateness + technical evidence + executed contracts + counterparty quality + security/support + liquidity + downside cash flow → stand-alone project credit`

Where project-company separateness matters to financing, identify material sponsor linkages such as:

- cross-default or cross-guarantee;
- affiliate supply, O&M, technology-licence, insurance or tax dependencies;
- ability to merge, reorganise or transfer project assets;
- shared liabilities or cash pooling;
- parent-dependent permits, IP or personnel; and
- any sponsor insolvency pathway that can impair the project.

Do not infer bankruptcy remoteness from the existence of an SPV alone.

## Technical and commercial maturity must converge before financing commitment

Technology projects mature through interdependent **technical** and **commercial** workstreams. A model can be financially
complete while the underlying project remains immature if one workstream is materially behind the other.

A useful maturity bridge is:

`technology proof / engineering → site and permits → cost estimate → EPC risk allocation → feedstock / utilities / offtake →
O&M / technology support → insurance / third-party diligence → debt and equity terms → FID / financial close`

At each project stage, identify which inputs are:

- measured or demonstrated;
- estimated from engineering;
- supported by an LOI/MOU;
- supported by a negotiated term sheet;
- supported by a binding offer; or
- executed and financeable.

A late-stage model should not continue to treat an early pilot estimate, indicative EPC budget or customer conversation as equivalent
to an executed commercial term.

## FEL / FEED as an evidence-maturity framework — conditional

Front-end loading / FEED can provide a useful stage-gated framework for process and industrial projects. The specific FEL naming,
deliverable percentage and cost-estimate class differ by sector and company, so do not impose the New Energy Risk examples as
universal standards. The durable audit principle is that **engineering definition, cost certainty, contracting and financing evidence
should deepen together as the project approaches FID**.

For each stage gate, test whether the model's certainty matches the available evidence for:

- process configuration and design basis;
- site and utility interfaces;
- mass/energy balance;
- design throughput and product specification;
- equipment selection and long-lead items;
- capex estimate and contingency;
- construction schedule and commissioning;
- operating cost and maintenance concept;
- feedstock or resource specification;
- product/offtake specification and pricing;
- environmental/permitting requirements; and
- technology-performance guarantees or residual scale-up risk.

If the model assumes a precise fixed capex, guaranteed output and bank-style leverage while design, site, product specification and
major contracts remain preliminary, classify the issue as **maturity mismatch**, not merely assumption uncertainty.

## FOAK / NOAK distinction and scale-up evidence

A first commercial deployment should not inherit the cost, uptime, yield or reliability assumptions of a mature fleet without a
bridge from demonstrated evidence to commercial-scale performance. Review:

`lab / bench evidence → pilot / PDU → integrated demonstration → commercial design → guaranteed / lender case`

For material performance drivers, capture the physical evidence underlying the assumption. Depending on the technology this can include:

- throughput or production rate;
- product purity / quality;
- conversion yield;
- energy efficiency;
- plant availability / uptime;
- mean time between shutdowns;
- ramp-up duration;
- resource or feedstock tolerance;
- operating turndown and dynamic response;
- degradation by cycles or operating hours;
- round-trip efficiency for storage; and
- reliability under representative environmental and operating conditions.

A technology-readiness-level label can summarise maturity but does not prove bankability. The audit should test the **actual scale,
duration, integration and representativeness of demonstrated performance**. Do not import a universal TRL threshold, fixed number of
commercial reference plants, fixed test duration or fixed scale-up ratio unless the applicable lender/program/technical adviser requires it.

### Integrated demonstration matters more than isolated component success

Where commercial performance depends on several unit operations working together, successful component tests do not by themselves prove
the integrated process. Bridge:

`input/feedstock handling → process steps → intermediates → product finishing → utilities/control → storage/export`

and identify whether the tested configuration captures the material commercial interfaces. If the financial model assumes full plant
availability or yield from separate component tests without evidence of integrated operation, retain explicit integration risk.

## Technology performance and contract guarantees must use the same measurement basis

Compare the technical evidence, EPC completion/performance tests, technology warranty/insurance tests, offtake product specification
and financial-model assumptions. High-value inconsistencies include:

- model capacity measured on gross output while guarantee is on net output;
- pilot yield achieved on a selected feedstock while supply contract permits a wider specification;
- model assumes annual uptime while completion test only proves a short performance run;
- product quality required by the offtaker is tighter than the technology guarantee;
- equipment warranty covers a component but not integrated-system performance; or
- performance LDs compensate only part of the debt-service/value loss from underperformance.

The project should not receive full credit for a guarantee whose test perimeter differs materially from the modelled cash-flow driver.

## Competitive economics and capital intensity — conditional

For technologies exposed to a competitive product market, compare the project to credible alternatives using the relevant physical and
economic measures. Examples can include:

- total installed cost per unit of capacity;
- conversion cost / levelised production cost;
- variable cash cost;
- delivered feedstock/logistics cost;
- energy intensity;
- expected utilisation; and
- cost position relative to competing supply.

This is not a requirement to build a generic industry cost curve for every project. Apply it where market share, merchant sales or customer
switching depend materially on competitive production cost. A project with excellent unlevered IRR at an unsupported selling price is not
commercially robust merely because its spreadsheet return is high.

## Development scope, budget and schedule are part of the financing case

For a development-stage technology project, distinguish **development capital** from permanent project financing. Before financial close,
the project can require material funding for:

- FEED / process engineering;
- owner’s engineer;
- pilot or demonstration campaigns;
- site option/acquisition and site studies;
- permits/environmental work;
- product/feedstock sampling and qualification;
- legal and contract negotiation;
- independent engineer and market reports;
- insurance diligence;
- financing/model advisers; and
- lender/investor due-diligence costs.

Trace:

`remaining development tasks → development schedule → development cost → committed development funding → permanent-capital readiness`

Do not treat permanent senior debt as available to fund pre-close development unless the actual facility permits it. Likewise, do not
assume every development dollar is recoverable in project sources and uses; distinguish sunk sponsor cost, eligible reimbursable cost,
capitalised project cost and affiliate fee according to the transaction.

### Development funding from counterparties is not automatically equity

Engineering fee deferrals, in-kind work, supplier credits and stakeholder contributions can reduce near-term cash need, but they can create
future payables, debt, equity rights, royalties or commercial concessions. Model their actual legal/economic form and avoid counting the
same support as both cost reduction and financing.

## Contract maturity and sequencing

Technology-project contracts typically move from commercial interest toward binding terms over time. A useful dependency matrix is:

`customer/offtaker need + product specification → site / permitting → feedstock/resource + utilities → engineering / EPC →
O&M / technology support → financing / insurance → FID`

The exact order varies. The reviewer should identify **which contract is gating other contracts** and whether the model assumes value from a
relationship that is still non-binding.

For each material contract, record:

- maturity: discussion / LOI / MOU / term sheet / binding offer / executed;
- quantity/capacity;
- product/input specification;
- price, indexation, caps/floors;
- term and renewal;
- payment timing;
- termination and cure;
- force majeure;
- assignment / lender step-in;
- credit support;
- performance guarantees;
- liability caps; and
- conditions precedent.

A term sheet can support a development model; it should not be presented as if final termination, liability, indexation and lender-assignment
terms are already fixed unless the term sheet actually resolves them.

## Contract term must support the debt and replacement case

For contracted revenues or key inputs, compare contract duration and renewal notice to the debt profile and project life. Do not rely only on
headline tenor. Test:

- contract start relative to COD;
- remaining firm term;
- scheduled volume/price through debt life;
- renewal option holder;
- renewal notice period;
- termination rights;
- replacement lead time and switching cost; and
- lender cure/assignment rights.

A contract that can terminate materially before debt maturity can create refinancing/default risk even when its original term looked long.

## Offtake and take-or-pay are still counterparty risks

A take-or-pay or minimum-purchase contract can materially reduce volume risk but does not eliminate:

- counterparty insolvency;
- payment delay;
- force-majeure interpretation;
- product off-specification;
- make-up rights;
- termination;
- concentration;
- index/basis risk; or
- replacement-market risk.

Separate:

`contracted quantity → deliverable / on-spec quantity → buyer obligation → invoice → cash receipt`

and test the downside path if the buyer cannot or will not perform.

## Feedstock / resource contract and process compatibility

For feedstock-dependent industrial infrastructure, model both **supply sufficiency** and **process compatibility**. At minimum where material:

- contracted quantity;
- minimum/maximum quality specification;
- seasonal or geographic concentration;
- aggregation/logistics;
- price/index and basis;
- substitution rights;
- inventory/storage requirement;
- supplier credit;
- force majeure; and
- replacement supply.

A project is not fully protected by a fixed feedstock price if insufficient or off-spec feedstock causes lower yield, higher O&M or shutdown.
Where many small suppliers are aggregated through an intermediary, assess the aggregator’s contractual and practical ability to replace
failed sources.

## Equipment-supply and EPC interface

Where specialist equipment is procured outside the main EPC contract, trace responsibility for:

- design integration;
- delivery;
- interface engineering;
- commissioning;
- performance test;
- warranty;
- delay;
- defects; and
- liquidated-damage / security recovery.

A collection of strong individual warranties can still leave an interface gap where no one party guarantees integrated plant output.

## EPC / construction contract form is evidence of risk allocation, not a bankability label

Lump-sum turnkey, guaranteed-maximum-price, EPCM, construction management and cost-plus structures distribute design, price, schedule,
interface and performance risk differently. Do not automatically call one form bankable and another unfinanceable. Map the actual terms.

For a technology project, test separately:

- fixed/target/open-book price;
- scope completeness;
- change-order regime;
- guaranteed completion date;
- performance tests;
- technology risk carve-outs;
- LD rates and caps;
- overall liability cap;
- consequential-loss exclusions;
- bond/LC/parent-guarantee support;
- subcontract interface; and
- contractor technical and financial capacity.

A nominal LSTK wrapper provides little protection if technology risk is excluded, liability is low, or the contractor lacks capacity to pay.

## O&M continuity and technology-provider dependence

Where the sponsor or technology developer performs O&M because no mature third-party service market exists, assess the project’s dependence
on that party. Review:

- O&M manuals and required procedures;
- spares and consumables;
- proprietary tooling/software;
- IP/licence continuity;
- trained operator pool;
- technology support;
- performance guarantees;
- warranty/insurance conditions;
- step-in rights; and
- credible replacement operator.

A project may be legally separated from the sponsor but operationally inseparable if essential IP, know-how or service capability disappears
with the sponsor.

### IP rights must survive the financing downside case where required

If operation requires proprietary technology, verify that project/lender rights remain sufficient through the relevant debt/project life,
including after sponsor default or step-in. Test licence term, assignability, termination, insolvency, cure rights, source-code/technical
documentation where relevant and any royalty/cash-flow requirement. Do not assume physical ownership of the plant gives lenders the right to
operate proprietary technology.

## Technology-performance insurance and guarantees — conditional

Where technology risk is mitigated by insurance, a guarantee or performance bond, model the instrument as a contingent recovery rather than
as elimination of technical risk. Identify:

- insured/guaranteed KPI;
- attachment/trigger;
- exclusions;
- deductible;
- limit;
- waiting period;
- claim evidence;
- payout timing;
- insurer/guarantor credit;
- policy term and renewal;
- reserve/experience account;
- interaction with EPC/warranty recoveries; and
- whether proceeds reinstate, cure debt service or flow elsewhere.

Avoid double-counting the same loss as recoverable from EPC LDs, supplier warranty and technology insurance unless the contracts genuinely
allow cumulative recovery.

## Structural enhancements should cure specific residual risks

Reserves, standby liquidity, guarantees, hedges, insurance, additional governance and contract protections should be linked to a specific
risk pathway. For each enhancement ask:

`risk → gross loss / timing gap → first-loss bearer → enhancement trigger → amount/timing of support → residual project loss`

Do not award generic credit uplift merely because the project has "more reserves" or "insurance".

## Financing-source fit — current market evidence required

Technology projects can draw on commercial banks, private credit, ECAs/DFIs, government loan/guarantee programmes, bonds and other capital,
but the appropriate source depends on current:

- project maturity;
- technology risk;
- market/offtake risk;
- required quantum;
- tenor;
- currency;
- construction risk;
- jurisdiction; and
- collateral / guarantee structure.

Historical market-size, tenor, pricing and DSCR observations from a 2021 guide are not current benchmarks. Use project-specific market
sounding or binding terms. The durable audit test is whether **the assumed capital source is demonstrably willing and able to finance the
actual risk package**.

A development project should not assume that because a financing product exists in theory it will fund the required amount, technology risk,
currency and tenor.

## Equity-provider fit, governance and economic rights

Technology-project equity can include sponsor common equity, strategic investors, infrastructure/private-equity capital, EPC/supplier
investment, preferred equity and tax/incentive-driven capital where the jurisdiction provides it. Model actual rights rather than one blended
equity percentage.

Where material, reconcile:

- contributed capital;
- preferred return or priority;
- liquidation preference;
- distribution waterfall;
- board/control rights;
- dilution;
- follow-on funding obligations;
- consent rights;
- transfer/exit rights;
- affiliate commercial contracts;
- tax-credit or incentive dependency; and
- sponsor technology/IP/O&M rights.

A strategic investor may accept different economics because it receives equipment sales, feedstock/offtake access or other commercial value.
Do not interpret a low stated equity return in isolation from those side economics.

## Technology-project downside must combine physical, commercial and financing failure

Use coherent downside cases rather than isolated percentage sensitivities. Examples include:

- lower demonstrated yield + higher feedstock cost + tighter product specification;
- delayed commissioning + extended development/EPC cost + expiring customer term sheet;
- underperformance + performance LD recovery capped below debt-service loss;
- supplier failure + replacement material premium + lower plant availability;
- offtaker stress + slower collections + working-capital draw;
- sponsor distress + loss of O&M/IP support + replacement mobilisation; and
- technology-policy support expiry + refinancing need.

The purpose is to test whether the contractual structure actually contains the physical risk rather than merely spreading it among multiple
model lines.

# Sector specialist modules

# Rail / railway infrastructure — conditional module

## Rail business structure

Classify whether the model is:

- vertically integrated railway;
- separated infrastructure manager + passenger/freight operators;
- freight concession;
- passenger franchise/service contract;
- metro/urban rail PPP;
- open-access network;
- dedicated mining/industrial railway; or
- mixed national railway with public-service passenger obligations.

The economic boundary between **below-rail infrastructure** and **above-rail operations/rolling stock** is
fundamental. Do not assume track owner and train operator are the same economic entity.

## Rail traffic units and revenue

Use the economically correct activity unit. Common measures include:

- freight tonnes;
- net tonne-km / gross tonne-km;
- train-km;
- passenger journeys;
- passenger-km;
- train paths; and
- access/terminal service units.

A model can show growing passengers but flat passenger-km if average journey length falls; these are not the
same demand metric. Freight tonnes and tonne-km likewise differ materially.

Where revenue is volume based, reconcile:

`traffic units × applicable tariff/fare/access charge × mix/discounts → billed rail revenue`

## Track access, haulage and capacity rights

A track access agreement generally grants an operator rights to use infrastructure, often through allocated
train paths, against an access charge. A haulage agreement is economically different: the infrastructure/main
operator may itself provide the haulage service for third-party wagons/freight.

Where track access exists, model the actual basis:

- reserved/allocated train paths;
- variable train-km/tonne-km charge;
- fixed access component;
- capacity reservation/non-use charge;
- performance regime;
- traction/electricity/service charges;
- station/terminal/yard charges; and
- regulatory adjustments.

Do not assume “network length × access charge” is sufficient when the charge is based on train paths or usage.

### Intercompany elimination

Where passenger/freight operators and infrastructure manager are consolidated, a track access charge paid by
one group entity and earned by another should be eliminated in the group view. This is rail-specific economic
boundary logic, not an invitation to test every generic intercompany account here.

## Rail capacity is not line kilometres

Rail network capacity depends on combinations of:

- number and direction of tracks;
- signalling/headways;
- train speed differentials;
- timetable/path conflicts;
- junctions;
- crossing loops;
- station/platform capacity;
- terminal/yard capacity;
- traction power;
- maintenance possessions;
- rolling-stock availability; and
- operating practices.

A model should not infer a proportional traffic capacity increase solely from added route-km.

## Rolling stock and fleet availability

Where the operator owns/leases rolling stock, reconcile:

`fleet units × technical availability × utilisation × consist/capacity × trips/distance → practical service capacity`

Maintenance and overhaul cycles can reduce available fleet. Growth in services without additional fleet,
utilisation improvement or capacity per train may be physically impossible.

## Freight rail economics

Where relevant, distinguish:

- commodity/customer mix;
- tonnes vs tonne-km;
- loaded versus empty movements;
- wagon/locomotive cycle time;
- terminal loading/unloading;
- port/mine/industrial interface;
- backhaul opportunities;
- minimum-volume/haulage contracts; and
- customer concentration.

A freight railway's capacity can be constrained by the mine/port/terminal chain rather than the track itself.

## Passenger rail and public-service obligations

Passenger rail may combine fares with operating/public-service subsidy. Where subsidy supports a non-
commercial service, model the actual basis:

- fixed support;
- per-service/per-passenger compensation;
- eligible cost/revenue gap;
- performance-linked payment;
- indexation; or
- periodic renegotiation/review.

Do not assume subsidy automatically rises to offset every loss unless the service contract provides a true
cost-plus or gap-filling mechanism.

## Rail traction mix

Where diesel/electric traction share is an assumption, it should affect the relevant physical and cost model,
including as applicable:

- electricity consumption;
- diesel/fuel consumption;
- locomotive/fleet requirements;
- maintenance;
- traction power infrastructure;
- electrification capex; and
- availability/reliability.

A traction-share input with no economic consequence is not a rail driver.

## Integrated versus separated structures

Where the model can switch between integrated and separated rail entities, verify the **economic perimeter**:

- operating entities and infrastructure entity each appear once in the selected view;
- internal access charges are eliminated in the integrated group;
- external third-party access revenue remains;
- shared/HQ costs follow the selected structure; and
- subsidies/access payments are not duplicated when entity boundaries change.

## Rail performance regimes

Open-access/regulatory regimes can include incentives or penalties for network disruption, delays or poor
performance. Where the model includes such a regime, link reliability/availability assumptions to the actual
payment/compensation rules rather than leaving them as narrative KPIs.

# Toll roads / bridges / tunnels — conditional module


## Highway model maturity, fiscal support and currency boundary

A simplified road-PPP simulator can be useful for education or early pre-feasibility, but it is not automatically adequate for project-specific
procurement, financing or investment approval. Match the model's depth to the decision stage. A bankable toll-road/concession model should
normally be able to represent the actual traffic study, vehicle classes, tariff mechanics, O&M/lifecycle, financing, public support and contract
risk allocation rather than rely on a generic annual teaching case.

Where public support exists, distinguish at least:

- **investment/capital subsidy** — reduces/finances construction-period funding need; and
- **operating subsidy** — supports service-period cash flow or an operating/revenue gap.

Do not record the same public support as both a reduction in project capex funding and recurring operating revenue unless there are genuinely
two separate entitlements.

For public road entities, determine whether toll collections are **retained by the road operator** or remitted to central/local government. Toll
revenue that must be surrendered is not unrestricted O&M cash for the entity. If the operator depends on appropriations, apply the public-
network recurrent-cost funding test rather than using gross toll receipts as proof of sustainability.

A one-currency or annual-only road model is a material simplification where debt/capex and revenue are in different currencies, or where
construction draw, interest, ramp-up, toll reset, debt service or reserve mechanics are materially intra-year. Model granularity should follow
the economic timing required by the concession and financing rather than copying an educational template.


### Toll-road operating-cost, subsidy and authority-cash-flow bridge

For a user-pay road model, a useful economic decomposition is:

`vehicle-class traffic × class toll × chargeable distance/transaction × collection → toll cash`
`+ ancillary / other road revenues + committed operating support`
`− concession-company fixed cost − road O&M − traffic-driven cost − periodic heavy maintenance − taxes`
`→ operating cash available for the financing waterfall`

Keep **periodic heavy maintenance cash** distinct from any accounting accrual or annualised maintenance expense. A teaching model may spread
a periodic resurfacing cost through the income statement; a bankable cash-flow model must fund the actual year in which the work occurs or
the reserve contribution that finances it.

Where operating cost changes with traffic, use the project-specific engineering/operations relationship. Do not copy the World Bank model's
illustrative stepwise per-vehicle schedule as a general cost curve. The useful principle is that road OPEX can contain:

- concession-company costs incurred even before opening;
- fixed operating costs after opening;
- variable traffic-related cost;
- routine/light maintenance; and
- periodic/heavy maintenance.

### Automatic subsidy-to-coverage mode is a funding-gap diagnostic, not committed revenue

An authority/pre-feasibility model can solve for the annual subsidy needed to achieve a target coverage ratio. That calculation can be useful
to expose the **support requirement**, for example conceptually:

`required support = target debt-service cash requirement + eligible operating cost − project cash revenue`

However, a subsidy generated by the model is not evidence that government has appropriated, authorised or contractually committed the cash.
For a bankability case, replace the plug with the actual support instrument and test:

- legal commitment;
- eligible amount;
- trigger;
- timing;
- indexation;
- appropriation/funding;
- cap/expiry;
- payment credit; and
- treatment in CFADS under the finance documents.

If the project only meets lender coverage because an unconstrained subsidy plug grows automatically, report the project as **support-dependent**
and quantify the fiscal requirement rather than describing it as intrinsically bankable.

### Vehicle-class and traffic-capacity reasonableness

A toll-road model should preserve the link between vehicle class, traffic growth, toll schedule and road capacity. Where the traffic study
provides multiple classes, inspect minimum/maximum or peak-period traffic against lane, interchange, tolling and service-level capacity.
A long-term linear traffic curve that exceeds physical capacity without congestion, diversion, widening or demand response is not a valid
financial forecast merely because the spreadsheet accepts the input.

### Project, sponsor and authority perspectives are different outputs

For a toll concession keep separate:

- unlevered/project operating return;
- sponsor/equity return;
- lender coverage and debt life;
- public investment/operating subsidy; and
- authority tax/fee receipts or retained public costs.

A positive public-sector net cash flow from taxes does not prove the concession is affordable if large contingent support is omitted, and a
high equity IRR does not prove the underlying road has a strong unlevered economic case.

### Legacy road-model insolvency tests are not universal

Historical teaching models may flag "bankruptcy" from an accounting-loss/equity formula or apply a fixed investor hurdle. Treat such tests as
jurisdiction- and model-specific diagnostics only. For a current concession, financial distress and insolvency must be assessed through the
actual local legal framework, liquidity, debt default/cure mechanics, net asset position where relevant and the project's capacity to meet
obligations as they fall due.

## Traffic and demand basis

For a greenfield or materially changed toll road, traffic forecasting should normally be grounded in a transport
study appropriate to the project. Relevant drivers can include:

- origin-destination demand;
- existing traffic counts;
- trip purpose;
- vehicle classes;
- economic/population development;
- competing and complementary roads;
- toll-free alternatives;
- network changes;
- travel-time saving;
- fuel/operating cost;
- toll level and willingness to pay;
- induced demand; and
- ramp-up after opening.

Do not treat a mature operating road's actual traffic history as if it were a greenfield forecast, or vice versa.

### Traffic forecast maturity must match the project decision

A traffic and revenue forecast should be reviewed in the context of the stage for which it was prepared. A pre-feasibility traffic
screen, a procurement forecast, an investment-grade/lender forecast and an operating update are not interchangeable. As the project
moves toward financial close, the evidence package would normally become more detailed through more complete surveys, a more
mature travel-demand model, refined toll/capture assumptions, wider scenario testing and independent due diligence.

For a material demand-risk road, identify at minimum:

- the forecast date and purpose;
- whether it is authority, bidder/concessionaire, lender or operating-model work;
- base-year/model year and forecast horizon;
- survey dates and the period represented;
- network and land-use assumptions current at the forecast date;
- toll strategy assumed;
- scenario/sensitivity suite;
- independent review or lender traffic-adviser work; and
- subsequent actual/outturn evidence that has become available.

Do not describe an early high-level forecast as "investment grade" merely because it has a long forecast horizon or produces a
single base-case revenue line. Equally, do not reject a simplified early-stage model for lacking transaction-level detail if its stated
purpose is only to screen options and estimate the order of magnitude of required support.

### Decompose traffic into forecast building blocks before testing revenue

For a road that relies materially on traffic, the reviewer should understand **where the traffic comes from**, not only the final AADT
or annual-traffic output. A useful decomposition is:

`observed / existing traffic`
`+ traffic reassigned from competing routes`
`− traffic diverted away because of tolls / disbenefits`
`= opening-day / in-scope captured traffic`
`+ underlying socio-economic growth`
`+ development-related traffic`
`+ induced traffic`
`± network / policy / other adjustments`
`= future traffic`

The exact decomposition is study-specific. The important distinction is between traffic that can be anchored to currently observed
movements and traffic that depends on future events or behaviour. Existing/reassigned/diverted traffic is typically most important to
opening-day debt service. Development, induced and long-run growth can be valuable to equity and long-duration investors but are
usually more uncertain and should not be allowed to disguise a weak opening-year credit case.

Check for double counting where, for example, the same new residential or commercial development is embedded both in the
socio-economic growth forecast and again as explicit development traffic, or where induced traffic is already embedded in an
elasticity/growth model and then added as a separate uplift.

### Opening-day traffic and long-term traffic are different credit propositions

Debt providers often place disproportionate weight on the traffic that can reasonably be expected around opening because this is when
outstanding debt can be highest and the project has the least realised operating history. For a greenfield or materially changed road,
separate:

- traffic already observable in the corridor;
- the proportion that must be reassigned to the new facility;
- the proportion likely to divert when a toll is introduced;
- ramp-up to mature route awareness/behaviour; and
- later growth that depends on macro, development or induced-demand assumptions.

A project should not be called resilient because a 30-year average traffic line is strong if the first debt-service years are weak. The
financial model should expose whether the early years rely on reserve draw, sponsor support, subordinated liquidity, MRG payments,
capitalised interest or covenant relief.

### Traffic-survey evidence and annualisation

For a transaction-grade traffic forecast, the underlying surveys are part of the economic evidence base. Where material, inspect the
traffic study for:

- classified traffic counts by vehicle type and relevant direction/location;
- origin-destination surveys or another credible trip-pattern source;
- journey/travel-time surveys;
- highway/network surveys covering lanes, speeds, distances, junctions/interchanges and capacity;
- the timing and duration of surveys;
- weekday/weekend, holiday, seasonal, event and abnormal-weather treatment;
- long-duration or automatic counts used to annualise short surveys;
- expansion factors used to move from sample observations to corridor-wide/annual demand; and
- a documented data-collection/audit trail.

Counts taken during an unrepresentative tourism peak, holiday, construction closure, school break, major event or unusual weather
period should not be annualised mechanically. Conversely, a seasonal road should not be forced into one "neutral" month if seasonality
is genuinely a defining feature of annual traffic.

### Trip matrices, segmentation and network representation

Where a travel-demand model underpins the traffic forecast, understand the model's physical meaning. Material elements commonly
include:

- origin/destination zones and trip matrices;
- vehicle/user classes;
- peak, off-peak and daily/other time slices;
- road links, length, speed, capacity and speed-flow relationships;
- junction/interchange constraints where material;
- competing tolled and untolled alternatives;
- route-choice / utility parameters; and
- any matrix estimation used for unobserved movements.

A model that matches aggregate corridor counts but cannot reproduce material origin-destination movements, travel times or competing
route usage may still be unsuitable for predicting reassignment to a new tolled facility. For a material demand-risk concession, seek
evidence of calibration to observed conditions and validation against data not simply used to force the calibration.

### Traffic-model calibration, validation and model-development evidence

The review should distinguish **calibration** from **validation**. Calibration adjusts trip matrices, network coding and/or behavioural
parameters so the model better reproduces observed conditions. Validation tests whether the calibrated model adequately reproduces
independent traffic counts, journey times, trip patterns and other relevant conditions.

For a material greenfield toll road, challenge:

- a network built mainly from desktop maps with no site validation;
- material links, junctions or competing routes omitted from the model;
- excessive matrix estimation masking weak survey coverage;
- calibration targets achieved only by implausible parameter changes;
- validation performed only against the same observations used to calibrate; and
- no model-development/validation report explaining residual mismatch.

Do not import a foreign transport authority's numerical validation thresholds as universal financial-model requirements. Use the
standards governing the actual traffic assignment and assess whether the residual modelling error is material to project cash flow.

### Generalised cost, route choice and toll capture

Traffic capture should reflect the economic choice faced by road users. A useful conceptual form is:

`generalised route cost = travel time × value of time + distance × vehicle operating cost + toll/charge − route-quality / reliability benefit`

The terminology and exact utility function vary by study. What matters is that the toll road competes against realistic alternatives on
the things users actually value. Where material, inspect:

- journey-time and distance savings;
- vehicle operating-cost savings;
- journey-time reliability;
- safety/comfort/convenience or a "motorway bonus" where supported;
- toll amount and tax;
- vehicle class;
- time-of-day or frequency discounts;
- toll-plaza / electronic-collection friction; and
- competing route congestion and quality.

A toll road offering only small or unusable time savings can have materially more elastic demand than a crossing or corridor that
removes a large detour. Do not assume that aggregate economic benefits translate one-for-one into toll-paying demand.

### Willingness to pay and toll-diversion evidence

Where willingness to pay is material to capture:

- prefer **revealed-preference evidence** where a sufficiently comparable existing tolled/untolled choice can be observed;
- where revealed preference is unavailable, assess the design, segmentation and realism of **stated-preference** surveys;
- reconcile the sampled population to actual vehicle, trip-purpose, income/business and frequency mix;
- distinguish respondents who refuse to trade time/quality for toll from users with a positive willingness to pay;
- inspect whether the study has imposed an unsupported positive route preference for the project road; and
- map the resulting willingness-to-pay / diversion curve into the traffic model and ultimately the financial model.

A single average willingness-to-pay number should not override materially different behaviour between commuters, business trips,
heavy-goods vehicles, discretionary/leisure users and occasional users where those segments drive revenue.

### Toll elasticity and revenue response

Where a toll-elasticity curve is used, distinguish **traffic sensitivity** from **revenue sensitivity**. Using the absolute magnitude of
toll elasticity as a conceptual guide:

- elasticity materially below one means a proportionate toll increase can increase revenue because traffic falls proportionately less;
- elasticity around one is the theoretical revenue-neutral / revenue-maximising region for the simplified demand curve; and
- elasticity materially above one means a proportionate toll increase can reduce revenue because traffic falls proportionately more.

This is a behavioural relationship, not a universal toll-setting formula. Elasticity can differ by user class, toll level, time, available
alternatives and economic conditions, and the relevant curve may be non-linear. The financial model should not optimise toll revenue by
assuming one constant elasticity outside the range for which the traffic evidence was developed.

### Minimum traffic-adviser evidence package — conditional transaction-grade road

For a material greenfield or demand-risk toll road, the traffic adviser / traffic-study evidence commonly needs to cover more than a
headline forecast. Where applicable, look for a coherent package including:

- corridor and competing-route **site visit** evidence;
- traffic-survey / data-collection report;
- base-year travel-demand model description;
- traffic-capture/diversion model and behavioural-parameter justification;
- model-development report;
- model-validation report;
- explicit forecast-assumptions table and sources;
- future network/development assumptions;
- annualisation methodology;
- ramp-up methodology;
- toll/evasion/enforcement assumptions;
- base, low and high traffic/revenue forecasts;
- sensitivity ranking of the most influential forecast inputs;
- plausibility / sense checks and benchmark comparisons; and
- support for authority, bidder or lender due diligence through the transaction.

Do not convert the source guide's illustrative survey durations, sample-rate suggestions or pilot-count lengths into universal rules. The
review question is whether the survey and modelling effort is proportionate to corridor complexity, traffic risk and the investment decision,
and whether the residual evidence gap is understood in the financial downside.

### Matrix estimation is synthetic demand and needs bias control

Origin-destination surveys cannot observe every movement, so traffic models may fill missing trip-matrix cells using matrix-estimation
techniques and traffic counts. Where this is material to the project road, inspect whether:

- the prior and final matrices are compared;
- average trip length and trip-end totals remain plausible;
- short-trip infilling or other synthetic movements have not materially changed the economic market;
- matrix estimation is constrained by observed O-D / count evidence;
- forecast growth is not applied to a synthetic matrix in a way that compounds an initial estimation error; and
- the model is peer reviewed / validated against traffic volumes, trip patterns and travel times.

A traffic model can appear to fit screen-line counts while containing a materially distorted synthetic trip matrix. The financial reviewer
should treat heavy matrix estimation as a source of opening-day traffic risk, not as observed demand.

### Toll strategy is part of the demand model, not merely a price input

The traffic forecast should use the **same toll strategy** as the financial model unless a documented scenario intentionally tests an
alternative. Reconcile, where applicable:

- toll points / distance basis;
- tariff by vehicle class;
- peak/off-peak or dynamic differentiation;
- frequent-user or account discounts;
- exemptions;
- indirect tax;
- cash/electronic payment assumptions;
- escalation/indexation formula and approval process; and
- opening-year toll and date of first increase.

If the finance model increases tolls but the traffic model holds capture unchanged, the model may overstate revenue. If the traffic
study assumes lower tolls than the concession model, the demand forecast may be internally inconsistent even though each model is
individually plausible.

### Future traffic growth: independent drivers, causality and back-casting

Long-run traffic growth can depend on GDP/income, employment, population, car ownership, fuel prices, mobility and land use. Where
these variables are used, challenge the model for:

- a causal relationship rather than mere historic correlation;
- double counting between interrelated drivers such as income and car ownership;
- independent and current macro/demographic forecasts;
- excessive reliance on government aspirational targets rather than base forecasts;
- transfer of a short historical relationship unchanged over several decades;
- structural breaks in mobility, technology or network conditions; and
- absence of back-casting or other reasonableness testing where the study claims a strong econometric relationship.

The skill does not require a regression model. Where data are weak, a simpler long-run assumption supported by evidence and robust
scenarios can be more credible than a complex equation whose apparent statistical precision exceeds the underlying data quality.

### Development traffic and induced traffic need separate evidence

Where future traffic depends on land-use development, inspect the status, scale, timing, accessibility and funded/approved pathway of
the relevant development. A concept masterplan or aspirational zoning change is weaker evidence than a committed and financed
scheme with delivery milestones.

Where induced traffic is material, confirm that it represents genuinely additional trips or behavioural response caused by improved
access/cost, rather than trips already counted as reassigned or socio-economic growth. Development traffic, induced traffic and ordinary
growth should each have a defensible boundary.

### Traffic uncertainty register

For a material user-pay road, the financial model should not reduce all future traffic uncertainty to one percentage haircut if different
sources have different timing and economic consequences. Consider separate scenarios or overlays for:

- ramp-up length and depth;
- socio-economic growth;
- development timing;
- induced traffic;
- toll policy / escalation delay;
- toll strategy and discounts;
- FX-linked toll or hard-currency debt mismatch;
- fuel-price effects on vehicle operating cost and route choice;
- competing road / public transport opening;
- complementary access-network delay;
- operational/tolling-system problems;
- weather and climate disruption; and
- discrete unforeseen demand shocks.

A discrete catastrophic demand shock should normally be identifiable separately from ordinary low-case traffic assumptions so that
its effect is not hidden inside a blended downside.

### Traffic forecast bias: optimism, distortion and procurement selection effects

Traffic risk can arise even where the modelling technique is competent because the forecast is produced inside a transaction with
incentives. Treat the following as distinct lenses:

- **optimism bias** — an inside-view tendency to overstate success or underweight adverse outcomes;
- **strategic misrepresentation** — deliberate distortion by a public or private party to improve approval/bid outcomes;
- **winner's curse** — the bidder with the most optimistic valuation/forecast wins when project value is uncertain; and
- **survivor's curse** — projects benefiting from positively skewed error/uncertainty are more likely to pass screening, financing and
  procurement and therefore appear disproportionately in the operating sample.

These labels are not findings by themselves. Use them to direct evidence tests. A high forecast may be correct; the question is whether
its assumptions, outside-view benchmarks, incentive environment and financing due diligence support it.

### Authority-side measures to reduce traffic-forecast bias

Where the authority is procuring a material demand-risk road, strong preparation can include:

1. an independent public-sector traffic study before tender;
2. third-party / PPP-unit / public-financier review and benchmarking of material assumptions;
3. an outside-view/reference-class check for capture, ramp-up and comparable toll behaviour;
4. sharing a validated base-year travel-demand model with bidders where the procurement strategy permits it;
5. requiring a credible level of financier involvement and lender traffic-adviser review at the appropriate procurement stage;
6. evaluating the realism of bidder traffic forecasts rather than ranking bids only by concession fee/toll/subsidy; and
7. a concession/security framework that does not reward an aggressive forecast with an easy expectation of later renegotiation.

Sharing the base-year model does not transfer responsibility for bidder future-year forecasts to government. Equally, an undisclosed
forecast-realism threshold or bid penalty can reduce extreme bids but should not force every bidder to converge mechanically on the
authority forecast or remove legitimate differences in risk appetite.

### Lender traffic adviser and traffic debt case — conditional

For a privately financed revenue-risk road, independent lender traffic due diligence can materially change the traffic case used for
credit. Identify whether lenders use:

- the sponsor/bid base case;
- an adjusted lender traffic case;
- a specific low/debt case;
- independent sensitivities; or
- a combination.

The financial model used for debt sizing/covenants should reconcile to the traffic case actually approved by lenders. Do not assume
that the shareholder base case is the lender case simply because both originate from the same traffic study.

### Traffic-risk index — use as a qualitative screen, not a probability or rating

The World Bank/PPIAF guide presents a traffic-risk index that screens error, uncertainty and bias. Its most useful concepts can be
used as a **qualitative checklist** for a road model. Consider:

- asset type: brownfield versus new greenfield / off-line works;
- trip mix: frequent/non-discretionary versus discretionary/infrequent;
- strength of project need / network gap;
- magnitude and monetisability of user benefits;
- history/familiarity with tolling;
- network connectivity;
- macro/demographic environment;
- dependence on new development;
- dependence on complementary infrastructure;
- FX/toll-setting exposure;
- stability/complexity of the tolling environment;
- quality and independence of government preparation;
- financier traffic due diligence;
- strength/enforceability of the concession/security framework; and
- bidder information/capability asymmetry.

Do **not** import the guide's illustrative 0/5/10 scores or low/medium/high cut-offs as a lender credit rating or universal model test.
Use the factors to identify where the forecast needs deeper downside work.

### Shadow-bid financial model — conditional authority tool

Before procurement, an authority can use a **shadow-bid financial model** to approximate how a rational bidder and lenders may view
the project. For a toll-road traffic-risk decision, this model should bridge:

`authority traffic base / low / high cases`
`→ project toll / payment revenue`
`→ O&M + lifecycle + taxes + other contract cash`
`→ CFADS / financing capacity`
`→ debt-service headroom and sponsor return`
`→ required toll / subsidy / guarantee / concession term`

The point is not to predict the winning bid exactly. It is to understand whether the project has enough underlying financial viability to
absorb demand risk and which risk-allocation structures are likely to remain bankable and affordable. A shadow-bid model should not
be treated as a substitute for market sounding or actual lender/bidder due diligence.

### Traffic scenarios should be translated into credit consequences

Traffic low/base/high cases are useful only if they flow through the actual revenue, O&M/lifecycle and financing mechanics. For each
material traffic scenario, trace at minimum:

`traffic by class → toll/payment revenue → CFADS → DSCR / LLCR or applicable lender metrics → reserve / lock-up / default / support response`

The credit classification should use the project's actual finance documents. A generic conceptual sequence can be:

- target/covenant coverage met;
- target missed but distributions still permitted;
- distribution lock-up / distressed zone; and
- default/cure/step-in zone.

Do not import illustrative ratio thresholds from a traffic-risk guide. The useful test is whether traffic downside moves the project
through the **actual** lender protection ladder.

### Road deal structuring is a three-way trade-off

For demand-risk roads, challenge the chosen structure against three linked questions:

- **risk transfer** — how much traffic/revenue risk is actually borne by the private party;
- **bankability** — can the private party finance that risk at sustainable coverage and return; and
- **affordability** — can users and/or government afford the toll, payment, guarantee or subsidy needed to make the structure bankable.

A structure that maximises traffic-risk transfer can still destroy value if private capital prices the risk so heavily that tolls or public
support become unaffordable. Conversely, removing traffic risk can create a large fixed/contingent public liability. If the model enters
an "unbankable", "unacceptable" or "unaffordable" zone, revisit scope, phasing, capacity, toll strategy, support and risk allocation rather
than hiding the problem in a higher tariff or lower investor hurdle.

### Match traffic-risk structure to risk-and-reward characteristics

The source guide groups road structures broadly by how much traffic risk is retained, injected, shared or transferred. Apply this as a
conditional structuring lens, not a mandatory hierarchy:

- high traffic risk + weak standalone toll economics may point toward government-retained availability structures;
- low standalone toll economics + manageable traffic risk can support a traffic-linked shadow-toll or blended approach;
- strong base-case economics + material downside uncertainty can justify MRG/revenue-sharing or other risk-sharing tools;
- strong economics + low downside risk may support a full user-pay concession/lease; and
- long-term growth uncertainty with limited opening-day risk may be suited to a variable/flexible term if the actual procurement permits it.

The correct result remains project-specific and must be reconciled to the governing fiscal, procurement and concession framework.

### Availability-payment road — traffic risk is retained, not eliminated

In an availability road, the private party may have little or no toll-revenue exposure, but traffic risk can still affect government:

- if government retains toll revenue to fund the availability payment;
- through congestion/capacity policy;
- through traffic-driven maintenance/service intensity where not fully transferred; or
- through the affordability of the long-term payment obligation.

The model should test the authority funding source and the payment credit separately from SPV traffic revenue. A road with weak
underlying economic need does not become a good project merely because an availability payment can make the SPV bankable.

### Blended availability — separate payment risk from retained toll economics

Where the private party earns both an availability payment and toll revenue, reconcile:

- the fixed/performance-linked government payment;
- toll revenue retained by the private party;
- whether toll revenue reduces the government payment or is additional;
- traffic downside borne by the private party;
- government payment credit / affordability;
- toll-setting rights and future policy constraints; and
- any sharing of upside.

A blended structure can reduce the authority payment requirement while preserving some traffic incentive, but it can also expose the
private party simultaneously to government counterparty risk and traffic risk. The model should not describe this as full traffic-risk
transfer or pure availability.

### Shadow toll — distinguish traffic risk from toll-collection and tariff risk

Under a shadow-toll structure, the authority pays the private party according to measured traffic rather than relying on users to pay the
private party directly. This can transfer **traffic risk** while leaving some or all user tariff, evasion, collection and potentially FX risk with
the authority.

Where the road also levies real tolls, keep the authority's user-revenue cash separate from the shadow-toll payment owed to the SPV.
Do not net them invisibly if the concession makes one an authority receipt and the other a contractual payment.

### Shadow-toll traffic bands — model the marginal payment curve

Some shadow-toll contracts use descending traffic bands, for example a high per-vehicle payment on the first band, lower marginal
payments at higher traffic and potentially a nil/very low upper band. Where such a mechanism exists, model traffic through each band
rather than applying one average shadow toll to all vehicles.

The band design can be used to protect lender debt service at lower traffic while limiting windfall equity returns at high traffic. It can
also make bids difficult to compare and create strategic bidding incentives if both band thresholds and rates are unconstrained. Reproduce
the actual bid/contract parameters and test both downside and high-traffic authority affordability.

### Minimum-revenue guarantee — debt protection and contingent liability

Where an MRG or revenue floor supports a user-pay concession, test **why it exists and whom it protects**. In many road structures the
floor is principally a way to reduce early lender exposure to opening-day/ramp-up traffic risk, not a promise to preserve the sponsor's
full base-case equity return.

Model:

- revenue definition and forecast reference;
- guarantee floor / trigger;
- payment period and expiry, including whether protection ends with senior debt;
- government payment timing and credit risk;
- aggregate/fiscal cap and appropriation if applicable;
- interaction with reserves, senior debt and distributions; and
- treatment after refinancing or debt repayment.

Stress the MRG as a **public contingent liability** under low traffic. If the sovereign/authority payment itself is weak credit, the nominal
floor may not provide the lender protection assumed in the model.

### Revenue sharing / cap-and-collar — downside and upside are one structure

Where government provides downside support and receives upside share, model the two limbs together. A symmetric structure can reduce
public exposure to persistent upside while providing downside protection, but the exact floor, cap, percentages and reference forecast are
contract-specific.

If the MRG level and another key bid parameter such as toll or capital subsidy are both bidder variables, assess whether the procurement
created a transparent comparable economic bid. Do not convert a complex two-dimensional bid into one unexamined "best price".

### Government subordinated / mezzanine support — conditional traffic-risk instrument

A government or development-finance subordinated facility can support traffic-risk roads by absorbing losses after senior debt and before
ordinary equity, or by filling a funding gap. Where used specifically to manage road demand risk, inspect:

- draw and repayment priority;
- whether payments defer when senior debt is under-covered;
- pricing and tenor;
- conversion / springing-lien or other exceptional creditor rights where applicable;
- intercreditor voting and security;
- whether the public tranche genuinely crowds in private capital rather than replacing it; and
- institutional separation between government's role as grantor/regulator and investor/lender.

Do not assume a junior public facility is free equity. It is a public investment/credit exposure with its own loss and recovery profile.

### Full user-pay concession / lease — test whether demand risk is genuinely transferable

A full user-pay structure is most credible where the project has strong in-scope demand, demonstrable user benefits/willingness to pay,
a stable tolling/policy environment and enough downside resilience for the private sector to absorb traffic risk without extreme risk
pricing. Brownfield toll assets can be materially different from first-of-kind greenfield urban toll roads.

For an authority monetising an existing road, also test the public economics of exchanging a future toll stream for an up-front premium.
A high concession payment is not automatically good value if it is supported by aggressive traffic assumptions that later drive
renegotiation or bailout.

### Flexible-term road concession — protects long-term revenue quantity, not early liquidity

Where the concession ends only after cumulative nominal or discounted revenue reaches a target, lower long-term traffic can extend the
term and reduce long-run revenue-quantity risk. However, the mechanism does not automatically solve opening-day/ramp-up liquidity. A
project can still breach debt service before the additional concession years have value.

For such a road, test the interaction between the variable concession expiry and:

- senior debt maturity/tail;
- refinancing need;
- ramp-up liquidity;
- lifecycle/major maintenance obligations during added years;
- tariff rights during the extension;
- maximum term / handback date; and
- the exact revenue counted toward the target.

### Operating traffic reforecast should preserve outturn evidence

Once a toll road is operating, compare actual traffic and revenue with the original and latest forecasts by material class and source. A
useful bridge can distinguish:

- opening-day/ramp-up variance;
- toll/capture variance;
- underlying growth variance;
- development/induced traffic variance;
- network/project timing variance;
- toll policy / collection variance; and
- one-off shocks.

Use this evidence to recalibrate future assumptions rather than rewriting the historical forecast or claiming the original forecast was
"correct" because later growth happened to catch up.

### Traffic-study-to-financial-model traceability

For a material revenue-risk road, the financial model should be traceable to the traffic study without re-performing the transport model in
Excel. At minimum reconcile:

`traffic study scenario / version`
`→ vehicle-class traffic by financial-model period`
`→ ramp-up and traffic-growth treatment`
`→ toll/discount/exemption assumptions`
`→ chargeable transactions and collections`
`→ road revenue`
`→ traffic-driven O&M/lifecycle/capacity consequences`
`→ financing and return outputs`

If the financial model contains manual traffic overrides, post-study growth uplifts or toll elasticities not present in the traffic evidence,
identify them as separate sponsor/authority assumptions and test them explicitly.

## Ramp-up

New toll roads can take time to reach steady-state use because users need to learn routes, developments may
not yet be complete, and travel patterns adjust. Where ramp-up is assumed:

- distinguish ramp-up from underlying long-run traffic growth;
- avoid double-counting both as full growth rates; and
- reconcile ramp-up to opening scope, competing network and user behaviour.

### Ramp-up liquidity / standby support — conditional

Some revenue-risk transport projects use a standby liquidity or contingent credit facility to bridge early operating revenue
shortfalls while traffic ramps up. This is **financing support**, not project demand or permanent revenue. Where present, model:

- availability window and expiry;
- permitted draw triggers and eligible uses;
- facility cap and any annual/periodic sub-limits;
- commitment/availability fees, drawn interest and other financing cost;
- ranking and repayment priority relative to senior debt and distributions;
- whether undrawn amounts revolve or disappear; and
- downside exhaustion if ramp-up is slower or deeper than the support was designed to cover.

Do not show the full committed facility as unrestricted cash from day one. A contingent ramp-up line may strengthen senior-debt
coverage while still leaving meaningful demand risk with the project once its amount or availability period is exhausted.

## Toll elasticity and vehicle mix

Revenue is not simply total vehicles × one toll when tolls differ by vehicle class or user type.

Model where material:

`vehicle class traffic × class tariff × discounts/exemptions × collection efficiency`

A toll increase can change demand. The response can differ by heavy vehicles, commuters, discretionary users
and available alternatives. Do not assume zero elasticity without support in a demand-risk model.

## Competing and complementary network

Changes to nearby roads can divert or feed traffic. If the concession includes non-compete protection,
compensation or network-change relief, model the contractual effect as well as the traffic effect.

## Collection and leakage

Separate physical traffic from chargeable traffic and collected cash. Consider where relevant:

- toll-free/exempt users;
- evasion/non-payment;
- transponder/account discounts;
- enforcement;
- bad debts;
- cash leakage; and
- transaction/payment-processor costs.

## Road payment structures

Distinguish:

- **real toll** — user traffic and toll/collection risk generally drive project revenue;
- **shadow toll** — authority pays by usage, transferring traffic risk but not user-collection risk in the same
  way; and
- **availability road** — project revenue primarily depends on availability/performance, not traffic volume.

Do not apply toll elasticity to an availability payment merely because end-user traffic uses the road.

## Pavement/lifecycle and capacity

Traffic can affect pavement deterioration and major maintenance. Heavy vehicles may drive wear
non-proportionally to simple vehicle counts. Where material, link traffic/axle loading to resurfacing or major
renewals using the project's engineering basis.

Capacity-driven widening/augmentation should respond to forecast congestion/service level and the actual
concession obligations, not automatically to a generic utilisation percentage.

## Output / performance-based road asset management — conditional

Where a road contract is an output- or performance-based road contract rather than a conventional measured-works contract,
separate the economic categories in the actual contract. World Bank OPBRC/PBC structures can combine:

- **management and maintenance services** paid primarily for achieving defined road service levels / output condition;
- **rehabilitation works** that bring the road to a defined initial standard;
- **improvement works** that add capacity, safety or other characteristics; and
- **emergency works** responding to qualifying unforeseen damage.

Do not treat every category as either a fixed availability payment or a conventional quantity × unit-rate works contract. For
the service-level component, model the contractual condition/KPI measurement, cure period and payment reduction mechanism.
For separately measured or instructed rehabilitation, improvement and emergency works, use their actual pricing, approval and
risk-allocation rules.

A performance-based structure often gives the contractor discretion over the methods and maintenance interventions needed to
keep the asset at the required service level. The financial model therefore needs enough preventive-maintenance and lifecycle
cash flow to make the promised service condition physically achievable. Cutting maintenance cannot be treated as pure margin if
it creates later service-level deductions, rehabilitation need or handback failure.

Where emergency works are separately compensable, distinguish qualifying external damage from ordinary deterioration or
contractor performance risk; the latter should not be converted into reimbursable emergency work merely because the asset is in
poor condition.

# Airports — conditional module

## Airport traffic units

Distinguish:

- passengers: origin/destination versus transfer/transit;
- passenger movements;
- aircraft movements;
- seats/load factor where relevant;
- cargo tonnes; and
- aircraft type/weight mix.

Do not use passenger growth as a proxy for every aeronautical revenue line.

## Aeronautical revenue

Depending on the airport/regime, aeronautical revenues can include:

- passenger/service charges;
- landing charges;
- parking charges;
- aerobridge/gate charges;
- security charges;
- terminal charges;
- cargo-related charges; and
- other regulated/contracted aviation charges.

Reconcile each material charge to its actual traffic/aircraft basis and regulatory or contractual setting.

## Non-aeronautical revenue

Separate non-aeronautical streams such as:

- retail/concessions;
- food and beverage;
- car parking;
- car rental;
- advertising;
- property/leases;
- lounges;
- ground transport; and
- other commercial services.

Passenger spend can depend on passenger mix, dwell time, terminal layout, retail area, international/domestic
mix and commercial terms. Do not simply grow total non-aero revenue with passengers if the underlying
commercial capacity or mix changes materially.

## Airline concentration and credit

A high share of traffic/revenue from one airline, alliance or route group can create both demand and credit
concentration. Model downside cases coherently: airline failure can reduce passenger traffic, aircraft movements,
terminal spend and receivables simultaneously.

## Airport capacity and expansion triggers

Capacity can be constrained by different assets at different times:

- runway movements;
- taxiways;
- stands/gates;
- terminal processing;
- security/border processing;
- baggage;
- road/rail access;
- parking; and
- airspace constraints.

Do not use a single “passenger capacity” number when the capex programme is triggered by a specific
bottleneck.

## Airport charge setting

Where aeronautical charges are regulated or subject to consultation/cost-recovery rules, model the actual
mechanism. ICAO guidance recognises airport charges, cost basis, collection and non-aeronautical activities as
separate economic elements. Do not impose one universal single-till/dual-till approach.



## Airport economic regulation, charges and financing — ICAO-style conditional extension

Where airport charges are regulated, overseen or negotiated under ICAO-style principles, reconstruct the economic chain rather than
assuming one revenue-per-passenger number.

### Cost basis for aeronautical charges

Identify the cost base attributable to the aeronautical facilities/services being charged, including where applicable:

- operating and maintenance cost;
- administration attributable to airport services;
- depreciation / asset consumption;
- financing or allowed cost of capital under the applicable framework;
- safety/security/environmental costs that the charging regime permits; and
- allocated common/shared infrastructure cost.

Separate costs attributable to non-aeronautical/commercial activities. Do not recover the same common cost in aeronautical charges and
again through a separate commercial cost allocation.

### Charge design should follow traffic unit and facility use

Depending on the airport, model individual charge bases such as:

- landing / take-off by aircraft weight or other defined basis;
- passenger / terminal charge by departing, arriving or transfer passenger as specified;
- aircraft parking / bridge / stand use;
- security / infrastructure charges;
- cargo charges; and
- other specific facility/service charges.

Reconcile traffic definitions carefully. Transfer/transit passengers, exempt users, freight tonnes, aircraft movements and MTOW are not
interchangeable.

### Charges, taxes and commercial agreements are different

Do not treat government taxes collected at the airport as airport operating revenue unless the airport is legally entitled to retain them.
