Where airport-user commercial agreements, incentive schemes, volume discounts or differentiated charges exist, apply the actual eligibility,
period and traffic base.

### Consultation, transparency and economic oversight can constrain the tariff path

Where the governing framework requires user consultation, cost-relatedness, nondiscrimination, regulatory approval or another economic-
oversight process, tariff escalation is not an unconstrained model plug. Test the timetable and evidence for proposed increases, and downside
cases where the charge increase is delayed, modified or rejected.

### Non-aeronautical revenue requires its own commercial drivers

Separate concessions, retail, food and beverage, car parking, property rents, advertising, lounges, utilities and other commercial income.
For concession revenue, distinguish fixed rent/minimum guarantee from percentage-of-turnover share and passenger-linked exposure. For direct
airport-operated businesses, model the associated operating costs rather than gross revenue only.

### Airport networks and cross-subsidy — conditional

Where an airport system/network uses profitable airports or non-aeronautical activities to support smaller airports or common services,
model the actual transfer/cross-subsidy mechanism. Do not assume every airport is financially standalone because accounts are presented by
location.

### Pre-financing and major capital programmes — conditional

If the airport charges include pre-financing of approved future capital investment, distinguish current operating cost recovery from future-
infrastructure funding. Test ring-fencing/escrow, project definition, consultation, timing and refund/reconciliation requirements where the
applicable charging framework requires them.

### Airport financing must reconcile traffic risk and charge-setting rights

For debt-financed expansion, bridge:

`traffic / movements / passenger mix -> aeronautical + non-aeronautical revenue -> allowed/negotiated charges -> operating cost -> capex programme -> financing / reserves -> debt service`

A high demand forecast does not make the expansion bankable if the airport lacks the right to set charges or retain commercial revenue needed
for debt service.

# Ports / marine terminals — conditional module

## Port business model and scope

Classify whether the project is:

- landlord port / port authority;
- terminal concession;
- integrated port/operator;
- container terminal;
- dry bulk;
- liquid bulk;
- Ro-Ro; or
- mixed/multipurpose.

Identify who provides and funds:

- breakwater/channel/basic infrastructure;
- dredging;
- quay/berth;
- yard/storage;
- cranes/cargo-handling equipment;
- gate/IT systems;
- pilotage/towage/marine services;
- road/rail hinterland links; and
- maintenance.

The public/private boundary materially affects capex and revenue.


### Mixed brownfield / greenfield terminal phasing

A port concession can combine an existing operating terminal with construction of a new terminal or rehabilitation/expansion. In that case,
separate:

- existing-terminal opening cash flow;
- rehabilitation outage and capex;
- new-terminal construction and commissioning;
- traffic migration/cannibalisation;
- common management and shared equipment;
- concession fee allocation; and
- any authority enabling works required for the new capacity.

Do not delay all project revenue until the new terminal opens if the concession already operates an existing asset. Conversely, do not add
new-terminal throughput on top of existing volumes without considering migration, market share and shared capacity.

### Container-traffic segmentation and tariff mix

For a material container terminal, revenue can differ by:

- gateway/domestic versus transshipment;
- full versus empty container;
- import/export;
- handling versus storage;
- vessel/berth or marine service; and
- contractual customer discount.

Model segment mix where those categories have different prices, dwell time or costs. A transshipment discount is not a generic reduction to
all handling revenue. Check the order of operations between tariff indexation, customer discount, volume and VAT/tax where applicable.

### Fixed and variable labour / operating-cost structure

Port operations can combine permanent local/expatriate management and operating labour with traffic-linked temporary labour or outsourced
services. Where material, connect:

`core staffing + operating windows + equipment fleet + throughput-driven variable labour → total labour cost`

and preserve currency exposure where different staff or service contracts are paid in different currencies. Throughput growth with flat
operating labour and maintenance requires an explicit productivity improvement or spare-capacity explanation.

### Concession / lease payment can contain multiple economic components

Where the operator pays the port authority, distinguish:

- fixed rent/lease;
- indexed fixed component;
- per-TEU / per-tonne throughput fee;
- percentage-of-revenue royalty;
- minimum guaranteed payment; and
- variable step-up or traffic-linked component.

Do not model one blended concession-fee percentage if the contract contains materially different bases, lags or floors. In a downturn, a
fixed component creates different operating leverage from a pure throughput royalty.

### Equipment procurement, ECA financing and currency boundary — conditional

Port gantry cranes and other major equipment can be imported under contracts denominated in a different currency from local civil works
and operating revenue. Where an ECA/export-credit facility is part of the financing, map:

`eligible equipment/content → covered contract amount → ECA-supported tranche → uncovered/local funding → currency/hedge → repayment`

Do not assume every imported equipment contract qualifies for ECA support or that the financing eliminates FX exposure. Use current
eligibility, guarantee and lender terms.

### Trustee / debt-service account and operating revolver — conditional

A port project may maintain a trustee-controlled account or DSRA sized to upcoming debt service and a separate revolving/working-capital
facility for operating shortfalls. Keep the two functions distinct:

- trustee/DSRA = restricted lender liquidity/security; and
- revolver = borrowable liquidity that creates debt, interest and repayment obligations.

A cash deficit cured by a revolver is not improved operating economics. Test the borrowing trigger, facility cap, repayment priority and
availability after a prolonged throughput shock.

### Sponsor return can include EPC / operator economics as well as equity

Where a sponsor is also contractor, equipment supplier or operator, distinguish:

`project-company return` from `sponsor-group total economic return`.

The sponsor can receive EPC margin, O&M fees, equipment margin, development fees, shareholder-loan return and equity distributions. These
cash flows can create conflicts in bid pricing, capex, lifecycle or related-party transactions. Do not count affiliate margin as both project
cost saving and shareholder value. Where material, test whether pricing/risk transfer is arm's length and whether the sponsor's consolidated
return changes the incentives assumed in the SPV model.

## Throughput and revenue

Use the appropriate unit:

- TEU;
- container moves;
- tonnes;
- revenue tonnes;
- vehicles;
- vessels/calls; or
- storage/service units.

Revenue can include:

- handling charges;
- wharfage;
- berth/marine charges;
- storage/demurrage;
- concession/lease income;
- land/property charges; and
- ancillary services.

Where the operator pays concession fees, royalties, rent or throughput fees to the port authority, do not count
those fees as both retained project revenue and authority revenue in the same economic perimeter.

## Port capacity is multi-bottleneck

Port/terminal capacity can depend on:

- channel depth and vessel size;
- berth length/occupancy;
- crane number/productivity;
- yard density/dwell time;
- storage capacity;
- gate throughput;
- labour/operating windows;
- equipment availability;
- rail/road hinterland capacity; and
- customs/inspection processes.

A terminal can have theoretical berth capacity but fail because the yard or gate is saturated. Expansion capex
should address the binding bottleneck.

## Dredging and navigational access

Where relevant distinguish:

- capital dredging;
- maintenance dredging;
- responsibility for dredging;
- target depth/draft;
- sedimentation;
- disposal/environmental constraints; and
- downtime/access restrictions.

A revenue forecast based on larger vessel classes is inconsistent if channel depth or berth capability cannot
accommodate them.

## Shipping-line/customer concentration and transshipment

Container terminals may depend heavily on a small number of shipping lines/alliances and may have different
risk for gateway versus transshipment cargo. Dry/liquid bulk terminals may depend on one mine, industrial
customer or commodity chain. Model concentration and contract tenure where material.

## Productivity and turnaround

Throughput growth should be consistent with operational productivity. Where material, reconcile volumes to
berth moves/hour, crane productivity, vessel turnaround, yard dwell and operating hours rather than assuming
throughput rises without operational change.

# Water and wastewater — conditional module

## Water balance before revenue

For a water utility/network, physical water should reconcile through the system. A useful economic chain is:

`water produced/purchased → system input → authorised consumption → billed consumption → billed revenue → collected cash`

Non-revenue water (NRW) sits between system input and billed consumption and can reflect physical losses,
commercial/apparent losses and authorised unbilled consumption depending on the adopted water-balance
definition.

Do not treat all system input as billable sales.

## NRW and NRW-reduction economics

Where NRW is material, separate the effect of:

- leakage/physical-loss reduction;
- meter accuracy/theft/commercial-loss reduction;
- pressure management;
- billing improvement;
- network rehabilitation;
- active leakage control; and
- additional source/treatment production avoided or deferred.

Reducing commercial loss can increase revenue without reducing physical production. Reducing leakage can
lower production/pumping/treatment needs and/or release capacity. Do not apply the same economic benefit to
every percentage point of NRW reduction.

## Billing versus collection

Billed revenue and cash collection are not the same. Reconcile:

`billed volume × tariff → bills issued → collection efficiency / timing → cash received`

A utility can improve meter/billing performance yet remain cash constrained if collection deteriorates.

## Tariff, subsidy and affordability

Water/wastewater revenues may combine user tariff, connection fees, subsidy and government support. Model:

- tariff blocks/classes;
- fixed versus volumetric charges;
- approved indexation;
- lifeline/social tariffs;
- cross-subsidies if material;
- tariff-reset timing;
- government compensation/subsidy; and
- collection.

Do not assume full-cost-reflective tariff increases if regulatory/political approval is required.

## Demand, connections and consumption

Separate:

- number of connections/customers;
- consumption per connection;
- industrial/commercial/residential mix;
- seasonality where material; and
- conservation/demand-management effects.

Connection growth can coexist with flat total demand if usage per connection falls.

## Source, treatment, pumping and network capacity

Trace the full physical chain:

`source availability → raw-water abstraction → treatment → storage → pumping/transmission → distribution → customer`

A treatment plant expansion does not create saleable water if raw-water rights or network capacity are binding.
Energy/chemical costs may vary with water produced/treated rather than billed volume.

## Wastewater

Where applicable reconcile:

`sewer connections / inflow → infiltration/inflow + collected sewage → treatment → effluent → sludge/biosolids → disposal/reuse`

Relevant constraints can include sewer capacity, pump stations, treatment capacity, discharge standards,
sludge handling and environmental compliance. Do not set wastewater volume equal to billed potable water
without considering project-specific return-to-sewer and infiltration assumptions.

# Power generation / energy infrastructure — conditional module

## Power-plant physical and unit-economics foundation

Do not begin a generation review with revenue, EBITDA or IRR. Reconstruct the plant's physical conversion chain and the units used to turn
capacity into saleable electricity.

At minimum distinguish:

- **installed/nameplate capacity** in kW/MW;
- **maximum theoretical energy** over the period if the plant operated continuously at nameplate output;
- **resource / dispatch limitation** where output depends on wind, solar irradiation, hydrology, tidal resource, fuel availability or market dispatch;
- **planned outage**;
- **forced outage / technical unavailability**;
- **gross generation**;
- **auxiliary/parasitic consumption and plant losses**;
- **electrical / transformer / collection-system losses** where within project perimeter; and
- **net metered/exported energy** used for billing.

A useful top-level identity is:

`maximum theoretical MWh = installed MW × hours in period`

with actual/saleable output derived only after the relevant resource, dispatch, availability and loss factors are applied. Capacity factor is a
physical outcome or summary statistic; do not use a capacity factor that already embeds outages and then deduct the same outages again.

Keep kW, MW, kWh, MWh, GJ and fuel-mass units explicit and dimensionally consistent. Unit conversion errors can create apparently plausible
revenue and fuel-cost outputs that are wrong by three orders of magnitude.

## Availability, capacity factor and outage stack

Treat different causes of non-generation separately where the project evidence allows it:

`resource / dispatch limitation + forced outage + planned minor outage + planned major outage + grid/curtailment limitation`

Do not assume these percentages are additive without considering whether their timing can overlap. For lender/downside analysis, inspect the
actual outage methodology and whether coincident outages/resource constraints have been treated consistently.

For each material planned outage or overhaul, identify:

- start/season and duration;
- affected capacity;
- frequency / cycle;
- associated maintenance or overhaul cost;
- impact on availability/capacity payments;
- impact on energy revenue;
- spare-part / contractor / vessel / rig constraints where relevant; and
- whether the outage timing is coordinated with seasonal low-resource or low-price periods.

A constant annual availability assumption is not enough where a plant has discrete major maintenance events.

## Fuel, efficiency / heat-rate and variable-generation cost

For fuelled generation, trace physical fuel input to electrical output. Depending on the source data, the model may use heat rate, thermal
efficiency, calorific/heating value or a direct fuel-consumption coefficient. The economic relationship should remain transparent:

`fuel quantity required ↔ fuel energy content ↔ conversion efficiency / heat rate ↔ net generation`

and:

`fuel purchase price + transport / handling / treatment + taxes/charges where applicable -> delivered fuel cost -> variable generation cost`

Where fuel is quoted by tonne, volume or energy unit, verify the conversion basis and gross/net calorific value convention used by the contract
and technical study. A change in efficiency or heat rate should change fuel consumption for a given net output unless the offtake/fuel contract
specifically compensates the project for that change.

If the PPA passes through fuel price but applies a contractual heat rate, isolate:

- price risk passed through;
- volume / efficiency risk retained;
- fuel-quality risk;
- transport/logistics risk;
- start-up fuel; and
- mismatch between fuel invoice index/date and PPA reimbursement index/date.

## Fixed versus variable O&M and lifecycle unit economics

Separate operating cost according to its physical driver:

- fixed O&M / plant management / insurance / site costs that accrue by period or installed capacity;
- variable O&M linked to MWh, starts, throughput or operating hours;
- consumables / chemicals / waste disposal;
- fuel logistics where relevant;
- planned maintenance;
- major overhaul / lifecycle replacement; and
- technology-specific field/network costs.

Useful diagnostic metrics include fixed O&M per kW-year, variable O&M per MWh, capex per kW and major-lifecycle cost per relevant unit. These
are **sense-check ratios**, not universal benchmarks. Any external comparison must be normalised for technology, scope, scale, location,
resource quality, grid works, environmental standard, price date and contract perimeter.

For annual output review, reconcile cash/revenue/cost per MWh or per kWh back to the physical generation schedule. An unexplained step change
in £/$ per MWh is often more informative than an unchanged headline EBITDA margin.

## Construction-to-operation bridge for generation assets

For an asset not yet operational, reconcile:

`development / enabling works -> EPC / construction spend -> interconnection works -> testing / commissioning -> COD -> operating life`

and, where material:

`real/base-price capex -> cash-flow-specific escalation/indexation -> nominal/MOD capex -> funding requirement -> IDC/fees -> total funded cost`

The construction programme should drive both capex timing and the date on which output can physically begin. A plant cannot generate merely
because the financial model reaches an assumed operations flag if EPC, fuel/resource, interconnection or acceptance milestones are incomplete.

Do not carry a quoted capex-per-kW benchmark directly into a model without a dated, phased construction scope. Conversely, use capex per kW
as a reasonableness check on the completed scope.

## Power tariff unit economics and required-tariff analysis

Where the offtake structure contains more than one revenue component, model them separately. Common forms can include:

- energy charge per kWh/MWh;
- standing/capacity charge per kW/MW or available capacity;
- availability payment;
- fuel/commodity pass-through;
- start-up or ancillary-service payment;
- renewable/environmental attribute revenue;
- inflation/index escalation; and
- performance deductions / LDs / true-ups.

For negotiation or bankability analysis it can be useful to **reverse solve** the base-year tariff or capacity payment that would be required to
meet a defined equity return or lender coverage target. Treat this as a diagnostic:

`required tariff for target return / coverage -> compare with executed/allowed/market/affordable tariff -> bankability or funding gap`

A tariff generated by Goal Seek or another optimisation is not evidence that the tariff is contractually available, regulator-approved,
affordable to the buyer or politically sustainable. A model that requires a tariff above the legal/contractual ceiling has demonstrated a funding
gap, not solved it.

## Power-project return boundaries

Where both sponsor equity and shareholder/subordinated debt exist, keep return measures distinct:

- **project / asset return** before financing;
- **ordinary-equity return** from equity contributions and equity distributions;
- **shareholder-debt return** from shareholder/subordinated loan interest, fees and principal; and
- **blended sponsor return** combining all sponsor-funded instruments where that is the decision metric.

Label real/nominal and pre-/post-tax bases. Do not compare a project IRR before financing with a blended sponsor IRR that includes shareholder
loan coupons, or use one to satisfy the other's hurdle without explanation.

## Power technology-specific physical lenses

Apply only the technology lens relevant to the asset. The book examples are instructional and their historic costs, capacity factors, incentives,
paybacks and returns are **not** benchmarks.

### Combined-cycle gas turbine (CCGT) / gas-fired thermal

Trace:

`gas supply -> gas turbine -> generator + hot exhaust -> heat-recovery steam generator -> steam turbine -> generator -> net export`

Review gas price/quality and transport, turbine and steam-cycle efficiency, auxiliary/cooling requirements, start profile, forced/planned outage,
major overhaul cycle and any PPA heat-rate/fuel pass-through. The combined-cycle output should reflect both generation trains where that is the
actual configuration.

### Coal-fired thermal

Trace:

`coal supply -> handling / milling -> boiler / steam cycle -> turbine-generator -> net export`

and the residual streams such as ash and flue-gas/emissions-control systems where material. Review fuel calorific value, quality penalties,
transport/stockpile losses, heat rate/efficiency, auxiliary load, ash/residue cost, environmental consumables/compliance and outage/overhaul.
Do not model coal cost only as £/$ per tonne without converting the delivered fuel quality into usable energy.

### Energy from waste / waste-to-energy

Reconstruct both the waste-service and generation sides:

`contracted / available waste tonnes -> accepted tonnes -> calorific value / moisture / contamination -> furnace / boiler throughput -> steam /
heat -> turbine-generator -> net electricity / heat`

and:

`gate/tipping fee or waste purchase cost -> residue / ash / hazardous stream -> disposal/recovery cost`

Some projects are paid to receive waste; others pay for feedstock; hybrids can exist. Use the actual waste contract. Do not simultaneously charge
and earn a gate fee on the same tonnes unless the contractual economics support both. Include environmental/emissions constraints that can cap
throughput even when nameplate electrical capacity is available.

### Solar thermal / concentrated solar power

Trace the actual technology chain, for example:

`solar irradiation -> collector / mirror field -> heat-transfer fluid -> thermal conversion / storage where applicable -> steam cycle -> generator
-> net export`

Review resource seasonality, optical/thermal losses, parasitic load, availability, cleaning/soiling, heat-transfer system, storage dispatch if any,
and major component maintenance. Do not import the source book's comparative efficiency claims as universal technology rules.

### Hydroelectricity and run-of-river

For reservoir hydro, connect:

`hydrology / inflow -> reservoir operating rules / head -> turbine flow -> efficiency -> gross generation -> auxiliary/losses -> net export`

For run-of-river, resource availability is directly constrained by river flow and environmental/minimum-flow obligations. A long-term average
capacity factor does not replace hydrology, seasonality and drought/downside evidence where material.

For pumped-storage hydro, distinguish pumping/charging from generation/discharge. It is an energy-storage/arbitrage service, not free primary
generation. The financial model should therefore identify energy purchased/used for pumping, storage/round-trip losses and discharge revenue or
capacity/ancillary payments according to the actual market/contract structure.

### Tidal / wave / marine generation

Trace the marine resource through device availability and the subsea/export system. Review seasonal resource pattern, marine-access windows,
subsea cable/interconnection, corrosion/fouling, vessel/logistics constraints, forced/planned outage and major maintenance. A high theoretical
resource does not eliminate marine availability and grid-connection risk.

### Wind - onshore

Trace:

`wind resource / distribution -> turbine power curve / gross output -> wake / electrical losses -> availability -> curtailment -> net export`

Review resource study basis, turbine availability, planned/forced outage, degradation where relevant, collection system, land/access and major
component replacement. Do not use the source book's historic onshore cost or capacity-factor examples as current standards.

### Wind - offshore

Use the same wind-resource bridge but add the material marine system:

`wind resource -> turbines -> inter-array collection -> offshore substation/export system -> grid`

Review vessel/weather access, foundation/substructure, subsea/export cable, offshore substation, corrosion, marine O&M, major component exchange,
curtailment/grid and decommissioning. Do not assume offshore O&M/access economics equal onshore simply because the turbines are similar.

### Geothermal

Use the separate geothermal resource-development submodule below. The distinctive issue is that drilling/resource confirmation and long-term
wellfield performance can drive bankability independently of the surface power plant.

## Energy-specific downside and combined-risk cases

Sensitivities should be derived from the project's risk assessment rather than copied from a generic template. Depending on technology and
contract, high-value cases can include:

- lower resource / capacity factor;
- higher forced outage plus higher variable O&M;
- major outage earlier/longer than plan;
- lower plant efficiency / higher heat rate;
- higher fuel/feedstock cost where not fully passed through;
- fuel logistics or quality shock;
- higher fixed O&M / lifecycle cost;
- construction delay/cost escalation;
- interconnection/curtailment constraint;
- lower tariff / slower indexation / PPA deduction;
- incentive or certificate shortfall where not fully committed;
- higher interest/base rate where floating exposure remains;
- shorter useful life / accelerated degradation; and
- combined technical + price + financing cases that reflect a plausible common cause.

Do not rely only on isolated +/-10% one-variable cases. Rank results by the economic exposure actually retained by the SPV after PPA, EPC, O&M,
fuel, hedge, insurance and support mechanisms.

## Power lender evidence and ratio traceability

For a limited-recourse power project, the lender ratios are not independent finance outputs; they sit at the end of a physical/contractual chain:

`resource / fuel + plant availability -> net metered output -> tariff / PPA payment -> operating/lifecycle cost -> CFADS -> debt service ->
ADSCR/DSCR + LLCR/PLCR`

Confirm the lender-ratio definitions, periods, reserve treatment, cash sweep/cure and minimum tests against the executed financing documents.
Where debt is serviced semi-annually, the model may appropriately use semi-annual debt periods while retaining finer construction/operating
periodicity. Time resolution should follow the real timing of construction, dispatch/resource seasonality, payment and debt service - not a
fixed modelling convention.

Where a data book, technical model, resource study, PPA term sheet, EPC/O&M contract or lender assumptions pack feeds the model, maintain an
auditable mapping from each material physical/economic assumption to the governing evidence. A plausible generation forecast with no evidence
trail is not lender-grade merely because the spreadsheet calculates correctly.

## Revenue contract: PPA / offtake before merchant forecast

Classify the economic revenue contract before modelling generation revenue. Depending on technology and market, the
project may receive:

- capacity / availability payment;
- energy/output payment;
- take-or-pay or minimum offtake;
- fixed or indexed tariff;
- contract-for-difference or similar support;
- renewable certificate / environmental attribute revenue;
- merchant energy/capacity/ancillary-services revenue; or
- a hybrid of contracted and merchant components.

For contracted IPPs, identify which payment component is intended to recover fixed/capital costs and which recovers
variable production costs. Do not expose debt service to merchant dispatch in the model if the executed PPA provides a
capacity/take-or-pay structure that deliberately removes that demand risk. Conversely, do not assume capacity revenue
if payment requires actual availability/performance conditions that the plant may fail.

## Energy cost and performance benchmarking — conditional

For generation assets, benchmark the physical/economic drivers that explain competitiveness rather than only the headline project IRR.
Useful project-specific measures can include:

- capital cost per kW/MW of net capacity;
- fixed O&M per kW-year;
- variable O&M per MWh;
- capacity factor / renewable yield;
- availability factor;
- heat rate / efficiency and fuel cost per energy unit for thermal assets; and
- levelised cost of energy or equivalent levelised cost measure where suitable for the technology and decision purpose.

A levelised cost is meaningful only when numerator and denominator use consistent nominal/real treatment, discount basis, energy volume,
asset life and scope. Do not compare a contracted renewable LCOE with a thermal plant metric that excludes fuel, carbon, transmission or
major lifecycle costs, or compare gross MWh with net saleable MWh.

Benchmarking should explain **why** project cost differs — technology, resource, scale, region, labour/material market, grid connection,
capacity factor, fuel logistics, environmental standard or financing/risk allocation — rather than impose a published industry average as
a pass/fail test.

### Multi-part PPA / offtake tariff and back-to-back operating guarantees — conditional

Some power/utility offtake contracts use multiple tariff components so that different risks are remunerated separately. Where present,
reconcile the project cash flows across components such as:

- fixed capacity/availability charge intended to recover fixed/capital cost;
- fixed or indexed O&M component;
- fuel/energy pass-through or energy charge;
- contract heat-rate / efficiency assumption;
- start-up or variable operating component;
- availability/performance penalty; and
- delay/performance liquidated damages or compensation.

Then compare the PPA exposures with EPC, O&M and fuel-supply protections. A fuel-price pass-through with an O&M heat-rate guarantee can
create a very different retained SPV risk from a single all-in tariff. Do not assume PPA protection is back-to-back unless timing, caps,
performance definitions, indexation and credit support match the relevant subcontract.

## COD, testing and performance acceptance

Distinguish mechanical completion, first energisation/synchronisation, reliability/performance testing, commercial
operation date and final acceptance. Revenue, PPA LDs, EPC LDs, debt milestones and tax/depreciation may attach to
different dates.

Where the PPA/EPC contract requires performance tests, reconcile:

- net/contracted capacity;
- efficiency/heat rate where applicable;
- auxiliary load;
- availability/reliability;
- emissions or other technical guarantees; and
- liquidated damages/remedial rights for shortfall.

## Generation bridge and loss stack

For renewable generation, bridge resource to saleable energy rather than applying a single opaque capacity factor:

`resource → gross generation → technical losses → availability/outage → electrical/network losses → curtailment →
auxiliary consumption → metered/exported energy`

Use technology-appropriate drivers such as irradiation, wind distribution, hydrology or other resource evidence. Keep
P50/P90 or other probabilistic resource cases distinct from arbitrary model sensitivities where such studies exist.

For thermal generation, reconcile dispatch/output to:

`net generation → heat rate / efficiency → fuel quantity → fuel price/index + transport/logistics → variable cost`

Fuel-price pass-through does not protect the project from inefficient heat rate, excess auxiliary load or fuel volume
caused by plant underperformance unless the PPA specifically does so.

## Dispatch, curtailment and deemed energy

Separate risks caused by the project from those caused by the offtaker/grid. Where contractually relevant, distinguish:

- economic dispatch;
- grid constraint;
- offtaker curtailment;
- project outage;
- resource unavailability;
- deemed generation/deemed energy compensation; and
- curtailment caps, thresholds or sharing.

Do not pay the project for all theoretical lost generation merely because a deemed-energy clause exists; apply the
actual eligibility and measurement basis.

## Grid/interconnection and transmission availability

Generation capacity is not saleable if interconnection or transmission is unavailable. Reconcile grid completion,
connection capacity, loss factors, outage/constraint regime and responsible party to COD and revenue. A plant-side
completion date cannot override a late grid connection unless the PPA provides compensation.

## Degradation, outage and major overhaul

Connect technology-specific degradation, forced/planned outages and major overhaul/replacement to generation,
availability payment, O&M and lifecycle capex. Do not use one constant availability factor over a long life where the
asset plan contains major overhaul or degradation effects.


## Geothermal generation and resource-development risk — conditional submodule

Geothermal power requires a separate resource-development lens because the commercially usable subsurface resource is not proven merely by
selecting a plant nameplate capacity. Apply this module only where geothermal resource/wellfield risk is material.

### Resource confirmation is a bankability gate, not a cosmetic study

Above-ground geoscience, geochemistry and geophysics can define a reservoir concept and drilling targets, but commercial resource
confirmation ultimately depends on drilling/test evidence and a feasibility basis appropriate to the field. For a project expected to use
limited-recourse finance, distinguish:

`resource concept / surface studies → exploration → confirmation/test drilling → proven commercial well/reservoir basis → wellfield development → plant construction → operations`

Do not let an early probabilistic resource estimate automatically become financeable MW. Where confirmation drilling is incomplete, label the
project as carrying material resource risk and reflect who funds that risk, what evidence is still required and how a shortfall affects plant size,
capex, schedule, tariff and financing.

### Geothermal development-phase financing and risk allocation

The early exploration/test-drilling phases can carry risk that ordinary senior project lenders are unwilling to accept. Depending on the actual
business model, those phases may be funded by government, grants/concessional capital, sponsor risk equity or specialised resource-risk
instruments, with commercial project finance introduced only after sufficient resource confirmation.

For each phase identify:

- party funding it;
- party owning the resulting wells/data/resource rights;
- completion/evidence required to move to the next phase;
- retained resource shortfall risk;
- impact on the PPA/tariff or public support; and
- reservoir/wellfield management control after private entry.

A public sector that de-risks exploration and test drilling has not necessarily removed all resource risk from the private project: long-term
reservoir performance, make-up wells, reinjection and resource management can remain with the SPV/operator.

### Physical resource-to-net-output bridge

Separate at least four capacity concepts where the project evidence supports them:

1. subsurface thermal/resource potential;
2. deliverable wellfield/steam capacity based on production wells and well productivity;
3. plant gross/nameplate electrical capacity; and
4. net saleable electrical capacity after auxiliary load, plant losses and network/interconnection constraints.

A useful physical chain for a flash-steam project is:

`reservoir / production wells → steam/fluid gathering → separation → turbine-generator gross output → auxiliary use / plant losses → export meter → revenue`

and the resource-management return path:

`separated brine / condensate → reinjection system / wells → reservoir sustainability`

The exact process differs by geothermal technology; use the actual plant design. Do not assume thermal MW, gross electrical MW and metered
PPA MW are interchangeable.

### Well productivity, well count and field infrastructure

Where material, reconcile required net capacity to:

- expected well productivity/distribution;
- number of production wells;
- number/capacity of injection wells;
- drilling success/dry-hole or underperforming-well risk where still retained;
- steam/fluid gathering capacity;
- drilling rigs/mobilisation and access infrastructure; and
- spare/replacement or make-up well strategy.

A capex model with a fixed 'wells' total but no bridge to required resource capacity can understate both resource shortfall and lifecycle
reinvestment risk.

### Resource decline, plant degradation and make-up wells are different mechanisms

Geothermal **resource decline** and **surface plant degradation/outage** are separate physical risks. Resource decline can reduce deliverable
steam/thermal potential even when the turbine-generator is mechanically available. Plant degradation can reduce conversion efficiency or
availability even if the reservoir remains strong.

Bridge, where supported by reservoir/technical evidence:

`resource decline → deliverable wellfield capacity → production shortfall → make-up well trigger → drilling success/cost/timing → restored or residual resource capacity → net generation`

and separately:

`plant degradation/outage → gross/net conversion capability → availability/energy shortfall → overhaul/replacement capex`

Do not use one capacity-factor haircut to conceal both risks if the project has an explicit make-up-well or plant-overhaul plan.

### Geothermal lifecycle and field O&M

Geothermal O&M can include field-specific activities beyond conventional power-plant maintenance, such as:

- well/workover programmes;
- scaling/corrosion management;
- steam/fluid gathering maintenance;
- reservoir monitoring;
- reinjection system maintenance;
- make-up wells; and
- environmental/resource monitoring required by permits or resource management plans.

Where these activities are needed to sustain contracted capacity, classify them as required operating/lifecycle economics rather than optional
upside capex.

### Geothermal PPA tariff structure

A dispatchable geothermal PPA can use different structures. Depending on the executed contract, distinguish:

- single all-in energy tariff;
- energy tariff with take-or-pay/minimum offtake; or
- separate **capacity** and **energy** charges.

Where capacity and energy are separated, the economic logic can be:

- capacity charge linked to **available contracted capacity**, intended to recover fixed/capital obligations, fixed O&M, taxes and sponsor/debt
  capital costs according to the PPA; and
- energy charge linked to **net energy dispatched/delivered**, intended to recover variable operating costs and other specified energy-linked
  items.

Do not make the capacity charge proportional to dispatched MWh if the PPA pays for declared/accepted availability, and do not pay capacity for
unavailable plant merely because nameplate MW exists. Apply any seasonal/period weighting, availability test, indexation, start-up/ancillary
charge and penalty exactly as the PPA requires.

Geothermal typically has no conventional fuel-cost pass-through. Do not import a thermal-fuel energy charge mechanically; model the actual
variable O&M/resource-related components of the geothermal PPA.

### Single-offtaker concentration remains credit risk

A long-term PPA can materially reduce market demand risk but may concentrate almost all project revenue in one utility/offtaker. Capacity or
take-or-pay protection does not remove counterparty payment risk. Reconcile the PPA payment profile to the utility's credit, government support,
letter of credit/liquidity facility or other credit enhancement and its expiry/call mechanics.

### Geothermal capex-scope completeness

Where within project perimeter, cost completeness can require distinct recognition of:

- exploration/test drilling already sunk or still to be funded;
- production/injection wells;
- field roads/pads and drilling mobilisation;
- steam/fluid gathering and separators;
- power island/plant EPC;
- grid interconnection;
- environmental/social and permit requirements;
- owner/project-management costs; and
- future make-up wells/field reinvestment.

Do not import case-study $/MW or well-cost benchmarks as standards. Use them only as external comparables after normalising geology, well depth,
technology, location, scope and price date.



## Battery energy storage systems (BESS) — conditional submodule

Apply this module to stand-alone storage and hybrid solar/wind/storage projects. Do not treat a battery as a generator with a single
capacity-factor assumption.

### Power, energy and duration are separate constraints

Track at least:

- MW charge power;
- MW discharge power;
- usable MWh energy capacity;
- nominal/nameplate MWh;
- minimum/maximum state of charge (SOC);
- duration at rated power;
- charge and discharge efficiencies / round-trip efficiency;
- auxiliary consumption; and
- interconnection/export/import limits.

The battery cannot simultaneously discharge more MW than its inverter/power limit or more MWh than its usable SOC permits.

### SOC continuity is the core physical reconciliation

For each dispatch period:

`closing SOC = opening SOC + charge energy * charge efficiency - discharge energy / discharge efficiency - self-discharge / auxiliary effects as applicable`

subject to minimum/maximum SOC and power limits.

If the model uses one round-trip efficiency factor instead of separate charge/discharge efficiencies, confirm the convention is internally
consistent and does not apply losses twice.

### Revenue stacking requires mutually compatible dispatch

Separate each revenue stream, for example:

- energy arbitrage;
- frequency response / regulation;
- reserve / contingency services;
- capacity or resource-adequacy payment;
- network support / congestion service;
- tolling / contracted availability;
- renewable-shaping / firming;
- demand-charge reduction / behind-the-meter savings; and
- other local market services.

For ancillary services, distinguish **availability/capacity payment** from **activation/response energy payment**. Do not apply both to all
reserved MW unless the market contract actually does so.

A battery MW committed to one service may not be simultaneously available to another. Model service stacking with the actual market
co-optimisation, reservation, SOC-headroom and exclusivity rules. Revenue stacking is not the arithmetic sum of standalone maximum
revenues.

### Degradation is an operating consequence, not only an accounting life

Battery degradation can depend on:

- energy throughput / equivalent full cycles;
- depth of discharge;
- average and extreme SOC;
- charge/discharge C-rate;
- temperature / thermal management;
- calendar ageing; and
- operating strategy.

The financial model should connect dispatch to degradation where material. A strategy that maximizes gross revenue can reduce lifetime
and future available MWh. Conversely, some ancillary-service strategies can earn availability revenue with relatively limited activation
and degradation.

### Augmentation and replacement

If the project is required to maintain contracted MW/MWh through life, model the chosen approach:

`natural degradation -> available MWh -> augmentation / module replacement -> capex + outage -> restored contracted capacity`

Do not assume constant usable capacity for 15-20 years unless the capex/O&M budget includes the required augmentation or the warranty/
contract genuinely transfers that obligation.

### Warranty and performance guarantees are operating constraints

Where OEM warranties specify energy throughput, cycle count, SOC window, temperature, capacity-retention curve or availability, test that
the dispatch strategy stays within those conditions. A merchant optimisation that breaches warranty limits should not simultaneously assume
full warranty recovery.

### Contracted versus merchant storage revenue

Classify each revenue stream by tenor and counterparty:

- fixed/tolling/availability contract;
- indexed contract;
- short-term ancillary-service contract;
- capacity-market commitment; or
- merchant market revenue.

Debt sizing should follow the lender-recognised contracted/merchant case rather than gross stacked base-case revenue. Where a merchant tail
is required to repay debt, test price, volume, saturation/competition and service-rule change.

### Hybrid renewable + BESS interface

For co-located assets, trace:

`renewable gross output -> plant losses / clipping -> charge to BESS or export -> BESS losses -> shared interconnection/export limit -> metered revenue`

Test whether the battery may charge from grid as well as the renewable plant and whether this changes tariff, tax/incentive, certification or
contract eligibility. Do not count renewable MWh once when exported and again when charged/discharged through the battery.

### Storage downside

Relevant combined downside cases can include:

- lower arbitrage spreads;
- ancillary-service price compression / market saturation;
- lower activation revenue;
- higher degradation from dispatch;
- lower initial usable capacity;
- higher augmentation cost;
- warranty exclusion;
- lower round-trip efficiency;
- outage / inverter replacement;
- grid import/export constraint; and
- delayed or adverse market-rule change.

The core contradiction test is whether the revenue case assumes aggressive cycling while the degradation/augmentation case assumes gentle
use.

## Offtaker and support credit

Where bankability depends on utility/offtaker credit, government guarantee, letter of credit or liquidity support,
reconcile the support's amount, duration, calling mechanics and expiry to the PPA payment exposure. A long-term PPA
with a weak counterparty is not equivalent to a sovereign-backed payment obligation merely because tariff is fixed.

## Merchant tail and end-of-support economics

If contracted/support revenue expires before technical asset life, separate the protected period from any merchant
tail. Merchant price, dispatch, imbalance, curtailment and market-access assumptions should begin only when the
contractual support actually ends. Do not capitalise a merchant tail using the contracted tariff.

## Decommissioning / site restoration

Where the project bears decommissioning, recycling/restoration, dismantling or site-reinstatement obligations, connect
them to end-of-life cash flows, security/funding mechanisms and concession/lease termination.

# Energy efficiency / ESCO / distributed-energy service contracting — conditional module

Apply this module where the investment thesis is primarily **avoided energy/water/operating cost**, an energy-service
performance contract, an ESCO/energy-service agreement, a property-linked retrofit, on-site generation for a host facility,
or a distributed-energy transaction whose repayment depends on the host's savings or service payments. Do not apply it
mechanically to a stand-alone utility-scale IPP whose revenue is principally third-party power sales; use the power-generation
module for that asset and this module only for any host-side service/savings interface.

## Start with the economic bargain: avoided cost is the revenue-equivalent

Energy-efficiency economics are unusual because the core benefit is often a **counterfactual cost that will no longer be paid**,
not a new third-party revenue line. Reconstruct the chain:

`baseline utility / operating cost without project -> implemented ECMs / distributed generation -> measured post-project use ->
adjusted counterfactual use without project -> verified avoided energy / water / operating cost -> host net savings ->
ESCO / lender / investor payment -> retained host cash benefit`

Do not model "savings" as an unsupported positive revenue plug. Savings exist only to the extent the pre-project counterfactual,
post-project actual performance, tariff/commodity price and adjustment methodology are credible.

Where the model is used for financing, distinguish at least:

- **engineering energy savings** — reduced kWh, therms/GJ, fuel, water or demand;
- **avoided utility cost** — energy savings translated through the applicable tariff, demand charge and other billing terms;
- **contractual guaranteed savings** — the amount or quantity the ESCO/guarantor is legally required to stand behind;
- **verified savings** — the amount determined under the agreed M&V method for the reporting period;
- **cash available for financing/service payment** — the portion of savings legally and operationally available to make payments; and
- **retained host benefit** — verified/realised savings after financing, ESCO fees, M&V, O&M and other project costs.

These amounts can differ materially. A guarantee is not proof that savings have occurred, and verified energy savings are not
necessarily equal to cash savings if tariffs, demand charges, operating conditions or billing structures change.

## Cost of delay — treat as a counterfactual, not marketing rhetoric

Where an otherwise viable retrofit is deferred for budget, financing or procurement reasons, quantify the supported cost of delay:

`avoidable utility / operating cost during delay + deferred asset-condition / replacement consequence + expired incentive / tariff
opportunity where actually applicable - financing cost avoided by waiting`

The useful question is not "is debt expensive?" but whether waiting destroys more value than the financing or implementation
cost it avoids. Do not assume immediate implementation is always optimal: include mobilization, procurement, lost production,
permit constraints, higher future efficiency, technology replacement, existing asset remaining life and any credible future funding
advantage.

## Project-development maturity: screening is not investment-grade evidence

Classify the technical evidence by its actual maturity rather than by a label alone. A useful progression is:

`portfolio benchmark / utility-bill screen -> walk-through / preliminary audit -> detailed energy survey and system testing ->
investment-grade / capital-decision audit -> design / implementation package -> commissioning -> operating M&V`

The terminology and formal audit levels differ by standard and jurisdiction. The durable rule is that a low-cost benchmark or
walk-through can identify opportunity, but it does **not** by itself support a lender-grade savings guarantee for a complex or
capital-intensive ECM bundle.

For a material retrofit, inspect whether the evidence package contains the appropriate combination of:

- historic utility bills and meter data by energy/water source;
- peak demand as well as consumption where the tariff contains demand charges;
- floor area / production / throughput and relevant intensity metric;
- occupancy, operating hours, shift pattern and seasonal use;
- weather variables where load is weather sensitive;
- building/process changes during the baseline period;
- equipment/system inventory, age, condition and control strategy;
- major O&M practices and maintenance history;
- tenant/lease allocation of utilities and project costs where relevant;
- prior audits, engineering studies and planned capital works;
- on-site measurements, short-term logging or simulation where required by project complexity;
- identified ECMs, costs, savings, interactions and implementation constraints; and
- a documented basis for excluded/rejected ECMs where that affects the selected bundle.

For portfolios, benchmark energy use/intensity can help prioritise sites, but a poor benchmark score is an **opportunity indicator**,
not proof of financeable savings.

## Baseline and adjusted counterfactual

A performance contract or savings-backed loan requires a defensible pre-project baseline. Reconstruct:

`historical meter / invoice data + occupancy / weather / production / operating-hours evidence + material building/process changes ->
baseline model -> agreed adjustment variables -> reporting-period counterfactual without ECMs`

Challenge a simple historical average where the facility's use is materially driven by weather, occupancy, production, operating
hours, building area, process mix or other changing factors.

Where regression, simulation or another statistical baseline is used, review:

- data period and representativeness;
- meter completeness and estimated bills;
- independent variables and causal plausibility;
- treatment of known abnormal periods;
- building/process changes during the baseline;
- residual/error behaviour;
- confidence/precision treatment where used; and
- whether the same methodology can be applied consistently in the reporting period.

An adjusted baseline should answer the counterfactual question: **what would the facility reasonably have consumed in the reporting
period had the ECM not been installed, given the actual reporting-period weather, occupancy, production and agreed adjustments?**

Do not create savings merely by changing the baseline methodology after installation.

## Measurement and verification — savings must be measurable enough for the payment mechanism

Where payments, guarantees or financing depend on savings, identify the governing M&V protocol/plan and translate it into model logic.
The model should distinguish:

`actual reporting-period use -> approved routine/non-routine adjustments -> adjusted baseline counterfactual -> verified quantity saved ->
applicable tariff / agreed valuation method -> verified monetary saving -> guarantee comparison -> payment / true-up / shortfall`

The required M&V accuracy is an economic choice: more metering, submetering, engineering analysis and third-party verification can
increase confidence but also reduces project economics through additional cost. Do not assume the highest possible measurement precision
is automatically optimal; match M&V rigor to the size, risk and payment dependence of the ECM.

Where savings are based on stipulated values rather than continuous measurement, identify which variables are stipulated, why they are
reasonable and which risks therefore remain with the host or ESCO.

Where the contract uses an independent verifier, reflect verifier cost, timing, disputes and delayed settlement if material.

## Energy-price risk and the difference between energy and dollar guarantees

Separate a guarantee of **energy quantity** from a guarantee of **dollar savings**.

- A fixed-energy guarantee transfers performance/consumption risk but may leave commodity/tariff-price risk with the host.
- A fixed-dollar guarantee can transfer more price risk to the ESCO/guarantor, but only within the contract's price floor, escalation,
  exclusions and adjustment rules.
- A shared-savings structure exposes both parties to the measured/verified savings basis and the agreed sharing percentage.

Historic shared-savings structures illustrate an important failure mode: if ESCO remuneration depends on future energy prices, both the
host payment and ESCO recovery can deviate materially from the expected equipment cost. Model the actual price formula rather than
assuming a fixed nominal ESCO fee.

Where a floor price or reference tariff is used for guarantee settlement, keep it distinct from actual market/utility prices and show who
wins or loses when prices fall below or rise above that basis.

## ECM bundle and interactive effects

A facility-wide performance contract can deliberately combine short-payback and long-payback measures. Reconcile each material ECM:

`existing equipment / operating condition -> intervention -> engineering quantity change -> interaction with other systems -> annual
energy / demand / water effect -> O&M / lifecycle effect -> project cost -> M&V treatment -> guarantee contribution`

Do not sum standalone ECM savings if measures interact. Examples include lighting heat reduction affecting HVAC loads, controls changing
hours of operation, chiller or boiler replacement changing pumping/fan loads, and distributed generation changing imported demand charges.

Where the project is packaged to achieve budget neutrality or a required overall payback/coverage, test that no ECM with negative standalone
or incremental value is hidden inside the bundle without an explicit strategic, compliance, resilience or lifecycle rationale.

## Commissioning is a financial control

Commissioning is not merely a technical close-out item where project cash depends on achieved performance. Link:

`installation complete -> functional / performance testing -> defects / punch list -> commissioning acceptance -> operating handover ->
M&V baseline/reporting start -> payment / guarantee commencement`

A model that starts full savings at physical installation while commissioning, controls tuning or staff handover continues can overstate
cash savings and lender coverage.

Where the contract requires re-commissioning or ongoing tuning, reflect those costs and their expected effect on savings persistence.

## O&M capability and savings persistence

Energy savings can erode if new systems are poorly maintained, overridden or operated incorrectly. Identify who owns the O&M obligation:

- ESCO / service provider;
- host facilities team;
- equipment OEM;
- third-party O&M contractor; or
- shared responsibility.

If the host assumes O&M after training, review staffing, competence, spares, controls access, preventive maintenance and budget capacity.
If the ESCO guarantees savings but the host controls O&M or operating hours, identify host-caused exclusions/cure rights and the resulting
allocation of risk.

For long contracts, distinguish manufacturer warranty life, ESCO performance guarantee life and actual asset useful life.

## Project Development Agreement / feasibility-development economics

Where an ESCO or developer incurs material pre-contract engineering and intellectual-capital cost, a Project Development Agreement (PDA)
or equivalent may define:

- site/data access and customer cooperation;
- scope and due date of detailed evaluation;
- ECM list and operating/O&M recommendations;
- estimated savings and implementation cost;
- required financing / budget-neutral criteria;
- development fee or reimbursement if the customer does not proceed;
- treatment of development cost if the full project proceeds;
- ownership/use of studies and work product;
- exclusivity or non-circumvention where applicable; and
- termination / dispute provisions.

Do not assume development cost is free simply because it is capitalised into the later project. If the project is cancelled, determine who
bears sunk development cost and whether any fee is legally payable.

## Performance-contract compensation structures

Identify the actual form of ESCO compensation. Common structures can include:

- guaranteed fixed-dollar savings;
- guaranteed energy-quantity savings;
- fixed shared savings;
- true/variable shared savings;
- service fee plus guarantee;
- hybrid guaranteed/shared structures; and
- host-owned equipment with third-party finance plus a separate performance guarantee.

For each structure, reconcile:

`verified saving / stipulated saving -> guaranteed amount -> ESCO fee / share -> financing payment -> shortfall cure -> host retained saving`

Do not treat the ESCO's share as both an operating cost and a debt service item unless the contract actually contains both obligations.

## Savings shortfall and cure mechanics

Where actual/verified savings fall below contractual guarantee, model the actual cure hierarchy. Possible remedies may include:

- cash shortfall payment;
- additional ECM / remedial works;
- service credit;
- savings buy-out;
- extension of the service/guarantee term;
- revised operational measures; or
- termination/default remedies.

Do not assume a shortfall is automatically paid in cash in the same period if the contract permits remediation, dispute, carry-forward or
term extension first.

## Payment commencement, acceptance and possession

Energy-service contracts can attach different consequences to installation completion, possession/beneficial use, acceptance and successful
performance verification. Trace which event triggers:

- title transfer;
- warranty start;
- savings guarantee start;
- service fee;
- financing/payment obligation; and
- termination value.

Do not assume "acceptance" and "payment commencement" are the same event unless the contract says so.

## Financing form follows risk allocation — do not choose by interest rate alone

For an energy-efficiency or distributed-energy transaction, the relevant commercial choice is not merely the lowest quoted interest rate.
Compare structures by:

- payment term and amortisation;
- fixed versus floating price/rate;
- upfront/down payment;
- balloon/residual exposure;
- collateral and guarantees;
- ownership and asset-removal rights;
- tax/accounting treatment under the **current** applicable rules;
- host covenant / debt-capacity impact;
- payment flexibility and termination value;
- who carries performance and maintenance risk;
- whether the payment can legally be passed through to tenants/users; and
- whether project savings remain positive after all financing/service costs.

Legacy labels such as "operating lease", "true lease", "capital lease" or "off-balance-sheet finance" do not determine current accounting
outcome. Review the actual contract and current accounting/tax rules.

## Cash purchase / host debt / lease / service agreement — economic boundaries

Distinguish at least four economic archetypes:

1. **Host-owned / internally funded** — host pays capex and retains all savings/performance risk.
2. **Host-owned / debt or finance lease** — lender finances asset; host retains performance/O&M risk unless separately guaranteed.
3. **Third-party asset ownership / lease** — host buys use of equipment; lessor retains specified ownership/residual risks.
4. **Energy/service performance contract** — host buys a service or output, with some or all performance risk transferred to the ESCO/SPV.

A service contract can itself be financed with debt, lease or bond capital. Do not classify the economic bargain solely from the financing
instrument used underneath it.

## Managed Energy Service Agreement (MESA) / utility-bill service structures

Where a managed energy service agreement or similar structure is used, reconstruct:

`historic / agreed energy-service baseline -> customer service charge -> provider pays utility -> provider funds ECMs -> actual utility cost ->
provider gross savings margin -> O&M / financing / M&V -> provider return`

This structure can address split incentives where service charges are passed to tenants similarly to utility costs, but only if the leases,
service contract and applicable law permit the pass-through. Do not assume tenants automatically fund landlord retrofit capex.

If the provider directly pays the utility bill, distinguish utility credit/payment obligations from customer service-payment obligations.

## Property-linked / PACE-type financing — conditional and jurisdiction-specific

Where a current programme legally allows project repayment through a property assessment, tax lien or analogous property-linked charge,
model the **actual** programme mechanics rather than historical U.S. PACE assumptions. Relevant questions include:

- enabling law and eligible property/project type;
- assessment amount, tenor, rate and priority;
- whether the obligation runs with the property on sale;
- mortgagee / senior lender consent;
- acceleration or non-acceleration on default;
- collection mechanism and remittance to investor;
- foreclosure / enforcement pathway;
- tenant pass-through rights under actual leases;
- transfer on ownership change;
- programme administrator fees;
- construction draw and completion requirements;
- M&V / energy-audit conditions; and
- any reserve, guarantee, insurance or public credit enhancement.

Where funding is pooled or securitised, distinguish:

- direct/open-market investor placement;
- warehouse / line-of-credit funding pending aggregation;
- bond/pool issuance after projects reach scale; and
- project-by-project owner-arranged finance.

The aggregation period can create funding, rate-lock and pipeline-volume risk. Do not assume the warehouse can be refinanced or securitised
at the forecast rate/date merely because the programme intends to do so.

## Mortgage and property-value interaction

A property-linked retrofit can protect or improve collateral by replacing obsolete equipment and lowering operating cost, but it can also
create a senior or competing assessment/lien. Review the existing mortgage documents, lender consent and default waterfall.

Do not automatically capitalise energy savings into property value. Where a real-estate valuation case includes value uplift, bridge:

`verified sustainable NOI reduction / other cash benefit -> market-supported capitalisation or DCF treatment -> transaction value`

and test whether the benefit is already captured in NOI, rent, occupancy, terminal value or another model line. Avoid double counting.

## Split incentives and tenant economics

For leased property, identify who:

- owns the building/equipment;
- pays utility charges;
- funds the retrofit;
- receives the savings;
- pays any property assessment or service charge;
- controls equipment operation; and
- owns residual/termination rights.

A landlord-funded retrofit with tenant-paid utilities can have weak owner economics even if whole-building savings are attractive. Conversely,
a service/assessment charge can shift cost to tenants only if lease terms and law permit it and tenant total occupancy cost remains acceptable.

## Lender underwriting — six risk categories for energy-service transactions

For savings-backed or distributed-generation debt, separately assess:

1. **Obligor credit risk** — ability and willingness of host/offtaker to make payments through the full term.
2. **Construction / installation risk** — completion on time, on budget and to acceptance/commissioning standard.
3. **Counterparty risk** — ESCO/EPC/O&M/guarantor solvency and capability.
4. **Technology risk** — reliability, maturity, O&M intensity, fuel/input needs and accepted technical standards.
5. **Performance risk** — whether payment/debt service falls if savings/output underperforms.
6. **Structural risk** — whether contracts actually allocate, secure and monetise the intended payment obligations.

Do not collapse these risks into one "ESCO credit" assumption.

## Credit risk versus performance risk

A high-value lender question is whether the lender is intended to take **obligor credit risk**, **project performance risk**, or both.

Where the end-user payment is unconditional despite equipment performance, the lender may principally underwrite customer credit while the
customer pursues ESCO remedies separately. Where payment is conditional on output/savings, the lender is exposed to performance unless a
creditworthy ESCO, surety, reserve, insurance or other mechanism covers the shortfall.

The model must reproduce this separation. Do not show unconditional debt service cash if the customer can suspend payment for poor
performance and no third party covers that suspension.

## Construction-period funding and completion protection

For financed retrofit/DG projects, reconcile:

`approved works / invoices -> draw request -> lien waiver / title evidence -> lender / escrow disbursement -> installed asset -> commissioning /
acceptance -> permanent payment commencement`

Depending on structure, completion protection may include ESCO/contractor recourse, parent guarantee, cash/LC collateral, payment/performance
bond, escrow control or lender step-in rights. Apply only executed protections and their caps/expiry.

## Payment logistics, lockbox and waterfall

Where customer payments combine debt service, ESCO/O&M fee and reserve funding, identify who bills, collects and allocates cash. A lender-
controlled lockbox may apply a waterfall such as:

`customer payment -> senior debt service -> required O&M / contractor amount -> required reserves -> subordinated / equity`

Use the actual contractual order. Do not count the gross customer payment as unrestricted SPV cash before senior allocations.

## Title, fixtures, security and removal rights

Distributed-energy and building equipment can become physically integrated into real property. Review whether project assets are legally
personal property, fixtures or part of the real estate and how this affects:

- lender security perfection;
- mortgagee priority/consent;
- mechanics/contractor liens;
- access and inspection;
- removal rights on default;
- roof/building reinstatement; and
- residual value.

Do not assume recoverable collateral value if removal is impractical, damages the host property, violates a site lease or has no secondary market.

## Early termination / make-whole

Where the host can terminate early, reconcile notice window, termination date and termination amount. Financing may require a pre-agreed
termination schedule or formula sufficient to repay outstanding principal plus contractual break/make-whole amounts.

For performance default, identify which party ultimately bears the termination payment. Do not assume the host always pays if the service
contract passes the liability to an ESCO/guarantor, and do not assume the ESCO pays if its liability is capped below the financing termination
value.

## Assignment and transfer

Lender/investor ability to assign financing can be important to liquidity, while host or service-provider assignment may be restricted because
credit and technical capability were underwritten on named counterparties. Reconcile:

- lender assignment rights;
- host change of control / site sale;
- ESCO/O&M replacement rights;
- site lease transfer;
- property assessment transfer; and
- consent requirements.

A financing assumed saleable in a secondary market but contractually non-assignable is a contradiction.

## Energy-savings insurance / guarantee credit enhancement

Where an insurer or guarantor backstops savings, model:

- insured/guaranteed savings definition;
- deductible / attachment point;
- cap and term;
- premium and payment timing;
- exclusions;
- claim evidence/M&V requirement;
- insurer/guarantor credit;
- waiting/dispute period; and
- recovery interaction with ESCO/EPC warranties.

Do not double count full recovery from ESCO guarantee, insurance and equipment warranty for the same shortfall.

## Solar / on-site generation PPA — host-side economic lens

For an on-site solar PPA or similar third-party-owned generation service, map at least:

`host / site owner <-> PPA sponsor / project SPE <-> EPC contractor <-> utility / interconnection / incentive bodies <-> debt / tax / sponsor equity`

The host is purchasing **energy/output**, not construction. The sponsor/SPE typically bears the construction, project financing, O&M and
incentive monetisation responsibilities, subject to the actual contract.

Host economics should bridge:

`on-site metered generation -> avoided utility purchases / demand effects -> host avoided utility cost - PPA payment -> host net saving`

Sponsor economics should separately bridge:

`PPA output revenue + current eligible incentives / environmental attributes + tax benefits where applicable - EPC/development cost - O&M -
inverter/major replacement - insurance/tax/site costs - financing -> sponsor/tax-equity return`

Do not use an old tax-credit percentage or incentive rate from a historical case study. Use current programme eligibility and the actual owner
entitled to claim each benefit.

## PPA escalation versus utility-price hedge

A host may accept a long-term PPA to obtain lower current power cost, price certainty or both. Compare the PPA tariff/escalator to the avoided
utility tariff by the **actual rate components** affected by on-site generation.

A flat comparison of PPA $/kWh to average utility $/kWh can misstate savings if demand charges, time-of-use prices, minimum bills, standby
charges, export credits, net-metering limits or other tariff items are material.

Where the PPA escalates annually, test host savings under low utility-price inflation as well as high inflation. A PPA can become above-market
even while the project remains technically successful.

## Solar site and physical constraints

For roof, ground or canopy projects, link site design to energy and financial output. Relevant constraints can include:

- usable roof/land/parking area;
- shading, orientation and tilt;
- roof age and remaining life;
- roof penetration/warranty requirements;
- structural loading;
- civil/soil/grading works;
- alternative use / opportunity cost of land or parking;
- cable distance to the interconnection point;
- fire/building/environmental permits;
- construction access and site disruption; and
- long-term O&M access.

A 20+ year PPA on a roof expected to require replacement during the term needs explicit removal/reinstallation or roof-renewal economics.

## Solar generation and lifecycle

For on-site PV, reconcile:

`DC installed capacity -> irradiation/resource -> gross output -> temperature/system losses -> degradation -> availability -> inverter / electrical
losses -> curtailment / export limits -> metered on-site/export energy`

Then connect long-term O&M, cleaning where required, insurance, inverter/major component replacement and end-of-term purchase/removal/site
restoration. Do not treat module warranty life as proof that all system components have the same useful life.

## Incentives, rebates and environmental attributes

Energy-efficiency and distributed-generation projects can depend on grants, rebates, tax benefits, renewable/environmental certificates,
utility incentives or special financing programmes. For every material support item identify:

- legal/programme source and current eligibility;
- applicant/recipient;
- application milestone and award status;
- amount/basis;
- performance or commissioning conditions;
- payment timing;
- expiry / budget cap / programme risk;
- tax treatment if material; and
- clawback/recapture conditions.

Do not treat an available programme as committed project cash before the required approval/award has occurred.

## Ancillary benefits — include only evidence-backed incremental cash/value

Energy projects can produce benefits beyond utility savings, including:

- lower maintenance material consumption;
- lower maintenance labour;
- deferred or avoided replacement capex;
- deferred procurement/administrative cost;
- avoided carbon/REC/environmental compliance purchases where actually incurred;
- improved property NOI/value where market evidence supports it;
- water savings;
- resilience or productivity benefits where specifically evidenced; and
- avoided regulatory penalties/charges where applicable.

Do not apply a generic percentage uplift to energy savings. Quantify each benefit separately, prove that it is incremental, and prevent double
counting with utility savings, O&M, lifecycle capex, property valuation, insurance or ESG assumptions. Soft benefits can be reported qualitatively
where they cannot be measured reliably.

The historical claim that announcing an energy project produces a predictable stock-price uplift is not a model input or valuation rule.

## Energy-efficiency project barriers as model-review prompts

Where a seemingly attractive project remains unfunded or unimplemented, test whether the model omits a real constraint such as:

- capital-budget competition;
- host credit/covenant capacity;
- lack of collateral or removability of installed equipment;
- split landlord/tenant incentives;
- short remaining tenant lease term;
- inability of property/facility manager to bind the owner;
- mortgagee consent;
- uncertain savings / weak M&V;
- inadequate maintenance capability;
- interconnection / utility approval;
- regulatory/incentive eligibility; or
- development/transaction cost disproportionate to project size.

The absence of up-front cash is not the same as the absence of economic value, but neither does positive engineering NPV guarantee a bankable
transaction.

# District energy / heat networks — conditional module

## Energy balance and demand bridge

Where a heat/cooling network is material, reconcile:

`connected load → hourly/seasonal demand → diversity/peak demand → network losses → generation requirement →
primary/backup plant dispatch → fuel/electricity input`

Annual heat sold alone can hide peak-capacity, seasonal commissioning and backup-plant requirements. Technical model
outputs should reconcile to the financial model's saleable heat and input-energy assumptions.

## Connection ramp-up and customer commitments

Separate:

- network passed/available buildings;
- contracted customers / anchor loads;
- signed versus heads-of-terms commitments;
- connection date;
- connected capacity;
- actual heat offtake; and
- take-or-pay/minimum volume if applicable.

Do not treat an addressable pipeline as contracted demand.

## Tariff and commodity/index mismatch

Reconcile heat tariff/fixed-charge/indexation to fuel/electricity, carbon and other variable input exposure. Where
customer price formulas and input costs use different indices or reset dates, model the retained basis risk rather
than assuming automatic pass-through.

## Network loss, plant efficiency and backup economics

Connect distribution losses, plant efficiency/COP, auxiliary electricity, backup/peaking plant and outages to fuel/
electricity cost and saleable heat. A static margin per MWh can conceal deteriorating physical efficiency.

## Metering, billing and collection

Separate metered heat delivered, billed revenue, customer credits/adjustments and cash collection. Where the project
includes retail/customer-interface responsibilities, customer churn/default and billing performance can be distinct
from physical heat demand.

# Regulated utility / network infrastructure — conditional module

Apply only where the project's economics are governed by a regulator or statutory revenue/price-control
framework. Do not impose a RAB model on a simple concession with contractual tariffs.

## Regulatory asset base / RAV roll-forward

Where a RAB/RAV framework applies, reconcile the regulator's own definition, conceptually:

`opening regulatory asset base + eligible/capitalised additions - regulatory depreciation - disposals +/- regulatory adjustments → closing regulatory asset base`

Accounting PPE is not automatically equal to RAB/RAV. Timing, eligibility, indexation, capitalisation and
regulatory depreciation can differ.

## Allowed revenue building blocks

A regulated revenue model may include some combination of:

- allowed return on RAB/RAV;
- regulatory depreciation;
- efficient/allowed opex;
- tax allowance;
- incentives/rewards/penalties;
- pass-through costs;
- prior-period true-ups;
- inflation/indexation; and
- other regulator-specific adjustments.

Use the actual regulatory determination, not a generic WACC or accounting-cost build.

## Revenue cap, price cap and volume exposure

Regulatory regimes differ in how volume changes affect revenue. A revenue-cap utility may have limited
long-run volume risk but timing/cash-flow true-ups; a price-cap regime may leave more volume exposure.

Do not infer demand risk from physical throughput without understanding the revenue-control mechanism.

## Regulatory incentives, outputs and true-ups

Where outputs/service quality drive rewards or penalties, translate them into the financial model. Likewise,
