where overs/underspend, inflation, pass-through items or revenue deviations are recovered in later periods,
model the lag and adjustment rather than treating every year's accounting revenue as final economic recovery.

## Reopeners and uncertainty mechanisms

Major regulated networks can have reopener, uncertainty, volume-driver or cost-pass-through mechanisms.
Where present, distinguish:

- base allowed expenditure;
- trigger condition;
- regulator assessment/approval;
- recovery timing; and
- sharing/disallowance risk.

Do not assume every actual capex dollar automatically enters RAB or earns a return.

# Social infrastructure — conditional module

Typical assets include hospitals, schools, justice/corrections, civic facilities and similar long-term service
assets.

## Availability payment as primary revenue

In a conventional social-infrastructure availability PPP, payment is normally linked to facilities/services being
available to the contracted standard, not to the number of patients, students, prisoners or staff using the
facility. End-user demand often remains primarily a public-sector risk unless the specific contract says
otherwise.

Do not model revenue as occupancy × tariff simply because occupancy is an operational KPI.

## Availability units and criticality

Where the contract uses areas/service units, deductions may vary by:

- affected area;
- criticality;
- duration;
- time of day;
- alternative accommodation; and
- repeat failure.

A small outage in a critical operating theatre, secure cell block or essential school service can have a different
payment effect from the same floor area of noncritical space.

## Soft services

Where cleaning, catering, security, grounds maintenance, waste or other soft services are included, test whether
costs are:

- fixed;
- volume driven;
- pass-through;
- benchmarked; or
- market-tested at intervals.

Long-dated fixed-price assumptions can be wrong if the contract periodically resets soft-service pricing.

## Lifecycle and handback

Social infrastructure often combines long concession terms with component lives shorter than the term.
Lifecycle renewal and final residual-condition obligations are therefore central to the economics. Do not allow
maintenance/lifecycle spend to collapse near expiry if the handback standard requires usable residual life.



## Hospital / health-service PPP — conditional specialist module

A hospital PPP can be a conventional availability-financed facility, a management contract, an outsourced clinical/ancillary
service, a joint venture, a revenue/profit-sharing enterprise or a hybrid. **Classify the scope and payment regime before applying
hospital operating drivers.** Do not assume every hospital PPP is project-financed or that every patient creates private-party
revenue.

### Clinical, administrative, facility and ancillary scope boundary

Map which party is responsible for each material function. Depending on the arrangement these can include:

- hospital building and lifecycle;
- medical equipment ownership, maintenance and replacement;
- clinical staff and clinical governance;
- nursing and allied health;
- general administration and finance;
- pharmacy, laboratory, imaging and other diagnostics;
- laundry, catering, cleaning, security, logistics and waste;
- IT / records systems;
- marketing / community engagement;
- procurement and inventory; and
- payer billing, claims and collection.

The general-management and clinical-management systems interact. An administrative cost saving that causes drug stock-outs,
staff shortages, equipment downtime or delayed treatment is not a free efficiency gain. Conversely, the financial model should
not charge the private party for clinical or staffing obligations retained by government.

### Need, catchment, demand and service mix

For a demand- or revenue-exposed health-service model, connect health-service need to actual activity rather than applying one
occupancy percentage to all services. Relevant drivers can include:

`catchment population + demographics + disease / referral profile + competing supply + insurance coverage + willingness/ability to pay`
`→ inpatient / outpatient / diagnostic / procedure demand`
`→ capacity, length of stay and utilisation`
`→ payer / case mix`
`→ billable service and cash collection`

Potential evidence includes historic admissions/outpatients, bed occupancy, average length of stay, referral patterns, disease
profile, population growth, competing public/private facilities, service gaps and payer enrolment. A population-bed ratio is an
input to capacity planning, not proof of revenue.

Where the PPP is a pure availability facility, patient demand may drive staffing intensity, utilities, consumables and wear without
directly driving the service payment. Preserve that revenue-risk boundary.

### Payer mix, social-health-insurer reimbursement and working capital

Hospital cash flow can depend materially on **who pays** and when. Separate, as applicable:

- direct/self-pay cash;
- public or private health-insurance reimbursement;
- government budget/service payment;
- grants/subsidies;
- employer or contracted-provider payments; and
- bad debt / charity / uncompensated care.

For insurer or government claims, model claim eligibility, case/rate basis, submission, rejection/return rate, approval and cash
lag where material. Revenue recognition is not cash collection. A high insured-patient share can improve credit quality while
increasing receivables if reimbursement is slow.

Working-capital drivers can include receivables, drug and medical-supply inventory, payables, payroll and other operating
balances. Do not import the guidebook's illustrative collection days or balance-sheet percentages; use current payer and
operator evidence.

### Hospital payment / compensation structures

A health-management PPP can compensate the private party through different mechanisms, including a fixed management fee,
reimbursement of eligible cost, activity/case payments, mark-up, revenue share, profit share, availability/service payment or a
hybrid. Apply the actual contract.

For a revenue/profit-sharing model, define the share **after** determining:

- eligible gross revenue and payer categories;
- refunds, bad debts and claim denials;
- eligible operating cost and related-party charges;
- capital/equipment cost and lifecycle treatment;
- management fee or mark-up if separate;
- taxes; and
- timing of settlement and audit/true-up.

Do not simultaneously model mutually exclusive bid compensation options. Where a management fee and profit/revenue share
both exist, prove the contract expressly provides both and that the same economics are not counted twice.

### Licensing, accreditation and service eligibility

Licences, hospital accreditation, specialty accreditation, insurer accreditation and professional staffing requirements can be
conditions to operating, billing or receiving particular reimbursements. For each material condition identify:

`requirement → responsible party → renewal / compliance date → service or revenue affected → cure / penalty / closure consequence`

A facility cannot earn fully reimbursable service revenue during a period in which the required accreditation is absent unless the
payer rules allow it. Likewise, licence renewal cost and compliance capex/opex should not disappear from a long-dated model.

### Medical equipment availability and lifecycle

Medical equipment can have a shorter economic/technology life than the building and can be critical to both service quality and
revenue. Where within the private scope, connect equipment fleet to:

- service volumes and capacity;
- maintenance and downtime;
- consumables;
- calibration / regulatory requirements;
- replacement and technology refresh;
- insurer/reimbursement eligibility where relevant; and
- performance deductions or lost activity.

Do not use building lifecycle as a proxy for medical-equipment renewal.

### Staffing, transition and workforce economics

A management or outsourcing PPP can transfer, redeploy or change the employment basis of existing public staff. If the
transition is within scope, reconcile:

`current staff / role → retained / transferred / redeployed / retired / rehired → transition cost → steady-state staffing and payroll`

Potential economic items can include severance/retirement, recruitment, training, duplicated transition payroll, agency/locum
cost, pension or public-service obligations where applicable and capacity development. Do not retain the full public payroll while
also adding a full private replacement workforce unless both actually remain.

Staffing should respond to activity and mandated ratios where relevant, but do not hard-code a generic staff-per-bed metric. The
actual clinical model, licence, labour rules and service standard control.

### Contract performance, quality and monitoring

Hospital PPP monitoring commonly spans both general-management and clinical-service dimensions. Only metrics connected to
contractual payment, required remediation, default, funding, licence/accreditation or management decisions need financial-model
consequences. Depending on scope, material metrics can include:

- service availability / waiting time / access;
- staffing and vacancy;
- equipment and drug/supply availability;
- infection/safety outcomes;
- referral or continuity-of-care failures;
- patient complaints and service quality;
- financial reporting / claims / audit compliance; and
- facility condition and regulatory compliance.

Trace the actual mechanism:

`measured indicator / evidence → threshold → cure or service action → payment adjustment / cost / default / accreditation effect`

Do not manufacture a financial penalty merely because a clinical KPI exists. Conversely, if the contract pays for performance or
requires a corrective action, the KPI cannot remain a dashboard statistic with no economic consequence.

### Hospital system versus single-facility economics

Where a PPP covers a network or hospital system, model inter-facility economics rather than summing identical standalone
hospitals. Potential system effects include shared procurement, central diagnostics, referral patterns, case-mix reallocation,
management overhead, transport/logistics and capacity balancing. These can create genuine scale economies, but they can also
move activity and revenue between facilities.

Prevent internal referrals or transfers from being counted as new system demand. Where one facility closes or changes service
level, reflect the receiving facility's capacity and variable cost.

### Public-health outcome versus private financial viability

Health outcomes, access and affordability can justify a public intervention even when the private project alone is not user-fee
self-financing. Keep the concepts separate:

`public-health need / outcome` is not automatically `private billable demand`.

Where the authority uses subsidy, insurance reimbursement or service payments to make the model viable, preserve the actual
funding source, eligibility and affordability. Do not solve a health-access gap by assuming unaffordable patient charges simply to
hit an investor return.

## Social / affordable and student housing — conditional infrastructure-project-finance boundary

Apply this subsection only where the housing project is being reviewed as social infrastructure, a public/PPP programme or a
project-financed operating asset; general property-development modelling belongs to the property specialist skill.

Classify the economic model first:

- development and sale;
- long-term rental / availability-supported housing;
- mixed tenure;
- student accommodation; or
- staged sell/hold structure.

For long-term rental or student-housing models, separate addressable beds/units from available units, occupied units, rent/fee,
concessions/arrears and collected cash. For a public availability structure, occupancy may affect cost/service need without driving
private revenue. For a development-and-sale structure, do not continue to earn rental income on units already sold unless a genuine
leaseback/management right remains.

Where both project-level and holdco debt are used, do not use holdco borrowings to make the project SPV appear more bankable.
Trace distributions available to service holdco debt after project covenants and reserves, and model failed-refinancing or cash-trap
risk at the correct legal level.



# Real estate / development finance — conditional specialist module

Apply this module to land subdivision, residential, industrial/logistics, office, retail, hospitality, mixed-use, build-to-rent,
student accommodation and other property developments where land value, development cost, sales/lease-up and property finance are
material. For social-housing PPPs, combine this module with the social-infrastructure payment/risk module where appropriate.

## Classify the property strategy before reviewing returns

At minimum distinguish:

- land acquisition / land banking / entitlement;
- development and sale;
- development and staged sale;
- build-to-rent / build-to-hold;
- develop-to-core then refinance;
- mixed sell/hold;
- refurbishment / repositioning; and
- income-producing acquisition with limited development works.

The model perimeter determines whether the principal value driver is **sales proceeds, stabilized NOI, residual land value, rental cash
flow, refinance proceeds or a combination**. Do not use an investment-property exit cap rate to justify a develop-and-sell model that
never holds stabilized NOI, or sales GDV to create terminal value for units already sold.

## Development-stage risk migrates over time

Reconstruct the project by stage:

`site control -> due diligence -> acquisition -> planning / entitlement -> design -> procurement -> construction -> completion -> sales / leasing -> stabilization -> refinance / sale / hold`

For each stage identify the major remaining risks and whether the model changes them when the milestone is achieved. Examples:

- before planning: entitlement/use/density risk;
- after planning but before fixed-price procurement: design and construction-pricing risk;
- during construction: completion, cost-to-complete and programme risk;
- after completion: absorption, tenant credit, concessions and stabilization risk; and
- after stabilization: valuation, rent-growth, cap-rate and refinancing risk.

Do not use one static development hurdle or contingency as a substitute for this risk migration.

## Site control, land basis and optionality

Identify whether the developer has freehold title, an option, conditional contract, long settlement, ground lease, joint venture, vendor
finance or other site-control structure. Model:

- option/premium/deposit timing;
- purchase price and acquisition costs;
- conditions precedent and long-stop dates;
- extension fees;
- forfeiture/cancellation exposure;
- planning-condition interaction;
- vendor/landowner participation or overage; and
- stamp duty / transfer tax / GST/VAT treatment where applicable.

Land control is not the same as land ownership and should not be treated as fully funded land on the balance sheet before the legal and
cash event occurs.

Where alternative uses, densities, staging or timing choices are credible, recognise **optionality**. Do not add a separate 'option
value' cash flow unless the valuation method supports it; instead test whether the land valuation and downside scenarios reflect the
real choices available.

## Development valuation and development appraisal are different questions

A **valuation** asks what the development property/land is worth on the applicable basis of value. A **development appraisal** asks
whether a specific scheme is profitable/viable for the developer or lender. They may use similar residual cash flows but should not be
silently conflated.

### Gross and net development value

For sale developments:

`GDV = aggregate expected market value / sale proceeds of completed units or components`

`NDV = GDV less selling/disposal costs where that convention is used`

For income-producing developments, the end value should be derived from supported stabilized income and the appropriate valuation
method, rather than by relabelling annual rent as GDV.

Trace every material GDV component to unit count/area, product mix, price/rate, incentives/concessions, escalation and settlement or
valuation date.

### Residual land value

A residual appraisal typically solves one of two questions:

`residual land value = development value - development costs - required developer return`

or

`residual developer return = development value - land - development costs`

In a timed DCF, the timing of each inflow/outflow and financing/discounting changes the answer materially. The residual output should
not be treated as a directly observed market value. Cross-check it against relevant land transactions, alternative use and market
behaviour where credible evidence exists.

### Static residual versus DCF

A static residual can be a useful screening method but can conceal development duration and financing carry. For multi-year, staged or
complex developments, use a timed cash-flow approach sufficiently granular to capture:

- land settlement;
- construction progress;
- professional and statutory fees;
- presales/settlements or lease-up;
- financing draw and interest;
- taxes;
- sales/leasing costs; and
- exit/refinance timing.

If a static margin and a DCF IRR point to different conclusions, investigate the timing rather than choosing the preferred metric.

## Developer-return metrics are not interchangeable

At minimum distinguish:

- **profit on cost (PoC)** = development profit / total development cost on the defined convention;
- **profit on value (PoV)** = development profit / development value on the defined convention;
- **project IRR** = time-weighted return on project cash flow before/after finance as explicitly defined;
- **equity IRR** = return on sponsor equity cash flows; and
- **equity multiple / MOIC**.

Cash margins ignore time. The same PoC/PoV applied to projects with different duration, staging or land timing can imply materially
different IRRs. Conversely, a high annualised IRR on a short project can coexist with a modest absolute margin. Never compare a PoC
hurdle directly to an equity IRR hurdle as though they are the same measure.

Where a developer quotes a hurdle, identify whether it is a **market input for land valuation**, an internal go/no-go return, a planning-
viability convention, a lender requirement or an observed achieved return. These are different concepts.

## Development cost stack

The model should identify all material costs appropriate to the asset and jurisdiction, including where relevant:

- site acquisition, transfer taxes/duties and legal costs;
- demolition, contamination/remediation and enabling works;
- hard construction and fit-out;
- preliminaries, general conditions and contractor margin;
- consultants, design, engineering, project management and quantity surveying;
- authority contributions, infrastructure levies, planning obligations and permit fees;
- utility, road, public-realm and off-site works;
- developer overhead / management fee where genuinely incremental;
- marketing, sales commissions and settlement costs;
- tenant incentives, tenant improvements and leasing commissions;
- rates/taxes/insurance/security/maintenance during construction and lease-up;
- finance arrangement/commitment/legal/valuation/monitoring fees;
- interest and other funding carry;
- contingency and risk allowances; and
- post-completion defects, retention, warranty and close-out costs.

Do not hide a known scope item inside a generic contingency merely to make the development margin appear stable.

## Cost timing, inflation and financing carry

Development value is highly sensitive to **when** costs and receipts occur. Model construction cost through a credible programme or
cash-flow curve, with separate treatment for fixed-price contracted work versus exposed/escalating work. Finance carry should follow
actual draw timing, not average total cost times an annual interest rate.

Where lender interest is capitalised or funded from an interest reserve, distinguish:

`interest expense -> cash interest paid / capitalised -> reserve draw / loan draw -> outstanding debt -> remaining reserve`

An interest reserve is liquidity, not economic income and not proof that the project can service debt after stabilization.

## Development facility sizing and sources & uses

For an acquisition/development/construction (ADC) facility, identify the binding sizing constraints such as:

- facility commitment;
- LTC on eligible cost;
- LTV on as-is / as-complete / as-stabilized value, as contractually defined;
- lender-recognised contingency;
- interest reserve;
- minimum equity contribution;
- presale/prelease or other conditions; and
- cost-to-complete / value-to-complete tests.

Do not calculate debt solely as a percentage of total development cost if the actual facility is constrained by multiple tests.

### Equity funding sequence

Follow the actual loan agreement: equity may be funded first, pari passu, pro rata, through a minimum equity balance or by another
agreed sequence. A lender requirement for sponsor equity to be injected before debt is a **risk-control mechanism**, not a generic
modelling preference.

## Cost-to-complete and remaining-funding test

During construction, continually test:

`remaining committed debt + undrawn committed equity + unrestricted project cash + committed recoveries`

against

`remaining hard cost + soft cost + contingency required + finance cost + interest reserve need + taxes/fees + completion/lease-up cost`

Use current actual spend, committed contracts, approved variations, claims and latest forecast to complete. A project is not fully funded
merely because original sources equalled original uses at financial close.

If remaining funding is deficient, identify the contractual cure: additional sponsor equity, guarantor support, facility increase, scope
change, asset sale, claim recovery or default/workout. Do not plug the gap with higher future sales.

## Developer / sponsor underwriting

Where the model supports a lender decision, review the developer separately from the project. Relevant domain evidence includes:

- completed-project track record by product and geography;
- experience with similar scale/complexity;
- financial capacity and liquidity;
- current portfolio and competing equity/capital calls;
- contingent liabilities and guarantees;
- contractor and consultant relationships;
- history of cost/schedule performance;
- ability and willingness to fund overruns; and
- reputation for completing projects through weak markets.

A high-value site is not a substitute for a sponsor capable of completing it.

## Construction contract and draw administration

Trace the lender's control chain:

`approved budget -> executed contracts -> progress / quantity-surveyor or inspector certification -> borrower equity requirement -> lender draw -> payment to contractor -> updated cost-to-complete`

Where applicable, test retainage, lien/claim controls, change-order approval, stored materials, contingency usage and confirmation that
property taxes/insurance/critical suppliers are current. Draw controls should prevent loan proceeds from funding costs outside the
approved project or ahead of actual work.

## Presales, preleasing and absorption

Presales/preleasing are only credit support to the extent they are **binding and economically credible**.

For unit sales, distinguish:

`reservation / expression of interest -> signed contract -> deposit received -> unconditional/finance-approved contract -> settlement -> cash collected`

Model cancellation, default, sunset clauses, finance conditions, settlement delay and deposit treatment where material. Do not count a
reservation as settled cash or an unconditional presale unless the legal status supports it.

For leasing, distinguish:

`space completed -> available -> lease signed -> tenant possession -> rent commencement -> free-rent / incentive period -> billed rent -> collected cash`

Physical occupancy, leased occupancy, economic occupancy and cash collection are different metrics.

### Absorption and price interaction

A faster sell-down or lease-up may require lower prices, higher incentives or marketing spend. Do not combine top-quartile pricing with
maximum absorption unless supported by market evidence. Downside cases should consider correlated effects between slower absorption,
longer interest carry, incentive pressure and exit/refinancing delay.

## Stabilization and bridge-to-term financing

Where construction debt is expected to refinance into permanent debt, define the stabilization test. It may depend on some combination
of occupancy, lease maturity, tenant quality, rent commencement, collections, NOI, DSCR, debt yield and valuation.

A completed building is not necessarily a stabilized building. A bridge loan intended to fund lease-up should not be assumed to refinance
on the practical-completion date unless the take-out lender's conditions are met.

## Income-producing property debt metrics

For stabilized or near-stabilized property, distinguish:

- **NOI** on the lender-defined basis;
- **DSCR** = lender-recognised NOI / debt service;
- **debt yield** = lender-recognised NOI / loan amount; and
- **LTV** = loan amount / lender-recognised value.

The model must reconcile rents, vacancy, concessions, bad debt, operating expenses and capital items to the precise NOI definition used
by the lender. Do not improve DSCR by excluding recurring property costs from NOI while still treating them as necessary to preserve
value.

## Property valuation states and lender collateral

Where relevant distinguish:

- as-is value;
- as-complete value;
- as-stabilized value;
- market value subject to assumptions/special assumptions; and
- liquidation/workout value.

A prospective as-complete valuation is not cash collateral available today. Test the assumptions required to reach that state, including
planning, completion, leasing/sales and market conditions.

For development property, residual valuation is especially sensitive to small changes in GDV, cost and time. Treat a large increase in
residual land value from a modest assumption change as a feature of residual leverage, not evidence of precision.

## Mixed-use and shared infrastructure

For mixed-use developments, model each component on its own unit/demand/revenue basis and then allocate shared land, basement,
parking, public realm, utilities, infrastructure and common-area costs using a defensible method. Avoid:

- double counting shared area/revenue;
- allocating all common infrastructure to the weakest-return component merely to support another component's margin;
- using one absorption curve for different submarkets; or
- treating internal transfers between project components as third-party revenue.

## Exit, refinance and residual value

For a hold or develop-to-core strategy, the exit value should follow supported stabilized NOI, capitalization/discount assumptions,
transaction costs and expected timing. Test:

`stabilized NOI -> forward market rent / vacancy / opex -> capitalization rate / DCF -> gross asset value -> selling costs / tax -> net sale proceeds`

For refinance, separately test lender debt capacity under the **future** interest rate, DSCR/debt-yield/LTV requirements and asset value.
Do not assume refinance debt simply equals outstanding construction debt.

## Mezzanine debt, preferred equity and holdco leverage

Where subordinate capital is used, identify its legal level, coupon/preferred return, PIK/accrual, cash-pay, maturity, control rights,
intercreditor restrictions and repayment source. A preferred-equity instrument can behave economically like mezzanine debt while having
different enforcement and accounting treatment.

Holdco/mezzanine debt must be serviced from distributions that remain after project-level senior debt covenants, reserves and cash traps.
Do not size holdco debt against gross property cash flow.

## Development downside design

At minimum consider correlated cases around:

- lower sales prices / rents;
- slower presales, sales or lease-up;
- higher incentives / vacancy / bad debt;
- construction-cost overrun;
- delay and longer interest carry;
- loss of planning density / scope;
- higher interest rates and reduced take-out leverage;
- higher exit cap rate / lower valuation;
- contractor or major-tenant default; and
- additional remediation / infrastructure / authority contribution.

The critical lender downside is often **cost overrun + delay + weaker value + weaker take-out debt at the same time**, not one-variable
sensitivities in isolation.

# Digital / communications infrastructure — conditional module

## Network demand and take-up

For fixed broadband/fibre networks, distinguish:

`premises passed / addressable sites → serviceable premises → connected customers / contracted wholesale capacity → active services → revenue`

Do not treat premises passed as paying connections.

Where relevant distinguish:

- wholesale versus retail model;
- open-access versus vertically integrated;
- residential/business/mobile-backhaul customers;
- committed versus forecast capacity; and
- connection timing/ramp-up.

## Revenue forms

Digital infrastructure can earn through:

- recurring wholesale access charges;
- retail subscriptions where the project genuinely includes retail service;
- dark-fibre leases;
- indefeasible rights of use (IRUs);
- capacity leases;
- connection/installation charges;
- colocation/landing-station services; and
- contracted backhaul.

An upfront IRU or installation payment is not economically identical to recurring service revenue. Prevent
revenue recognition/cash timing from disguising the contractual economic period.

## Capacity, resilience and redundancy

Physical/digital capacity can be constrained by:

- fibre strands/spectrum/cable capacity;
- backhaul;
- active electronics;
- landing stations;
- power/cooling;
- peering/interconnection;
- redundancy requirements; and
- customer premises activation capacity.

Where service-level commitments require redundancy, do not model 100% of installed capacity as saleable if a
portion must be reserved for resilience.

## Technology obsolescence and upgrade cycles

Long-life ducts, towers or fibre can outlive active equipment and transmission technologies. Separate passive-
infrastructure life from active-electronics/technology refresh. Growth in traffic/capacity without upgrade capex
can be implausible even if the physical cable remains usable.

# Solid waste / resource recovery — conditional module

## Waste volume and payment basis

Classify revenue as applicable:

- collection/service payment;
- gate/tipping fee;
- availability payment;
- minimum-tonnage/take-or-pay;
- processing fee;
- sale of recyclates/energy/other by-products; or
- combination.

Distinguish municipal/commercial waste volumes, contracted volumes and market merchant volumes where
material.

## Scope and mass balance

Reconcile:

`waste received → pre-treatment/sorting → recyclables/recovered products → organics/process streams → rejects/residual → landfill/disposal`

Do not model 100% of waste as both recovered product and residual disposal.

## Landfill airspace and closure

Where landfill is part of the asset, capacity is finite. Model:

- available airspace/cell capacity;
- density/compaction basis;
- cell development capex;
- leachate/gas systems;
- progressive closure;
- final capping; and
- post-closure monitoring/treatment obligations.

A landfill's concession can end before post-closure obligations do.

## By-product revenue

Energy, recyclate, compost, recovered material or carbon-related revenue should be linked to recoverable yield,
quality, market price and contractual ownership. Do not count gross theoretical energy/material yield without
process losses or disposal/reject costs.

# Infrastructure benchmark discipline

Do **not** use universal pass/fail ranges for:

- DSCR/LLCR/gearing;
- concession length;
- availability deductions;
- tariff escalation;
- traffic growth;
- passenger growth;
- airport spend/passenger;
- port utilisation;
- NRW percentage;
- regulatory WACC;
- RAB returns;
- lifecycle capex;
- maintenance cost;
- capex per km/unit;
- handback residual life; or
- infrastructure IRR.

Normalise any external benchmark for:

- asset class and technology;
- greenfield/brownfield/operating stage;
- scale and capacity;
- geography and labour/material market;
- base date and currency;
- scope boundaries;
- procurement/delivery model;
- concession/regulatory regime;
- operating standards;
- service level;
- climate/geotechnical conditions; and
- risk allocation.

A number outside a published range is not itself a finding. The finding is the **unexplained inconsistency with
credible project evidence, contract economics, physical capacity or relevant comparable experience**.

# Common infrastructure-model failure patterns

1. **Wrong revenue-regime classification** — user-pays, usage-linked, availability and regulated revenue are
   treated as economically interchangeable.
2. **Demand risk transferred by label only** — PPP is assumed to mean private demand risk although the
   payment mechanism protects the project from volume.
3. **Full service payment before service commencement** — recurring availability revenue starts before the
   asset has passed the contractual acceptance condition.
4. **Availability deductions are decorative** — outage/performance stress does not reduce payment.
5. **Deductions double-counted** — the same failure is deducted through both unavailability and another
   performance mechanism beyond what the contract permits.
6. **Indexation mismatched to cost exposure** — revenue and operating costs inflate on incompatible bases
   without contractual/economic explanation.
7. **Pass-through assumed where risk is retained** — utility/input cost increase is automatically reimbursed
   although project company bears it.
8. **Risk retained despite contractual relief** — stress case charges full gross impact while ignoring a clear
   compensation/relief mechanism.
9. **Risk compensated twice** — explicit cash-flow compensation plus duplicate contingency/risk uplift.
10. **One completion date drives everything** — construction completion, testing, acceptance and revenue start
    are conflated.
11. **Interface packages complete independently** — model earns revenue although a critical package is late.
12. **Nameplate capacity overrides the bottleneck** — demand exceeds terminal/network/system constraint.
13. **Volume grows without augmentation** — forecast exceeds practical capacity while expansion capex stays
    flat.
14. **Lifecycle capex disappears** — long-lived concession assumes initial construction lasts unchanged for the
    full term.
15. **Final-year maintenance holiday** — spend collapses near expiry despite handback-condition requirements.
16. **Terminal value survives handback** — project books a sale/perpetuity value for an asset transferred to the
    authority without compensation.
17. **Concession term confused with asset life** — cash flows continue beyond legal operating rights.
18. **Generic termination value** — all termination causes use one compensation amount.
19. **Government support double counted** — grant reduces capex and is also added as revenue/cash without
    contractual basis.
20. **Minimum-revenue guarantee treated as incremental base revenue** — support is added on top of the full
    forecast instead of only activating below trigger.
21. **Site availability assumed at financial close** — land/right-of-way/resettlement constraints are ignored.
22. **Climate risk is a generic haircut** — no pathway from physical hazard to service/cash flow.
23. **Outside-view evidence ignored on a megaproject** — highly favourable cost/schedule/demand forecast is
    accepted without relevant outturn comparison.
24. **Historical overrun averages imposed mechanically** — reference-class statistics become mandatory
    generic uplifts despite poor comparability.
25. **Low-demand scenario freezes variable cost and capex decisions** — no operational or capacity response.
26. **High-demand scenario captures revenue but not wear/capacity capex**.
27. **Rail traffic units are mixed** — passengers, passenger-km, tonnes, tonne-km and train-km are treated as
    interchangeable.
28. **Rail access revenue uses route-km instead of actual access basis**.
29. **Rail access charge survives group consolidation** — internal cost and revenue both remain in integrated
    group economics.
30. **Rail capacity equals line length** — train paths, signalling, junctions, terminals and rolling stock are
    ignored.
31. **Rail fleet grows services without available rolling stock**.
32. **Rail traction mix has no fuel/power/fleet consequence**.
33. **Passenger subsidy is an unlimited loss plug** without service-contract basis.
34. **Road forecast has no O-D/network basis** for a material greenfield demand-risk concession.
35. **Road ramp-up and underlying growth are double counted**.
36. **Toll increase raises price but never changes traffic** despite material alternatives/willingness-to-pay risk.
37. **Road traffic equals cash-paying traffic** — exemptions/evasion/collection are omitted.
38. **Availability road is modelled like a real-toll road**.
39. **Airport passengers drive every revenue line** regardless of aircraft movements, passenger mix or
    commercial capacity.
40. **Airport airline concentration disappears in downside** — traffic falls but airline receivables/non-aero
    effects do not.
41. **Airport passenger capacity is one number** despite runway/stand/terminal bottlenecks.
42. **Port berth capacity ignores yard/gate/hinterland constraint**.
43. **Port throughput grows with no dredging/depth or equipment capability**.
44. **Port concession fee is counted twice or omitted** across authority/operator perimeter.
45. **Port customer/shipping-line concentration is invisible**.
46. **Water system input equals billed sales** — NRW is skipped.
47. **NRW reduction benefit is duplicated** as both extra billed volume and full avoided production cost without
    physical reconciliation.
48. **Water billing equals cash collection**.
49. **Water connections and consumption per connection are conflated**.
50. **Treatment capacity expands while source/network remains constrained**.
51. **Wastewater volume is hard-linked to potable sales without infiltration/return-flow basis**.
52. **Accounting PPE is treated as regulatory RAB/RAV**.
53. **Every capex dollar automatically earns regulated return** despite prudency/eligibility or lag.
54. **Revenue-cap utility is modelled with full long-run volume risk** without true-up consideration.
55. **Regulatory incentives/reopeners are omitted from revenue recovery**.
56. **Social-infrastructure revenue is tied to occupancy** despite availability-based contract.
57. **Social-infrastructure soft-service cost stays fixed forever** despite contract market testing/benchmarking.
58. **Premises passed equals digital customers**.
59. **Digital network sells resilience capacity twice** — reserved redundancy is treated as saleable capacity.
60. **Passive fibre life is applied to active electronics** — technology refresh capex disappears.
61. **Waste mass balance creates more recovered products than input** or omits rejects.
62. **Landfill has infinite capacity** — airspace/cell development is absent.
63. **Waste by-product revenue ignores yield, quality and disposal costs**.
64. **Ancillary revenue is treated as risk-free core revenue** although dependent on traffic, occupancy or market
    demand.
65. **Contract variation affects capex/opex but not payment/tariff mechanics**.
66. **Contractual base case is overwritten by current forecast** — the model can no longer reproduce contractual
    compensation/refinancing calculations.
67. **Bid model survives financial close unchanged** — executed debt, hedge, fee, contract or subcontract terms are
    not reflected in the close model.
68. **PPP clause exists only as a label** — payment, indexation, variation, refinancing or termination economics do
    not reproduce the operative contract formula.
69. **Construction sources and uses balance only in total** — timing produces an unfunded cash deficit before a
    grant, debt draw or equity contribution becomes available.
70. **Future government grant funds past construction** without a bridge facility.
71. **Equity funding sequence contradicts finance documents** — debt is drawn before required sponsor funding or
    completion support.
72. **DSCR uses the wrong CFADS perimeter** — ratio is mathematically correct but includes cash not available to
    senior lenders or omits required cash deductions.
73. **Debt is sized to a headline gearing assumption despite lower cash-flow debt capacity**.
74. **Debt sculpting ignores lifecycle or reserve funding** and therefore overstates debt service capacity.
75. **No debt tail despite assumed lender reliance on a tail** — debt repayment effectively depends on perfect
    performance through concession expiry.
76. **Restricted reserve cash treated as distributable cash**.
77. **Distribution lock-up is cosmetic** — equity distributions continue after covenant/default/forward-cover tests
    fail.
78. **Trapped cash vanishes** rather than being retained, swept, reserved or later released.
79. **Bid-to-close interest adjustment compensates the wrong financing component** — bidder margin or hedge cost is
    neutralised despite the procurement retaining that risk, or vice versa.
80. **Hedge notional decouples from debt** — swap remains materially over- or under-hedged without explanation.
81. **Refinancing gain accrues entirely to shareholders despite contractual sharing**.
82. **Mini-perm refinancing is assumed automatic** — short initial tenor rolls into long-term debt with identical
    economics and no availability risk.
83. **Compensation event is funded for free** — project receives later compensation but incremental financing cost
    and funding availability are ignored.
84. **SPV assumes perfect subcontract pass-through** despite mismatched liability caps, dates, relief events or
    performance standards.
85. **Authority affordability is inferred from SPV bankability** — contingent and long-term government cash
    obligations are not separately assessed.

86. **Authoritative document superseded but model still uses bid assumption** — executed financing/project terms
    differ but the model retains an earlier indicative input.
87. **Cross-adviser models describe different projects** — technical capacity/date, legal obligation and financial
    assumption cannot be reconciled.
88. **Technical model aggregation erases economic constraint** — annual total ignores seasonality, peak, outage,
    curtailment or commissioning that drives cash flow.
89. **Financial close is mistaken for drawability** — facility is used before conditions precedent/security/insurance
    or other draw conditions are satisfied.
90. **Undrawn commitment treated as available after drawstop/default** — later funding block is ignored.
91. **Debt tranches are synthetically blended across different currencies/tenors/priorities** so refinancing or cash
    priority is misstated.
92. **Covenant breach jumps straight to termination** despite cash trap, cure, waiver or step-in mechanics.
93. **Unlimited sponsor cure assumed** despite no committed equity/support obligation.
94. **Termination ignores lender direct-agreement cure/step-in period**.
95. **Credit support booked at face value as immediate cash** despite cap, expiry, call condition, delay or weak
    counterparty.
96. **Insurance makes downside disappear** — deductible, waiting period, business interruption or reinstatement
    timing is omitted.
97. **Insurance premium-sharing mechanism is treated as generic inflation** rather than contract-specific actual/base
    cost sharing.
98. **Contracted revenue is treated as credit-risk-free** despite concentrated/weak authority, offtaker or anchor
    customer.
99. **FX pass-through assumed to eliminate all currency risk** despite lag, partial coverage or index-basis mismatch.
100. **Public guarantee shown only at face value** — trigger/timing/probability-sensitive fiscal exposure is not
     modelled.
101. **Public-sector comparator is not service-equivalent** — different maintenance, timing or output creates false
     value-for-money.
102. **IFRIC 12 classification is inconsistent with concession rights** where statutory accounting is in scope.
103. **Construction delay sensitivity ignores critical path** — all costs move together regardless of actual schedule
     dependency.
104. **Cost contingency stack double counts risk** across D&C contingency, owner contingency, quantified risks and
     generic overrun.
105. **Original capex overrun percentage used on a nearly completed project** instead of remaining cost-to-complete.
106. **Lifecycle underspend is assumed to be efficiency** with no condition/renewal evidence.
107. **Debt-free final years are treated as risk-free** despite handback/lifecycle obligations and reduced lender
     discipline.
108. **Power PPA capacity/energy economics are conflated** — fixed-cost recovery and variable energy recovery do not
     follow the contract.
109. **Renewable resource becomes saleable energy without loss/availability/curtailment bridge**.
110. **Fuel-price pass-through also compensates poor plant efficiency without contractual basis**.
111. **Grid curtailment, project outage and resource shortfall are treated as the same event**.
112. **Plant COD occurs before interconnection/transmission is available** without deemed-energy or other protection.
113. **Merchant tail uses contracted PPA price or availability protections after contract expiry**.
114. **Heat-network addressable load is treated as contracted connected demand**.
115. **Heat tariff indexation perfectly offsets input energy cost despite different indices/reset dates**.
116. **Correlated downside is modelled as isolated sensitivities only**, hiding compound delay/funding/contract effects.
117. **A single worst case stacks mutually inconsistent assumptions** and therefore does not represent a physically or
     contractually executable scenario.
118. **Late-stage model retains stale feasibility assumptions** after binding supplier, lender, regulator or executed contract
     evidence has superseded them.
119. **Fixed-rate debt is treated as both cheap certainty and free flexibility** — early prepayment, refinancing, change or
     termination produces no make-whole or hedge-break consequence.
120. **Legal substitution is modelled as instantaneous contractor replacement** with no market capacity, mobilisation,
     transition or replacement-price effect.
121. **Payment/tariff/change formula is never exercised outside the zero-event base case**, so caps, lags, thresholds or
     sharing errors remain hidden.
122. **User-pay tariff is a financial plug** — service scope, cost/revenue requirement, affordability, demand/collection and
     subsidy/VGF are not reconciled.
123. **Government guarantee is represented only by face value or a binary flag** despite material probability/timing risk.
124. **Risk is transferred without matching decision-right controls** — for example, government bears FX loss while the
     protected parties can freely increase foreign-currency borrowing.
125. **Authority-mandated bid assumption is mistaken for a protected economic entitlement** after financial close.
126. **Strategic sponsor support survives an equity sell-down in the model** even though the support/retention obligation
     expires or changes on transfer.
127. **Renegotiation is presented as a neutral forecast update** although tariff, term, support, debt or risk allocation moves
     material value between parties.
128. **Pre-agreed contractual adjustment and discretionary renegotiation are conflated**, obscuring whether compensation
     is an entitlement or a newly negotiated concession.
129. **Government/lender protection removes meaningful private downside but the model still claims unchanged risk transfer**
     and project-cash-flow bankability.
130. **Same performance failure is economically penalised twice** even though the contract's no-double-remedy rule prevents
     cumulative recovery, or a separate remedy is wrongly suppressed because a deduction also applies.
131. **Persistent breach jumps directly to termination** without contractual warnings, recurrence/cure periods and lender rights,
     or recurring breach never escalates despite an express persistent-default mechanism.
132. **Service payment ring-fences debt service/finance charges from performance deductions** without an express contractual
     protection, hollowing out the intended service-performance risk transfer.
133. **Reviewable service price resets automatically by inflation** even though the contract requires benchmarking/market testing,
     efficient-provider evidence or an escalation from one value-testing method to another.
134. **Public capital contribution moves ahead of private capital or milestone achievement**, weakening the base-case private
     capital at risk or allowing an equity bridge to become a permanent reduction in sponsor exposure.
135. **Insurance proceeds are assumed to rebuild automatically** despite a contractual/lender economic reinstatement test or
     alternative use-of-proceeds waterfall.
136. **Uninsurable risk becomes free project protection** — saved insurance premium remains in the service payment, market
     availability is never retested, or authority compensation is assumed without the contract's trigger.
137. **Private-default termination is hard-coded to one fixed value** even though the contract selects between retender/liquid-
     market and no-retender/expert-valuation pathways.
138. **All debt is treated identically on termination** despite different treatment of bank/bond/subordinated instruments,
     mitigation, hedge breakage or an option to transfer/assume senior debt instead of cash repayment.
139. **Handback security is double counted or omitted** because authority withholding/escrow, SPV reserve and lifecycle-surplus
     retention are not distinguished.
140. **PSC competitive neutrality is a generic efficiency uplift/penalty** rather than the governing jurisdiction's ownership-
     related cash-flow adjustment.
141. **A disputed or ambiguous contract interpretation is embedded as certain model economics** with no alternative value case or
     recognition that the entitlement/payment basis remains unresolved.
142. **Variable-term revenue concession is modelled with a fixed expiry date**, so weak/strong demand changes revenue but not the
     contractual term, debt tail, lifecycle or handback timing.
143. **Revenue floor and upside sharing/cap are modelled independently**, allowing inconsistent or double public/private demand-risk
     protection rather than the contract's integrated support-and-sharing mechanism.
144. **Base case assumes discretionary renegotiation will rescue an uneconomic bid** through future tariff, term, subsidy or scope relief
     that is not an existing contractual entitlement.
145. **Disputed claims are either booked as certain cash or ignored altogether** — claimed, certified/agreed, disputed and settlement
     amounts/timing are not distinguished.
146. **Capex value engineering is credited without whole-life consequence** even though the design choice changes maintenance,
     availability, lifecycle or handback economics borne elsewhere in the structure.
147. **Risky public guarantee is treated as economically costless because near-term expected cash is zero**, despite a material
     contingent exposure where the authority analysis claims to value risk transfer.
148. **Performance-based road maintenance is modelled as input reimbursement**, so road condition/service-level failure has no payment
     consequence and contractor intervention choices do not affect margin.
149. **Road rehabilitation, improvement, maintenance and emergency works are collapsed into one payment/risk basis** despite the
     contract pricing and allocating those work categories differently.
150. **Completion support releases at PC/COD despite the finance-defined completion test still being unmet** — guarantees, contingent
     equity or cost-overrun support disappear before the required technical/performance/seasoning/funding conditions permit release.
151. **Public support is counted twice** — capital grant/VGF reduces the construction funding requirement and also inflates operating
     CFADS or debt capacity without an express financing basis.
152. **Infrastructure book value is treated as lender collateral recovery** — specialised asset accounting or replacement value substitutes
     for project cash-flow, contract, termination and enforceable security recovery.
153. **FX modelling addresses devaluation but ignores convertibility/transfer/repatriation** — local cash exists, but hard-currency debt or
     distributions are assumed payable despite a material transfer restriction risk.
154. **Ramp-up liquidity is treated as permanent unrestricted cash or guaranteed revenue** despite being conditional, capped and temporary
     financing support.
155. **Concession/franchise fee is omitted or economically free** — the authority payment has no funding, tariff, leverage or return
     consequence, or is double counted with a separate benefit-sharing obligation.
156. **Model is ratio-bankable but the financing market cannot fund it** — debt sizing assumes tenor, currency, hold capacity or lender
     competition that current project-relevant market evidence does not support.
157. **Preferred-bid affordability survives unchanged to financial close** even though permitted rate/inflation/timing adjustments materially
     increase the final authority payment.
158. **Long-term authority payment has no credible budget/funding route** — contractual PPP obligations extend for decades but the model
     assumes cash availability from a short annual budget without the applicable appropriation/support mechanism.
159. **Variation pricing is ad hoc and finance-blind** — prescribed rate cards/benchmark/open-book rules or lender consent/funding effects are
     ignored.
160. **Payment formula is correct but invoice-to-cash is wrong** — certification, disputes, tax, true-ups, contractual payment lag or late-
     payment interest are absent from operating liquidity.
161. **Construction delay is assumed fully recovered through LDs** — gross lost revenue/IDC/escalation and retained loss exceed the capped,
     delayed or disputed contractor recovery.
162. **Known live project risk disappears from the current forecast** despite a current risk register/technical/contract-management record
     showing a material unresolved exposure.
163. **Expiry or termination assumes free service continuity** — authority reprocurement, interim operation, mobilisation or transition costs
     are omitted despite a retained continuity obligation.
164. **Financial return is presented as economic return** — SPV/investor cash flow is relabelled EIRR/ENPV without the societal resource-cost,
     transfer and externality perimeter required by the governing appraisal methodology.
165. **Economic appraisal compares the project with zero rather than a credible without-project case**, overstating incremental benefits or
     omitting costs/activities that would occur anyway.
166. **Economic benefits are double counted or detached from the physical service engine** — two monetisations of the same accessibility,
     willingness-to-pay, reliability, resource-saving or similar effect are added together without a methodological bridge.
167. **Economic viability is assumed to prove financial sustainability, or vice versa** — tariff/support/O&M/lifecycle funding is not bridged
     to sustained service delivery despite the model claiming both conclusions.
168. **Operating model diverges from financial close before actuals are introduced** — a new post-close structure cannot reconstruct the agreed
     financial-close economics and the unexplained conversion difference is mistaken for current performance.
169. **Current forecast opens from the old financial-close balance sheet** — actual cash, debt, reserves or working-capital balances are ignored
     even though the concession has already moved away from the original forecast path.
170. **Actual-to-forecast cut-over manufactures or loses cash** — opening accruals, prepayments, receivables or payables unwind arbitrarily at the
     reporting boundary rather than according to the underlying payment obligation.
171. **The same actual transaction has inconsistent PPP classifications** across statutory/management cash flow and lender CFADS, creating an
     omission, duplicate or covenant-friendly reclassification without economic basis.
172. **Current total-contract outcome cannot be explained from financial close** — actual outturn and remaining reforecast do not bridge to the
     change in service payments, costs, debt, liquidity, lender ratios or sponsor returns.
173. **Shareholder history is rewritten by the reforecast** — actual equity/sub-debt contributions, repayments or distributions are replaced with
     current forecast timing when calculating sponsor returns or sale value.
174. **Historic lender ratios are recalculated from today's forecast** — prior-period CFADS, debt service, debt/reserve balances or covenant results
     no longer reflect what actually occurred.
175. **Staged service payment uses the wrong progress measure** — partial revenue is based on spend or schedule percentage although the contract
     pays by certified facility/section/service availability, or sectional payment rights are omitted and the model assumes all-or-nothing COD.
176. **Periodic lender update has no concession-credit bridge** — a material change in ratios or statements from the prior lender-recognised model
     cannot be attributed to actual outturn, current assumptions, contract events or justified logic changes.
177. **Management reporting becomes a second economic truth** — actual/budget/reforecast board reports no longer reconcile to the operating
     PPP model and financial accounts despite being used to manage the same concession.
178. **Distress restructuring is modelled as painless refinancing** — debt is resculpted or maturity extended without showing which stakeholder
     provides value, changes payment/operating terms, or bears the resulting risk and liquidity consequences.
179. **Secondary-market stake value ignores the sponsor instrument package** — sale/buy analysis values ordinary dividends alone while omitting
     shareholder debt, fees/interest, transfer restrictions, residual obligations or current concession risks.


180. **Project sustainability is inferred from a small incremental O&M percentage** — the existing public network is already underfunded,
     so the new asset has no credible basis for receiving full maintenance.
181. **Budget allocation is treated as maintenance performed** — approved appropriation is adequate on paper but execution/cash release is
     persistently lower than the unconstrained O&M requirement.
182. **Existing-network O&M inflation/backlog is ignored** — nominal budget growth is attributed entirely to the new project although the old
     asset base also requires escalation and catch-up maintenance.
183. **The wrong public agency is tested for recurrent-cost capacity** — construction agency finances the project but a different operator must
     fund service-period O&M.
184. **Project financial viability is treated as operator sustainability** — an individually viable asset sits inside a utility with liquidity,
     cross-subsidy, debt or compensation-receipt weaknesses that can starve the project of resources.
185. **Discretionary subsidy is called intrinsic cost recovery** — annual appropriation/grant is assumed certain and the project is described as
     self-financing without testing fiscal support.
186. **Real project FIRR is mixed with nominal entity forecasts or nominal cost of capital** under a methodology that requires consistent real
     project evaluation.
187. **Project WACC is replaced by sponsor hurdle rate, social discount rate or operator corporate WACC** without regard to the governing
     project financial-evaluation methodology.
188. **Tariff/toll escalation is a viability plug** — assumed increases conflict with law, regulator authority, historical revision pattern,
     affordability or actual collection.
189. **Covenant controls an uncontrollable outcome** — operator is required to achieve demand, legislative appropriation or regulator approval
     rather than take measurable actions within its control.
190. **Appraisal-stage sustainability survives material scope/financing/entity change without re-evaluation**.
191. **VfM means cheapest** — lower-cost option fails minimum service/performance or omits material non-valued effects.
192. **Affordability and VfM are conflated** — an attractive procurement option exceeds the authority's credible funding envelope.
193. **The same transferred risk is priced in cash flows and again in the VfM discount rate** without methodology support.
194. **Early generic optimism/risk uplift is retained on top of detailed project-specific quantified risk**, double counting maturity-stage risk.
195. **Bid sanity check uses a stale comparator** that no longer reflects current scope, timing, market price or risk allocation.
196. **Operational PPP model cannot reproduce current indexed/deducted service payment**, leaving contract administration dependent on an old
     bid payment or manual adjustments.
197. **Operational savings come from cutting required maintenance/lifecycle**, improving short-term authority/SPV cash while weakening service,
     handback or long-run VfM.
198. **PPP change is priced only at direct capex** — financing, payment, O&M, lifecycle, insurance, performance and termination knock-on effects
     are omitted.
199. **Series of small PPP amendments is never assessed cumulatively**, obscuring a material change in the original economic bargain.
200. **Late-life remedial liability exceeds residual project cash with no reserve/bond/security**.
201. **Pre-feasibility road teaching model is treated as bankable project evidence** despite simplified vehicle, currency, timing, O&M or finance
     mechanics.
202. **Road tolls remitted to government are treated as operator O&M cash**, overstating public-road sustainability.
203. **Investment subsidy and operating subsidy are economically conflated** — construction support is double counted as service-period revenue
     or recurring support is incorrectly netted from capex.
204. **Geothermal bankability is based on above-ground resource potential without adequate drilling/confirmation evidence**, or the financing
     assumes commercial resource risk has disappeared when it remains with the project.
205. **Geothermal thermal resource, wellfield capacity, gross plant MW and net metered MW are treated as one capacity number**.
206. **Geothermal resource decline and plant degradation are collapsed into one capacity-factor haircut**, disconnecting make-up wells and
     surface-plant overhaul from their actual causes.
207. **Geothermal make-up-well plan is absent or financially disconnected** despite reservoir decline assumptions that cannot sustain contracted
     capacity through project life.
208. **Geothermal capacity charge is driven by dispatched MWh rather than contractual available capacity**, or unavailable MW is paid as if
     available.
209. **Geothermal energy tariff imports a generic fuel pass-through** despite the actual PPA having no conventional fuel component.
210. **A bankable PPA is assumed to remove all revenue risk** — single-offtaker payment/credit concentration and support expiry are ignored.

211. **Energy-efficiency savings are booked with no defensible baseline or counterfactual** — the model treats an engineering estimate as realised cash without a measurement/adjustment basis.
212. **The same raw utility-bill reduction is called both energy savings and verified avoided cost** despite material weather, occupancy, production or tariff changes.
213. **An energy-quantity guarantee is modelled as a fixed dollar guarantee** even though the contract leaves commodity/tariff-price risk with the host.
214. **A fixed-dollar savings guarantee ignores the contractual energy-price floor/cap or valuation convention** that limits the ESCO's obligation.
215. **Shared savings is modelled as a fixed ESCO payment** with no periodic measurement, true-up or reconciliation to actual/verified savings.
216. **The adjusted baseline is changed after implementation solely to preserve projected savings**, rather than applying the agreed M&V adjustment rules.
217. **Commissioning has not occurred but full recurring savings begin at installation completion**, disconnecting payment from demonstrated operating performance.
218. **O&M savings are counted as an ancillary benefit while the same maintenance cost has already been removed from project opex**, double counting the benefit.
219. **Avoided replacement capex is claimed as a project benefit while the same replacement capex remains in the host's future capital plan**.
220. **Property-value uplift is added on top of an exit value already capitalising the same energy-driven NOI increase**, double counting value.
221. **An ESCO guarantee is assumed to eliminate all project risk** even though host credit, energy-price, baseline, M&V, O&M, termination or force-majeure risks remain.
222. **Savings insurance and an ESCO guarantee are both modelled as full first-dollar recoveries for the same shortfall** without deductible, cap, priority or subrogation mechanics.
223. **A project development agreement can be cancelled without the model recognising the agreed development/reimbursement cost** after the ESCO has incurred protected development work.
224. **Performance payments begin on a date that does not satisfy the contractual acceptance/possession/performance-verification trigger**.
225. **A warranty is treated as an energy-savings guarantee** even though the warranty covers only equipment defects or workmanship.
226. **The host is said to outsource all performance risk but also bears the same performance shortfall in its cash flow** without an offsetting guarantee/payment reduction.
227. **A performance contract is treated as a financing instrument alone** and the model omits service, maintenance, M&V and guarantee obligations that determine the economic payment.
228. **A MESA/service provider pays the utility bill but the host model also pays the same utility invoice**, duplicating operating cost.
229. **A property-linked assessment is said to transfer with the property but sale triggers full balloon repayment in the model** without support from the actual programme/documentation.
230. **Tenant pass-through is assumed to solve the split incentive although the leases do not permit the assessment/service charge to be recovered from tenants**.
231. **A property-linked senior/super-priority lien is assumed to have no effect on the existing mortgage lender** and required consent/intercreditor mechanics are ignored.
232. **The transaction is described as off-balance-sheet solely because it is called a lease, PPA, MESA or service contract**, with no current accounting analysis.
233. **Lender underwriting relies on unconditional host credit while the host may suspend payment for project underperformance**, with no guarantee, reserve or other protection for that payment risk.
234. **Construction funding is advanced before acceptance while no completion support, bond, guarantee, retainage or other completion protection exists**.
235. **Installed retrofit equipment is assigned a high liquidation value despite being a fixture or having little economic removability/secondary market**.
236. **Early termination payment is below outstanding financing/break cost but the model still repays lenders in full**, leaving an unfunded termination gap.
237. **The host PPA economics claim both the avoided grid bill and all project tax/incentive benefits although the third-party sponsor owns/monetises those benefits**.
238. **The solar sponsor model claims an incentive/rebate that is only available to or has already been received by the host**, double counting support.
239. **A solar PPA is called cheaper because the PPA $/kWh is below an average utility energy rate**, while demand charges, minimum bills, time-of-use structure or non-bypassable charges that remain payable are ignored where material.
240. **A long-term rooftop solar PPA extends beyond roof remaining life with no removal/reinstallation, roof-renewal or access economics**.
241. **Solar modelled output exceeds interconnection/export or site-load constraints without curtailment/export-limit treatment**.
242. **Solar module degradation is modelled but inverter/other major component lifecycle is assumed infinite**, or warranty life is used as asset-life evidence for the whole system.
243. **Energy incentives/rebates are modelled as committed cash before award/eligibility/commissioning conditions are satisfied**.
244. **A historical energy announcement stock-price study is used as a predictable valuation uplift**, converting a dated empirical observation into a project cash-flow rule.
245. **A generic percentage uplift is applied for ancillary energy benefits** instead of proving incremental maintenance, avoided capex, compliance or property-value cash effects.
246. **Installed MW multiplied by hours is treated as net billable generation** despite resource limitation, forced/planned outage, auxiliary load, grid loss or curtailment.
247. **Capacity factor already incorporates outages and the model separately deducts the same forced/planned outage**, understating generation twice.
248. **Thermal plant efficiency/heat rate changes but fuel quantity does not change for the same net MWh**, breaking the physical fuel-energy conversion.
249. **Fuel-price pass-through is assumed to cover all fuel risk** although heat-rate, fuel-quality, transport, start-up or index-timing exposure remains with the SPV.
250. **Energy and capacity/standing tariff components are economically conflated** — dispatched MWh drives a capacity charge or available MW drives an energy-only payment contrary to the offtake contract.
251. **A reverse-solved tariff that achieves target IRR/DSCR is presented as an available contractual tariff** despite exceeding the executed, regulated, market or affordability constraint.
252. **Renewable/environmental support continues through asset life despite the actual programme/contract ending earlier**.
253. **A discrete major plant outage has no associated generation, availability-payment, maintenance or lifecycle-cash consequence**.
254. **Pumped-storage discharge is modelled as free primary generation** with no pumping/charging energy, storage loss or corresponding market purchase.
255. **Waste-to-energy economics both charge a feedstock purchase cost and earn a gate/tipping fee on the same waste stream without contractual support**.
256. **Hydro/run-of-river output ignores hydrology, minimum-flow or seasonal water constraints** and is driven only by nameplate MW.
257. **Tidal/wave generation assumes a flat annual resource and ordinary land-based maintenance access** despite material marine seasonality/access constraints in the project evidence.
258. **Offshore wind adopts onshore O&M/access/cable assumptions** despite offshore vessel, subsea/export, marine-weather and component-exchange requirements.
259. **Project IRR, ordinary-equity IRR and blended sponsor return are mixed** so shareholder-loan interest/fees appear as both project cash cost and sponsor return without a clear perimeter.
260. **A technology option's fictional textbook cost/capacity factor/payback is treated as a market benchmark** without normalising technology, scope, location, price date, resource or contract perimeter.



Additional real-estate, storage, airport, E&S and resilience failure patterns:

211. **Development appraisal is presented as market valuation without a basis-of-value bridge** — the developer's own hurdle is treated as market evidence.
212. **Residual land value is reported with false precision** despite material sensitivity to GDV, cost, duration or planning assumptions.
213. **Profit on cost, profit on value and equity IRR are compared as interchangeable hurdles**.
214. **A constant developer margin is applied to projects of materially different duration and risk without checking the implied annualised return**.
215. **As-complete/as-stabilized value is treated as today's collateral value** before completion/lease-up conditions are satisfied.
216. **Original sources and uses still balance but current remaining funding is below current cost-to-complete**.
217. **Interest reserve is used to keep a weak ADC loan current after the property should be self-funding**, masking deterioration.
218. **Interest reserve is replenished with new debt without re-testing current project feasibility and value**.
219. **Presale reservations/EOIs are treated as unconditional contracted settlements**.
220. **Lease signed, possession, rent commencement, economic occupancy and cash collection are collapsed into one occupancy metric**.
221. **Construction completion is treated as stabilization and permanent refinance occurs before lender take-out tests are met**.
222. **A bridge loan assumes permanent take-out at the original valuation despite slower lease-up and higher market rates**.
223. **Loan sizing uses one LTC percentage although actual facility is constrained by lower LTV/cost-to-complete/presale or commitment limits**.
224. **Developer equity is assumed pro rata even though the finance documents require equity-first funding**.
225. **Known construction variation is hidden inside unused contingency while both remain separately available**.
226. **Sales downside lowers price but leaves absorption and finance carry unchanged**, understating correlated development risk.
227. **Lease-up downside lowers occupancy but leaves concessions, tenant improvements and leasing commissions unchanged**.
228. **Exit cap rate is sensitised without changing refinance capacity or sale proceeds where both depend on the same value**.
229. **Holdco/mezzanine debt is serviced from gross project cash before senior covenants/reserves**, ignoring structural subordination.
230. **Mixed-use internal transfer is counted as third-party revenue at both components**.
231. **BESS revenue stack sums mutually exclusive maximum service revenues** without MW/SOC reservation constraints.
232. **BESS dispatch earns activation energy while SOC never changes**.
233. **BESS round-trip losses are omitted or applied twice**.
234. **Battery usable MWh remains constant for life with no degradation or augmentation cost** despite a capacity-retention contract.
235. **Aggressive arbitrage cycling drives revenue but degradation assumes low-cycle standby service**.
236. **Battery warranty recovery is assumed although modelled dispatch breaches cycle/SOC/temperature limits**.
237. **Ancillary-service availability and activation payments are both applied to all reserved MW regardless of actual market rules**.
238. **Hybrid solar/wind+BESS exports the same renewable MWh directly and again after battery discharge**.
239. **Battery merchant revenue is debt-sized as fully contracted revenue**.
240. **Airport passenger revenue uses total passenger counts for a charge that applies only to departing/non-transfer passengers**.
241. **Airport tax collected for government is treated as airport retained revenue**.
242. **Airport aeronautical charge includes cost already allocated to non-aeronautical commercial activity**.
243. **Airport expansion debt assumes charge increase that requires regulatory/user approval but no approval downside is modelled**.
244. **Airport non-aeronautical concession revenue is gross turnover with no minimum/percentage-share contract logic**.
245. **Profitable airport/network cross-subsidy is omitted even though central costs/support are transferred across airports**.
246. **E&S action-plan obligation is a financing condition but has no cost, timing or drawstop consequence**.
247. **Unresolved resettlement/access issue has zero schedule impact despite being required for lawful possession**.
248. **Climate disclosure requirement is modelled as a cash cost but the actual physical climate exposure is omitted**, or vice versa.
249. **Resilience benefit is inserted as SPV revenue even though it is an avoided public/economic loss with no payment mechanism**.
250. **Resilience capex is included but no committed funding source exists**.
251. **Climate-hardening design assumes full resilience while routine maintenance needed to preserve that resilience is underfunded**.
252. **Exceptional climate event is treated as force majeure while ordinary foreseeable weather/maintenance risk is contractually private**.
253. **Insurance payout is assumed immediate after disaster with no deductible, waiting period or reinstatement timing**.

# Infrastructure contradiction patterns

Treat the following as high-value contradictions requiring explanation rather than automatic errors:

- “availability payment” + direct proportional link to traffic volume;
- “private demand risk” + guaranteed full revenue floor;
- “service commencement” later than first full operating payment;
- outage scenario + unchanged availability payment;
- inflation-indexed payment + flat nominal revenue;
- fixed payment + full pass-through of every cost despite no contractual basis;
- concession expires + cash flows continue indefinitely;
- handback required + terminal sale proceeds;
- low demand + same capacity expansion date;
- high demand + unchanged major maintenance;
- rail traffic growth + no train paths/fleet/terminal capacity;
- integrated rail structure + internal access charge in consolidated EBITDA/revenue;
- toll increase + unchanged demand in a price-sensitive corridor without explanation;
- airport passenger growth + no terminal/runway/stand capacity response;
- port throughput growth + no yard/berth/hinterland response;
- water production growth + flat pumping/treatment energy/chemical use without efficiency explanation;
- falling NRW + no change in billed water, production, cost or capacity anywhere;
- regulated capex + no RAB/allowed-revenue consequence where the regime is RAB based;
- social PPP user occupancy decline + revenue decline despite pure availability payment;
- fibre premises passed + identical active connections from day one;
- landfill tonnage + no airspace consumption; and
- climate hazard worsens + no capex, downtime, insurance, performance or service consequence;
- executed financing differs from bid assumptions + unchanged financial-close model;
- contractual base case required for compensation + base case overwritten with actual forecast;
- debt draw precedes required equity contribution or conditions precedent;
- positive DSCR + cash includes restricted reserve or non-CFADS receipts;
- covenant breach + continuing shareholder distributions;
- major refinancing + no authority gain-share consequence despite contractual sharing;
